Preface 

THE METHODS of recording specific activities in the accounts of a business are discussed in Accounting II in relation to two of the fundamental forms of business organization, the partnership and the corporation. The text presents some of the situations which the corporate manager must resolve with the aid of the figures prepared and arranged by the accountant. Also indicated are the methods by which the accountant designs his statements to draw attention to certain specific relationships. Thus the student perceives the purposes that may be served by the records and statements which he studies in both Accounting I and Accounting II. 

Furthermore the student is introduced to the uses of accounting data in budgeting and in forecasting. 

Customarily a businessman wishes to discuss his problems with his banker. The banker, therefore, should understand management functions; he should know what information he can expect to glean from the accountant's statements and what assistance is available to him through the use of the statements. He should be able to consult with the businessman and appraise the small business intelligently.

The management problems of business are presented from the viewpoint of the administrator in a large organization for purposes of clarification. The manager of a small business requires the same kind of information and the same recording services from his accountant.

The terminology used in this text represents the author's preference. Although the author's choice of terms is agreeable to the Institute, the students are urged to familiarize themselves with other usages since there is considerable divergence of opinion among accountants concerning the terminology to be used in describing the various statements which they prepare.

Administration, University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, Michigan.

It is important that the student develop an understanding of the function of accounting in banking and in business. A banker who understands the uses of accounting is better equipped to appraise the value of a business to the community and to serve the customers of his bank.

ACCOUNTING II CHAPTER I  

ACCOUNTING for business activities is basically the same whether the legal form of the organization be a single proprietorship, a partnership, or a corporation. Even the special corporate forms, such as municipal and state governmental units, have accounting systems that follow the same basic patterns. The differences in accounting for these different legal forms lie only in the accounts representing the ownership and the recording of the changes therein. The objective of Chapters I to IV is to present these differences in accounting insofar as they apply to partnerships and corporations. 

Good accounting always seeks to portray the facts and the relationships of any business situation as clearly as possible. It therefore becomes necessary to inquire into the significant characteristics that are unique to partnerships and corporations, for it is these facts, legal and business, that must be portrayed by accounting. In 

Accounting I our discussions were based on the single proprietorship as a business unit. Therefore it is advantageous to study the partnership situation by contrasting it with the single proprietorship. 

THE SINGLE PROPRIETORSHIP 

From earliest times individual owners have possessed the right of using, selling, or exchanging their property at will. The single proprietorship consists of an individual (with or without employed assistants) who is engaged either in the buying and selling of property or in some other profit-earning enterprise. Small businesses customarily are conducted as single proprietorships. Partner- 1

ships and corporate organizations usually are larger in size, volume of activity, and territorial extent. 

In a single proprietorship all the assets are owned by an individual who is personally liable for all the debts, who enjoys all the profits or suffers all the losses, and who commands all phases of management. These are the basic characteristics of the single proprietorship form of organization.

Advantages of the Single Proprietorship Form 

The single proprietorship offers a number of advantages that may be compared with those of partnerships and corporations. 

One advantage is the comparative flexibility of beginning, altering, and terminating the business. An individual can begin a single proprietorship business without obtaining a charter (as in the case of a corporation), or without working out agreements with partners (as in the case of a partnership). Except for the possible requirement of obtaining a license, he is free to engage in almost any legitimate business without further formality. He may alter the nature or objectives of his business and may even terminate its existence without consulting other persons and usually without the necessity and difficulty of obtaining government consent. 

A second advantage is the personal satisfaction derived from individual ownership and control. A sole proprietor does not need to confer with co-owners on matters of business policy or to be accountable to associates for his success or failure. He can keep his affairs secret. An individual owner enjoys all the profits of the enterprise.

The third advantage is promptness of action. As sole director of the enterprise, the individual proprietor can make major decisions with greater speed than can the partnership or the corporation. In these organizations several persons must give opinions on and consent to such matters as sale of the business, a change of policy, or liquidation. Also on matters of internal organization, the individual proprietor can reassign duties among his employees with a minimum of delay.

Disadvantages of the Single Proprietorship Form Opposed to these advantages, the single proprietorship has certain limitations and drawbacks which must be remembered.

One drawback to the sole proprietorship is its limited capital. 

A single individual rarely can amass the funds required for large scale, far-flung business.

A second disadvantage of this form of business organization is the unlimited personal liability for debts. The general intent of the law is that all the single proprietor's property (whether devoted to personal enjoyment or business use) shall be subject to the payment of his debts.

The third disadvantage of the sole proprietorship is the limited managerial ability. The success or failure of the single proprietorship usually depends on the judgment and ability of one person. 

Before making his decisions the owner may seek advice, but in the end, the responsibility is his alone. Sometimes two heads really are better than one, and an organization with more than one owner usually possesses a managerial ability that is both broader in scope and better balanced in judgment. 

THE PARTNERSHIP 

Numerous instances of strong and successful business partnerships exist. Stockbrokers and commodity brokers, contractors, miners, and merchants frequently function under the partnership form of organization. Small family businesses, such as husband and wife or father and son(s) combinations, regard the partnership form as the natural basis for their operations. Professional people, such as physicians, lawyers, and dentists also must use the partnership form when they desire to practice as joint owners; it is illegal for a corporation to practice such professions. 

The student of partnership accounting should be familiar with the legal requirements, specific characteristics, advantages, and limitations of the partnership form of organization. A knowledge of these fundamentals is essential. The principles of partnership accounting were developed largely because of the unique features of the partnership. Furthermore from the business standpoint, a study of the principles of partnership accounting highlights certain business concepts and relationships that are not so clearly evident in the other forms of organization. These principles fall into three general categories: (1) organization and capitalization, (2) distribution of profits, and (3) dissolution.

Legal Aspects of the Partnership 

The law of partnership is basically the result of the long experience known as the common law. Much of this experience, however, has been enacted by the various states into specific statutes. 

In 1914 a committee of lawyers drafted a Uniform Partnership Act, consisting of forty-five sections. Thirty-five states and Alaska have adopted this Act. In states which have not adopted the Uniform 

Partnership Act, the separate statutes control, but these laws usually are found to be similar since the force of circumstances is recognized by the law. The discussion of partnership law and accounting is based upon the Uniform Partnership Act. 

An understanding of the accounting representation of legal and business facts makes the objective facts themselves much easier to understand. Thus the study of the partnership and corporation law strongly complements the study of the parallel accounting.

The Uniform Partnership Act defines a partnership as follows: 

"A partnership is an association of two or more persons to carry on as co-owners of a business for profit."1

This definition indicates in a general way some of the basic characteristics of the partnership. Since a partnership is an association of individuals, it therefore has many of the features of the single proprietorship form and has some of the same advantages and limitations. However, the co-ownership of the partnership property and of the profits require discussion. 

A partnership comes into existence only by a legally binding agreement made between two or more persons. This agreement may be either written or oral. In forming a partnership it is always preferable that the agreement should be carefully prepared in writing and be signed by all the partners. This agreement serves as a set of rules by which the partners conduct themselves and their business. This agreement or contract frequently is known as the articles of copartnership. 

A partnership is a commercial marriage; it is rife with opportunities for disagreement. But as in all matrimony, forethought and reasonableness during the planning stages can reduce future 

1 Uniform Partnership Act, sec. 6 (1). States adopting the Uniform Partnership 

Act frequently use different section numbers.

disputes to a minimum. Written articles of copartnership that are carefully drawn aid greatly in the avoidance of discord. Oral agreements are of little value because of the latitude of interpretation by the partners and the difficulty of proof in court. In the absence of agreement between the partners, the applicable partnership law serves as a basis for settling partnership disputes; it also serves as a basis for regulating the relations of the partnership with outside parties. 

The Partnership Agreement 

When properly drawn, the articles of copartnership should contain at least the following information: 

1. The name and address of the partnership 

2. The name and address of each partner 

3. A statement concerning the kind of business in which the partnership is to engage 4. The duration of the partnership (Provision is often made for business life insurance, that is insurance on partners' lives so that, when a partner dies, funds may be available to pay his equity to his estate. Provision may also be made to pay the equity of a deceased partner in instalments.) 

5. The relationship of the partners to one another and to the partnership, with specific reference to: a. The rights and duties of each partner b. Capital contributions c. Withdrawals of capital d. Basis of dividing profits (1) The amount of interest to be allowed on capital or to be charged on withdrawals (2) Compensation for partners' services or abilities (partners' salaries) (3) The basis for dividing profits and losses if other than equal e. The time for closing the books to determine each partner's interest periodically 6. The method of terminating the partnership, including methods of appraising and of terminating the interests of deceased or otherwise incompetent partners.

It is usually desirable to have some provision in the articles for

the settlement of partners' disputes by arbitration. On occasion arbitration avoids long, expensive lawsuits and perhaps even prevents the dissolution of the firm. 

The partnership agreement may bind the partners by mutual consent to almost any arrangement about capital, profits, and management of the partnership. By unanimous consent, partners may qualify, amend, or rescind the partnership agreement at any time and in any respect and so continue peaceably. But where a partner tries to enforce what he considers to be his right, based on his interpretation of the agreement (or, in the absence of agreement on the point, upon his interpretation of partnership law), his final recourse is to bring suit on the issue and to abide by court decision.

The Uniform Partnership Act 

Individual sections of the Uniform Partnership Act provide for the specific characteristics of the partnership. These characteristics are presented in partial quotations from sections of the statute and with brief pertinent commentary. (Where italics appear in the quoted matter, they are used by the author of this text merely for emphasis.) 

1. Organization and capitalization. Section 24, Extent of prop erty rights of a partner, provides: "The property rights of a partner are (1) his rights in specific partnership property, (2) his interest in the partnership, and (3) his right to participate in the management." Thus a partner usually has an interest in the capital of the partnership, a share in the profits, and a voice in the management although the absence of any of the three rights would not necessarily preclude his being a partner. Note that rights (1) and (2) constitute the chief problems of partnership accounting. 

Section 2, Definition of terms, provides: "Person includes individuals, partnerships, corporations, and other associations." Thus a corporation could become a member of a partnership if its charter so provided.

Section 18, Rules determining rights and duties of partners, in regard to the admission of new members, provides: "No person can become a member of a partnership without the consent of all the partners.'

Section 9, Partner agent of partnership as to partnership busi-

ness, provides: "Every partner is an agent of the partnership for the purpose of its business, and the act of every partner, including the execution in the partnership name of any instrument, for apparently carrying on in the usual way the business of the partnership of which he is a member binds the partnership, unless the partner so acting has in fact no authority to act for the partnership in the particular matter, and the person with whom he is dealing has knowledge of the fact that he has no such authority." The partners may, of course, divide the managerial duties among themselves on the basis of special abilities, but any arrangement may be changed by them at will and has no effect on the rights of outside parties unacquainted with the internal agreements of the partnership.

Section 18 also provides: "All partners have equal rights in the management and conduct of partnership business." Thus, in the absence of agreement to the contrary, all partners have an equal voice in the management of the partnership regardless of the amounts of their capital investments. 

2. Distribution of profits. Section 40, Rules for distribution, provides: "The partners shall contribute, . . . the amount necessary to satisfy the liabilities; but if any, but not all, of the partners are insolvent,... the other partners shall contribute their share of the liabilities, and, in the relative proportions in which they share the profits, the additional amount necessary to pay the liabilities." 

It may be generally stated that except in a limited partnership, each partner is held personally liable for all debts of the partnership if they exceed the partnership assets, but in the event that he is forced to pay more than his share of losses, he may have recourse at law against the other partners to the extent of their deficiencies. Such recourse is subject to technical qualifications that involve different legal procedures in different states. 

Section 18 provides: ". subject to any agreement between them... Each partner shall be repaid his contributions, whether by way of capital or advances to the partnership property after all liabilities, including those to partners, are satisfied This provision explains the necessity for keeping a separate capital account for each member of a partnership, to determine the amount to which each shall be entitled upon his withdrawal from the partnership or upon dissolution. It also explains the necessity • •

for keeping partners' loan accounts when advances other than capital are made.

Section 25, Nature of a partner's right in specific partnership property, provides: "A partner is co-owner with his partners of specific partnership property . . ." The consequences of this provision and other portions of the law are such that, if A contributes $5,000 cash and B contributes $5,000 cash and $5,000 merchandise in the formation of a partnership, the transactions would be recorded thus: Cash

A, Capital 

Cash Merchandise

B, Capital 

5,000 5,000 5,000 5,000 10,000

In the absence of a contrary agreement, each partner is credited with the amount of his contribution, but the assets are debited to proper asset accounts, no effort being made to distinguish between A's assets and B's assets. Such assets have become partnership property in which A has a one-third interest (as determined by his capital account in relation to the total capital) and B has a twothirds interest.

Except by mutual consent, A or B would be denied the right to withdraw the specific assets contributed since such assets had become partnership property. The balances of the partners' capital accounts may be regarded, therefore, as measures of that portion of the assets intended to be left more or less permanently in the business. If a partner wrongfully withdraws his capital, he may be held liable for any resulting damages to the partnership or to other partners. Section 18 provides: ". subject to any agreement between them, . . . each partner shall . . . share equally in the profits . and must contribute towards the losses, whether of capital or otherwise, according to his share in the profits." In other words, when the partners have a specific provision in their partnership agreement as to the sharing of profits or losses, that agreement controls. In the absence of such a provision, however, the law provides that profits shall be shared equally regardless of the amount of capital invested, and that when there is an agreement as to

profits, but none as to losses, all losses shall then be shared in the same ratio as the profits are shared. 

Accountants find it necessary to know and to apply the profit and loss ratio in distributing partnership profits which is simply the ratio between the capital accounts balances. In reference to the distribution of profits, section 18 of the law provides, in substance, that, in the absence of agreement to the contrary, a partner who makes any payment or advance to or for the partnership, beyond the amount of capital which he agreed to contribute, shall be paid interest (at the legal rate) on such advance; that, in the absence of agreement or special circumstances, a partner is not allowed interest on capital invested; and that, in the absence of agreement, no partner is entitled to remuneration (usually referred to as partners' salaries) for services or ability contributed to the partnership.

Section 42, Rights of retiring partner or estate of deceased partner when the business is continued, provides: "When any partner retires or dies, and the business is continued . . . without any settlement of accounts as between him or his estate and the person or partnership continuing the business, unless otherwise agreed, he or his legal representative... may have the value of his interest at the date of dissolution ascertained, and shall receive as an ordinary creditor an amount equal to the value of his interest in the dissolved partnership with interest, or, at his option ... the profits attributable to the use of his right in the property of the dissolved partnership; provided that the creditors... shall have priority on any claim arising under this section..." 

3. Dissolution. Section 29, Dissolution defined, provides: "The dissolution of a partnership is the change in the relation of the partners caused by any partner ceasing to be associated in the carrying on as distinguished from the winding up of the business." 

Section 30, Partnership not terminated by dissolution, provides: "On dissolution the partnership is not terminated, but continues until the winding up of partnership affairs is completed." Both sections 29 and 30 use the expression winding up in the sense of liquidation, that is, the sale of the assets and payment of the proceeds to creditors and owners. Dissolution is thus distinguishable from liquidation. Every liquidation is a dissolution, but not every dissolution involves a liquidation.

Chief Causes of Partnership Dissolution 

Partnerships are dissolved for a number of reasons, among them the following: 

1. The sale of a partner's interest or the admission of a person to membership in the partnership. When a partner sells his interest to another person who thereupon, with the consent of all partners, becomes a member of the firm, or when a person is admitted as a new member of the partnership, the old partnership ceases to exist and a new one comes into being. 

2. The withdrawal of a partner either with or without the consent of the other partners. When a partner withdraws from the partnership with the consent of the remaining partners, there is, of course, a change in the partnership personnel and consequently a dissolution. When a partner withdraws his capital without the consent (or prior agreement) of the other partners, he may be liable to them in damages for any losses they may suffer as a consequence of the withdrawal. A withdrawing partner continues to be personally liable for partnership debts incurred prior to his withdrawal and must give to each creditor known to have done business with the firm notice of withdrawal in order to avoid liability for debts subsequently incurred. A notice of withdrawal published in a newspaper is usually deemed to be sufficient as to creditors who had no dealings with the old partnership prior to the withdrawal but who subsequently became creditors of the new partnership. 

3. Unanimous consent of the partners. The partners may agree, either in the original articles of copartnership or subsequently, that the partnership will be dissolved on a certain date and such agreement will operate to terminate the partnership on the date fixed in the agreement. Also, if the partnership was organized for some particular purpose or venture, the accomplishment of that purpose would terminate the partnership. 

4. Personal disability of any partner. The misconduct of any partner (failure to perform the obligations or duties agreed upon or acting in bad faith toward the partners) may cause a dissolution. 

In addition, death, insanity, or bankruptcy of a partner results in a dissolution of the firm. 

5. Bankruptcy of the partnership. When the firm is forced to

sell its assets to satisfy creditors either under the National Bankruptcy Act or under state insolvency acts, the partnership terminates. 

6. Illegal purpose. When the purpose for which the partnership was organized becomes illegal, the partnership ceases to exist for that purpose. 

7. Sale of the partnership. The sale of all the partnership property to another person, firm, or corporation dissolves the partnership. Dissolution also results where the partnership reorganizes and becomes a corporation. 

Advantages and Disadvantages of the Partnership Form As compared with the single proprietorship, the partnership form of organization enables a business to accumulate greater resources and to attain broader and more specialized managerial ability. As compared with the corporation, it has the advantages of relative freedom from governmental regulation as to permissible activities and freedom from the formalities of stockholders' and directors' meetings. Furthermore, it is generally true that profits earned through the partnership form are taxed less heavily than those earned through the corporate form. On the other hand, corporate profits may be the subject of Federal income taxation when earned by the corporation and may also constitute taxable income to stockholders when paid out to them as dividends. In other words, such profits may be considered loosely as subject to "double taxation."

The partnership form subjects the partners to the chief disadvantages of (1) unlimited personal liability for firm debts, (2) limited life, (3) nontransferability of ownership except by unanimous consent, and (4) the hazard of irreconcilable disputes. 

Kinds of Partnerships

The law classifies partnerships into two groups, general and limited. General partnerships, the chief characteristics of which have been presented, comprise (1) partnerships organized to operate for an indefinite length of time in the conduct of a general line of business and (2) partnerships organized for a particular period of time or for a specific purpose, upon the accomplishment of which the partnership is dissolved. For instance, two or more

building contractors might combine to undertake a particular construction project with no intention of continuing their partnership beyond its completion. 

Other examples of special purpose partnerships are the joint venture and the syndicate when they exist long enough so that they may be considered as carrying on a business. For example, two or more business concerns might combine forces as a joint venture to import and sell a single shipment of merchandise, to share the resulting gains or losses, and thereupon to dissolve. A syndicate usually refers to the temporary association of a group of investment bankers for the purpose of acquiring and selling a large issue of corporate stock or bonds, and of dividing among themselves the resulting gains or losses and thereupon dissolving the syndicate.

The limited partnership is defined by the Uniform Limited Partnership Act in section 1, Limited partnership defined, as follows: "A limited partnership is a partnership formed by two or more persons having as members one or more general partners and one or more limited partners. The limited partners as such shall not be bound by the obligations of the partnership." ...

Section 2, Formation, provides the manner of forming, operating, amending, and terminating a limited partnership, as follows: 

Formation.-(1) Two or more persons desiring to form a limited partnership shall (a) Sign and swear to a certificate, which shall state 

I. The name of the partnership,

II. The character of the business, III. The location of the principal place of business, IV. The name and place of residence of each member; general and limited partners being respectively designated, V. The term for which the partnership is to exist, VI. The amount of cash and a description of and the agreed value of the other property contributed by each limited partner, VII. The additional contributions, if any, agreed to be made by each limited partner and the times at which ... they shall be made,

VIII. The time, if agreed upon, when the contribution of each limited partner is to be returned, IX. The share of the profits or the other compensation by way of income which each limited partner shall receive by reason of his contribution.

(b) File for record the certificate in the office of [here designate the proper office].2

Some states require newspaper publication of the formation of a limited partnership. 

In addition to the foregoing, the act provides that a limited partner may contribute cash or other property but not services to the partnership; that his name shall not appear in the partnership name; that he shall not be liable as a general partner for partnership debts (beyond the amount already invested) unless he takes part in the control of the business; and that he may receive a share of the profits.

The failure of a limited partner to comply strictly with the provisions of the law would probably relegate him to the status of a general partner. 

Classes of Partners

The law recognizes three classes of partners: general partners, limited partners, and partners by estoppel. 

General partners and partners by estoppel are subject to unlimited personal liability for the debts of the partnership, whereas limited partners are not subject to liability for debts beyond the amount contributed by them to the partnership assets. General partners and limited partners acquire their status through being participants in the partnership agreement, whereas partners by estoppel acquire their status by operation of law and not as participants in the partnership agreement. 

1. General partners are characterized (unless otherwise agreed) by their rights to participate in the partnership assets, to share in the profits, and to have a voice in the management, as well as by personal liability for partnership debts (regardless of mutual agreement to the contrary). As a result of their ability to limit certain of their rights by agreement, two subclasses of general partners are occasionally referred to as dormant partners and secret partners. Dormant partners or silent partners are those partners who by agreement take no active part in the management of the partnership. A dormant partner is sometimes described as one who is both secret and silent, whereas a silent partner is not 2 The usual requirement is that the certificate shall be filed in the county clerk's office of the county in which the principal place of business of the partnership is located.

necessarily a secret partner. Secret partners are those partners who fully participate in the partnership assets, profits, and management, but they are not known as partners to outsiders. 

2. Limited partners are members of the partnership who have conformed to the requirements of the limited partnership law. 

3. Partners by estoppel are those partners who by virtue of conduct or admission cause outsiders to believe them to be members of a partnership and who, as a consequence, are estopped by law from denying that they are partners and hence are held liable as such.

THE CORPORATION 

Chief Justice Marshall of the United States Supreme Court once stated: "A corporation is an artificial being, invisible, intangible, and existing only in contemplation of law." In his Manual of 

Commercial Law, second edition, Spencer declares: "... a corporation is a collection or association of natural persons joined together by the authority of the state or government, with the capacity of continuous succession, and endowed with the power of acting in many respects as a single individual."4 

Legal Aspects and Basic Characteristics 

From a legal standpoint a corporation is deemed to have an existence separate and distinct from the existence of those natural or legal persons who participate in its ownership (that is, the stockholders). The chief characteristics of the corporation are as follows: 

1. It is created by law and it operates and can be terminated only under the authority or sanction of law. 

2. Its existence is frequently perpetual but may be for only a term of years. It exists by virtue of shares of ownership that are freely transferable either by sale or by the laws of inheritance. 

3. It possesses rights and powers that permit it to acquire, hold, 3 See Dartmouth College v. Woodward (1819), 4 Wheat. 518, 636, 4 L. ed. 629. 

4 Spencer, Edward W., A Manual of Commercial Law (2nd ed.), p. 310. The 

Bobbs-Merrill Company, Indianapolis, 1913

and sell property, and to transact general business in its own name and not in the name of its owners; it can sue and be sued at law, like a natural person. 

4. It is empowered to employ agents (directors and corporate officers) to perform its acts in its name. 

5. It bears sole responsibility for its own debts, and it is obligated to pay such debts from its own assets. In the absence of negligence, fraud, or other wrongdoing on the part of its agents and owners, they are free from any personal liability for the debts of the corporation. 

6. It has the rights and powers to contract with its owners as outsiders, to sue them, and to be sued by them. 

Whereas a partnership comes into existence as a result of agreement among the partners, individuals who would create a corporation must approach the proper government officer and must apply for a charter describing the rights and powers that the corporation shall have. When the charter has been received, the corporation's agents may transact business for and in the name of the corporation. 

Persons may be admitted to membership (as owners) by the issuance to them of shares of stock in exchange for cash or other property contributed to the corporation or for services rendered to it. 

Thus whereas the single proprietorship and the partnership may engage in almost any kind of business as long as they do not violate the general civil or penal laws, the corporation may not engage in any business or, in fact, undertake any transaction unless express or implied authority to do so is granted to it either by law or by its charter. The charter, however, may contain what is known as a general purpose clause that gives it very broad powers. The agents of the corporation, in seeking authority for their acts in its behalf, must look to the following sources of authority: 

1. The Constitution of the United States 

2. The federal laws (acts of Congress) and regulations of bureaus, commissions, and other agencies organized under those laws 3. The constitution of the state in which the corporation receives its charter 4. The general laws, particularly the stock corporation statutes, of the state in which the corporation receives its charter and the laws of other states in which it transacts business 5. Local laws and ordinances of counties, cities, and other gov-

erning bodies having authority over the corporation or its property 6. The charter granted to the corporation by the state in which it is organized 7. The bylaws of the corporation adopted by the stockholders 

8. The minutes of all the meetings of the stockholders 

9. The minutes of all the meetings of the directors. 

When a corporation attempts an act which is clearly beyond its express or implied powers, such an act is generally termed ultra vires and may have various consequences to the corporation and to its agents, depending on the possible interpretations of the courts. Thus it is important to distinguish carefully between the corporation as such and its stockholders, officers, and employees. 

Classes of Corporations 

There are a number of classes of corporations which originate under the laws of the several states. The terminology used to describe these classes may vary, but the ordinary business corporation, by whatever name it may be called, is common to all. 

The principles of corporation accounting in this text are related primarily to the business stock corporation. As defined in the New 

York law, for example, a stock corporation is one which issues shares of stock and is authorized by law to distribute dividends to stockholders; a business corporation is one that is formed under or subject to the stock corporation law and distinguished from corporations of a special nature formed under or subject to the banking law, the insurance law, the railroad law, the cooperative corporations law, or other applicable laws. 

The Creation of a Corporation: The Charter 

Most states have enacted general corporation laws that provide rules for the creation, operation, and termination of privately owned (stock) business corporations. Laws, as well as the procedure under them, vary widely. In the state of New York, whose law is fairly representative, individuals (not less than three) desiring to form a corporation are required to make a written application for a charter, or certificate of incorporation, addressed to the secretary of state. Upon its approval by the office of the secretary of state, this application becomes written evidence of the charter

and is effective from the date of its approval. The application must be signed by each applicant and must contain, in general, the following information: 

1. The name of the corporation. The name must be different from that of any other corporation organized or legally doing business within the state and must not contain the word bank, trust, insurance, or other designation which implies a business activity that is governed by special empowering statutes. 

2. The purposes for which the corporation is formed. With the exception of the practice of certain professions, such as law, dentistry, and medicine, and the operation of certain businesses, such as banking, trusts, or insurance, there are few restrictions as to the kinds of business in which the corporation may, upon application, receive permission to engage. Unless a power is requested in the application and is approved, the corporation acquires no such power. 

3. The amount of the capital stock and the number and par value (face value assigned in the charter) of shares which the corporation shall have the right to issue; or, if the corporation is to issue shares of no-par value, a statement giving the number of such shares and other information relating thereto. 

4. Complete information concerning the number of shares, par or no-par value, preferences, designations, privileges, voting powers, restrictions, and qualifications, if shares are to be issued in classes other than common stock. 

5. The names of the city (village or town) and county (within the state) in which the principal office of the corporation is to be located. 

6. The duration of the corporation, which may be perpetual, unless limited to a term of years under its charter or applicable laws. 

7. The number of directors, which must be not less than three. 

8. The names and addresses of the original directors, who are responsible for the conduct of the business until the first meeting of the stockholders, at which the directors for the succeeding year are elected. 

9. The name and address of each subscriber to the certificate of incorporation and the number of shares of stock which each agrees to take.

10. A statement to the effect that all the subscribers to the certificate of incorporation are of full age, that at least two-thirds of them are citizens of the United States, that at least one of them is a resident of the state of New York, and that at least one of the persons named as a director is a citizen of the United States and a resident of the state of New York. 

11. If meetings of the board of directors are to be held only in 

New York, the certificate or bylaws must contain a specific provision to that effect.

Customarily, three copies of the certificate of incorporation are prepared and mailed to the office of the secretary of state. Upon his approval (evidenced by signature and seal), two copies are returned to the office of the corporation; one of them is retained and the other is filed with the clerk of the county in which the principal place of business of the corporation is located. Certain fees must be paid for filing with the secretary of state and with the county clerk, and an organization tax, based upon the number of shares authorized to be issued, must be paid. 

Internal Management of the Corporation 

The ownership of the corporation is vested in the stockholders, who, unless restricted in some way by virtue of the class of stock acquired, are entitled to one vote for every share of stock owned and may so vote on any matter properly before the stockholders for decision. The power to adopt and amend a set of bylaws is vested in the stockholders of the corporation, but they may delegate this authority to the directors. The stockholders, by vote, elect the directors at their first meeting and thereafter at annual meetings. They may authorize the issue of capital stock and in many states, delegate this authority to the board of directors, although stockholders generally may not delegate the power to increase or decrease the capital or the authorized number of shares of stock.

The ability of the stockholder owners of the corporation to delegate powers to a few persons, namely, the board of directors, for the purpose of central management is one feature of the corporate organization that has enabled many corporations to attain great size and economic power. Many stockholders, widely scattered geographically, frequently delegate the right to vote at

stockholders' meetings, subject of course to their right to revoke such delegation at any time. The delegation of the right to vote is effected by the granting of a proxy. 

The directors, elected as stated, in turn elect the officers of the corporation, who have direct charge of its activities. The officers usually consist of a president, a vice president, a treasurer, and a secretary, who customarily divide the responsibility of managing the internal functions of the organization, making periodic reports to the directors and stockholders. The laws of the different states vary considerably on these points. Advantages of the Corporate Form Some of the advantages afforded by the corporate form of organi zation are as follows: 

1. The corporation may accumulate relatively large amounts of capital. As compared with the single proprietorship and the partnership, the corporation can accumulate greater capital because a large number of persons contribute the capital as owners. 

Therefore, corporations are enabled to undertake ventures of much larger proportions than may be undertaken through other forms of organization. 

2. The stockholders possess limited liability. In general private property of the stockholders is not subject to the debts of the corporation. This feature encourages people to invest in corporations; such people might hesitate to subject their private property to the risks involved in the partnership. 

3. The shares of ownership are transferable. The stockholder has the right to sell, pledge, give, or bequeath his stock, usually without affecting the affairs of the corporation in any manner. 

4. The corporate form lends itself to efficient centralized management. The nature of its internal organization permits centralized control of the corporation's activities, and the large amount of resources of some corporations enables them to employ executives of the highest caliber. 

5. The corporation has continuity of existence. Unlike the single proprietorship and the partnership, the corporation need not dissolve or liquidate because of the death, insanity, or bankruptcy of any stockholder, inasmuch as his shares are transferable and may pass to another.

Disadvantages of the Corporate Form The corporate form of organization has certain limitations, as follows: 

1. It is subject to considerable legal regulation and restriction. 

The law does not permit corporations to engage in certain kinds of business. The law restricts and regulates them in many ways in the conduct of an authorized business, particularly in the case of public utility, banking, trust, and insurance companies. Many reports and formalities of various kinds are frequently required by law. 

2. Minority stockholders are in a weak and powerless position. 

As contrasted with a partner's equal voice in the management, a stockholder who owns less than fifty per cent of the total shares issued may find himself with no voice in the management if the other shareholders are opposed to his views. 

3. Double taxation is one of the disadvantages of the corporation. The Federal Revenue Act imposes a tax on the net income of a corporation. When any part of the corporate net income remaining after taxes is paid to stockholders in the form of dividends, such dividends also are subject to federal (and frequently state) income taxes imposed on each recipient. 

Corporation Accounting 

Corporation accounting includes accounting for different classes of capital stock, for capital stock subscriptions, for bonds, for dividends, for surplus, and for surplus reserves. These aspects of accounting are presented in subsequent chapters relative to the issue of stock, the declaration and payment of dividends, the acquisition of treasury stock, and so forth.

The Banker's Interest in the Form of Organization The banker must be careful to visualize the ramifications of the legal structure of the borrower. When John Jones requests a loan for "his" grocery business, the actual contract may be made between the bank and the Jones Corporation in which John Jones holds a controlling stock interest. In this case Jones' private wealth is not a security for the proposed loan; only the actual assets of the corporation are security for the loan. On the other hand, the Jones 

Grocery business actually may be a partnership with the entire

private wealth of partners Smith and Wilson as well as that of Jones liable for the amount of the loan, even though Jones is the active partner in the business and manages it, in most respects, as though he were in fact the sole owner. 

The credit picture of a business may show a very different impact of tax liabilities for current and past periods, depending on whether the business is organized as a single proprietorship, a partnership, or a corporation. Since unpaid taxes are high priority liabilities, these differences may actually be the determining factors in appraising the safety of a loan. Other complex considerations must be clearly understood in dealing with a borrowing business. If the bank's equity is to be fully protected, the banker must carefully weigh such factors as the rights of the principal individuals involved as stockholders or partners with regard to salaries, drawings, or specific aspects of management authority.

CHAPTER II PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

ACCOUNTING for the formation of a partnership may be very simple or relatively complex depending on the realities of the situation. 

At the simplest formation we may have two or three individuals contributing equal amounts of cash. For this simple situation the following entry would be made in the journal for the newly formed partnership: Cash

Jones, Capital Smith, Capital Wilson, Capital 

30,000 10,000 10,000 10,000

If there were no further specifications in the partnership agreement on this point, the three men would presumably, though not necessarily, share equally the responsibilities of management as well as the net income. In designing the remainder of the accounting system (that is the accounts for other assets, and for liabilities, and for revenues and expenses) and in designing the specialized journals, the accountant would proceed in exactly the same way as in the case of the single proprietorship. This part of the work depends on the nature and volume of business expected and it is not affected by the fact that the business is legally organized as a partnership.

VARIATIONS IN PARTNERSHIP FORMATION 

Beyond the simplest pattern of organization, the conditions may vary in two ways. First, the partners may contribute differing amounts of cash, and the journal entry would be made in the following manner:

Cash 

Jones, Capital Smith, Capital Wilson, Capital 

60,000 30,000 20,000 10,000 22

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

23 In this case the capital accounts clearly assume their basic function-to record the varying contributions of the partners. The fact that their capital accounts vary considerably does not indicate that the partners have corresponding rights in control. It is well established that partners have equal rights in control regardless of the fact that their capital contributions may not have been equal in amount.

The basic principle of equal control is often amended, however, by specific agreement among the partners to divide the routine management duties and responsibilities among themselves in accordance with their special abilities. Thus if Jones is known to be a superb salesman, Smith a production genius, and Wilson a financial wizard, the partnership agreement sensibly may provide that Jones shall be the sales manager, Smith the production manager, and Wilson the administrative director. In fact the opportunity to combine such varied capacities may be the most important reason which causes them to decide upon the formation of the partnership for the subsequent conduct of their business enterprise. 

Despite such special delegations of duties, the partners are still regarded as equal partners when there arise basic matters of control that are beyond the routine management of their special areas. Incidentally, of course, it may take $60,000 to finance their proposed businesss venture, and therefore, the opportunity to combine their resources through the partnership form of organization is a considerable advantage to the partners in the conduct of the business.

The fact that capital contributions are equal, in the first illustration, and that the contributions are in a 3:2:1 ratio in the second does not necessarily establish the ratios in which the profits and losses are to be shared. Statements referring to the partners as "equal partners" or as partners having a "3:2:1 capital ratio" refer only to the capital contributions and not to the control or to the profit sharing. 

Entries Showing Contribution of Noncash Assets 

When the organization pattern varies the second way, one (or more) of the partners contributes assets other than cash to the formation of the partnership. In this case it is best to show the

When these journal entries are posted and a statement of condition is drawn up from the resulting accounts, we have the portrayal of a typical small business partnership, as shown in Figure 1.

(on formation of the partnership) The accounting entries in Figure 1 are rather obvious. Some of the business factors lying behind them, however, are less apparent but are of very real importance. In this illustration for example, before the agreement to join could be made, the pros-

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

25 pective partners, especially Wilson, should consider the propriety of the asset valuations to be used in the entries for the original contributions. Presumably Wilson's cash presents no problem of appraisal but the proper value to be placed on the other assets might involve lengthy argument and eventual compromise. Smith may have claimed that the value of his real estate was $25,000. 

Jones may have argued that the value of his merchandise was $30,000, his store equipment $3,500, and his receivables $6,000. 

If these valuations had been accepted without question, the journal entries would have been similar in form but different in amounts. The statement of condition would have been in perfect balance but its asset values and the capital accounts of Jones and Smith would have been inflated considerably. These valuations would have constituted a misleading portrayal of the facts. They would have been clearly disadvantageous to Wilson whose capital was correctly valued. Any oversight of an asset or error in its valuation during the organization of a partnership would prove significant subsequently when the business is sold or liquidated. 

If prospective partners are fully aware of their interests, they carefully review the values to be accepted, or possibly resort to arbitration by disinterested outsiders to establish the values. An audit by a certified public accountant might reasonably establish the values of the accounts receivable, the merchandise, and the store equipment. Possibly an appraisal company might have to be called in to obtain a reliable valuation for the real estate. 

A variation in the pattern of entries occurs when a partnership is formed by the addition of cash or other specific assets of one or more persons to a business in operation when that business already has an adequate accounting system. In this case the business may continue to use the old accounting system, showing entries for the new assets and capital accounts as though the old business is continuing. Although this system may be workable it tends to obscure the fact that a new legal unit, a new partnership, has come into existence. Under this method there is also less chance to revalue the assets of the old business which may be necessary to effect an equitable showing for the incoming partner(s). 

Closely related to this situation is that in which an existing partnership adds another partner. In this case the partnership's accounting system is usually continued in use. Again it is possible

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

to make the entries properly to reflect the equities of the several partners. But the need for revaluations must be watched very carefully. 

So far, we have not considered the possibility that the assets being contributed to the business may have liabilities attached to them in such a way that the partnership may have to assume those liabilities. In our illustration for instance, if the real estate contributed by Smith is subject to a $6,000 mortgage, Smith obviously is not really contributing $20,000 of value to the business. Smith's contribution is no more than $14,000, which is the difference between the agreed value of the real estate and the mortgage thereon. The entry to effect Smith's contribution and the assumption of the mortgage by the partnership is: 

Land 

Building 

Mortgage Payable Smith, Capital 

2,500 17,500 6,000 14,000 The same basic relationships prevail when the liabilities are part of a going business rather than a claim on some particular asset and when the going business comprises a new partner's contribution to the business. In our illustration for example, if Jones has accounts payable $1,800, notes payable $2,500, and accrued wages (wages owed) $200 at the time the partnership is organized, the entry reflecting his contribution is:

Cash Merchandise

Accounts Receivable Store Equipment Accounts Payable Notes Payable Accrued Wages Jones, Capital

In reading the accompanying examples and in working the problems of this chapter, the student banker should place himself in the position of the supposed partner and ask, "Would I be willing to have the entries made in that way? Do such entries properly reflect my equity in the business?”

Partnership Net Income and Drawings 

The pattern of entries used in accounting for the determination of profits in the partnership (current entries, adjusting entries, and closing entries leading up to the figure of net income for the period) falls into the same pattern as in the single proprietorship and in the corporate form of business. Note, however, that the partnership as such does not pay federal income taxes on its profits so this item is omitted from the partnership picture. The partners do not escape these taxes, however, since each partner is taxed individually on his distributive share of the net income regardless of whether he has withdrawn it. The law requires the partnership to file an informational partnership return of income reporting the net income of the business and the share which each partner has in it.

When the net income to the proprietorship has been determined, there remains the unique problem of how properly to present the division of that net income among the several business partners.

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

In the single proprietorship the final entry is usually made closing the net income from the Expense and Revenue account into the proprietor's Capital account as follows: 

Expense and Revenue John Dawson, Capital 

16,300 16,300 Basically the same technique is used for a partnership since the net income for the accounting period constitutes additional capital. 

However there are two complicating factors. First, the division of the net income must be calculated in accordance with the partnership agreement, which in this respect may be either extremely simple or relatively complex.

Second, the partners usually wish their capital accounts to show their original capital contributions, which constitute permanent equities, undisturbed by temporary increases resulting from their net incomes or by decreases resulting from their withdrawals. To accomplish this segregation of permanent capital from temporary capital, a Drawing or Personal account is established for each partner. Temporary changes in capital are reflected in Drawing accounts. Thus if we assume that the partnership provides for equal shares and that the year's net income is $9,000, the entry for the division of this net income is: 

Expense and Revenue 

Jones, Drawing 

Smith, Drawing 

Wilson, Drawing 9,000 3,000 3,000 3,000

It is obvious that as the partners withdraw cash for personal use, the entries should be: 

Jones, Drawing

Cash 

Smith, Drawing

Cash 200 200 350 350 It is generally assumed that each partner is entitled to draw an amount of cash equal to his share in the net income as reflected by the credit balance in his Drawing account. He may describe this withdrawal as "drawing out his profits," but it should be clear that he is really drawing cash to the amount of his share of the net income.

Any credit balance in his Drawing account must be recognized as capital, even though he may intend to withdraw it in a relatively

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

29 short time. The statement of condition for Jones, Smith, and 

Wilson after the $9,000 of net income has been shared and after Jones and Smith have withdrawn $200 and $350 respectively is shown in Figure 3.

A practical financial problem that is closely related to partnership accounting is illustrated in Figure 3. Although the partners are entitled to withdraw a total of $8,450 in accordance with the credit balances in their Drawing accounts, they have managed their financial affairs in such a way that it is impossible to withdraw such large amounts of cash. The low cash balance of $5,950 may be explained by the sizable increases in accounts receivable, merchandise, and store equipment. These changes may represent entirely proper and intentional expansion of the business. The partners may have made these changes deliberately. They must realize, however, that they cannot use the cash for such expansion and also expect to have cash available for withdrawal. This type of expansion is usually known as plowing the profits back into the business, and it is typical of today's expansion-minded business world. If the partners are able now to restrict themselves in their cash withdrawals all is well. A portion of the balance in each drawing account becomes permanent capital. But if they persistently draw cash to the full amount of their respective drawing account balances, their business will soon find itself short of cash. If the partners then ask the bank for assistance, it would be very desirable for the banker to understand how the partners got themselves into difficulty in order to reach an intelligent decision. Bankers should be aware of the fact that the distinction between capital in the Capital account and the capital in the Drawing account is relative. The drawing account balances may range from temporary to permanent depending on varying conditions and policies. 

Ideally the partners ought to reach a specific decision as to what portion of the earnings are to be left in the business. They should order the bookkeeper to close such amounts into the Capital accounts.

Drawing accounts occasionally develop debit balances. If a partner has withdrawn all the credits in his Drawing account but needs more money, he may be allowed to draw additional amounts against his anticipated share of current profits, which have not as yet been determined and entered on the books. Such a withdrawal

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

31 is entered the same way as any other withdrawal entry. When posted to a Drawing account it creates a debit balance therein. 

When a statement of condition is prepared while there is a debit balance in a Drawing account, that balance is shown as a subtraction from the partner's Capital account. 

Withdrawals may cause an awkward situation between partners. 

Assume for instance, that one partner habitually withdraws all his profits soon after they are credited to his Drawing account and then draws additional amounts to create a debit balance therein, while the other partners exercise restraint and maintain considerable credits in their Drawing accounts. Assume further that the business is trying to expand and that the cash is tight and entails frequent borrowing from the bank. The provident partners might feel that their free-drawing partner was taking undue advantage of them and thus become embittered toward him. To prevent such a situation with its dangerous potential, the partners should make specific provision in the partnership agreement as to the partners' withdrawal rights. For instance: (1) withdrawals creating a debit balance could be prohibited entirely, (2) the written consent of all partners could be required for such withdrawals, or (3) partners could be penalized for their debit balances by the imposition of interest charges thereon at a high rate. Airtight clauses of this kind in the partnership agreement, providing for all eventualities, offer assurance of enduring relationships among the partners. 

Partnership Profit-Sharing Agreements 

The equal sharing of partnership profits, which in the absence of specific agreement is assumed by law, often does not do justice as between the partners. If the amounts of capital vary significantly, the fact should be recognized in the profit-sharing agreement. 

One way to recognize this fact is to divide all profits in proportion to capital contributions. This arrangement is fair when the capital is the predominant factor in the partnership business. In many businesses, however, capital is only one of several significant factors. The capital contribution can receive partial weight in the profit-sharing plan by stipulating that the partners be credited for interest on their Capital accounts at a stated rate. Any profit distributed by use of an interest rate is shared in proportion to capital.

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

The time, energy, intelligence, or other specific capacities that the partners contribute to the service of the business may be more significant than the dollar value of the capital contributed. If all partners are equally capable and devote full time to the business, they should be recompensed by sharing the profit equally. If, however, one partner is acknowledged to be more astute than the others or if he makes a larger contribution than the others by expending more time or by assuming greater management responsibilities than they do, he should be rewarded accordingly. He can be rewarded by giving him a fixed dollar share of the net income, which is often designated as salary to the managing partner. Another way to compensate the business manager is to give him an arbitrary advantage in the profit-sharing ratio agreed upon. 

Thus if partners A, B, and C make capital contributions of $30,000, $20,000, and $10,000 (that is in a 3:2:1 ratio) and if partner A is to be compensated for performing extra duties, the profit-sharing ratio could be fixed as 4:2:1. This ratio favors partner A by giving him profits in excess of his capital ratio. If the business should yield profits that are very high, an advantageous profit-sharing ratio would be more beneficial to A than a fixed salary.

One final factor is contributed by each partner, namely his ability to take a risk. Because partnerships have unlimited liability, the risk taken by a partner is not actually in proportion to his capital contribution to the business but in proportion to his total personal fortune. Assume that A, B, and C each contribute $10,000. If C is considerably wealthier than the other partners and if the ability to obtain credit is very important to the contemplated business, then C's contribution is unique. One way to reward him for this unique contribution is to establish the profit sharing at a ratio of 1:1:2, or 3:3:4, or 10:10:11, or at whatever arbitrary ratio that properly expresses the degree of importance to be attached to the use of his personal credit rating. 

The actual partnership profit-sharing agreement evolves as an attempt to recognize all these factors and to weigh each factor properly. Under various circumstances the partners' several contributions may change during the lifetime of the business, and the profit-sharing agreement may need to be amended to recognize the new facts. The partners have the right to change their agree-

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

33 ment by mutual consent at any time. When advising the prospective partner, the banker should be aware of the intricacy of the underlying factors so that the agreement when signed is fair to all parties for the foreseeable duration of the partnership. 

To illustrate: Assume that partners Hanson, Mork, and Clark have Capital accounts of $50,000, $30,000, and $20,000 respectively. 

Assume that the profit-sharing agreement provides for an annual salary share to Clark of $5,000, interest of 6% on their Capital accounts, and remaining profits to be shared equally. Assume the partnership net income for the year is $21,500. The journal entries in Figure 4 and the T-accounts in Figure 5 demonstrate the profit sharing of this business. JOURNAL Page 62

NET LOSSES AND THE PROFIT-SHARING AGREEMENT 

Partners normally expect that their operations will be profitable and establish their profit-sharing agreement accordingly. A year of poor business, however, may cause them to suffer a net operating loss or to earn a net income that is too small to cover the shares prescribed, such as salary to the managing partner and interest on the Capital accounts. Sometimes the partnership agreement includes a specific provision for loss-sharing ratios. If no such special ratio is provided the final losses must be shared in the profit ratio. 

Provisions regarding salary and interest shares must be observed even though net income is insufficient to cover them. If the entries JOURNAL Page 62

To illustrate: Assume the same profit-sharing agreement for 

Hanson, Mork, and Clark, but assume that the net income to be distributed is only $3,500. The entries in Figure 6 and Figure 7 demonstrate the reasoning to be followed. 

As this agreement works out Hanson has a final net income of $500 for the year, Mork has a final net loss of $700 for the year, and Clark has a final net income of $3,700. Although Clark seems to fare better than the others, one should remember that Clark has contributed a year's effort for which he normally receives $5,000. 

Even if there had been an operating net loss in place of the $3,500 net income, the entries for salary and interest would be made in the same way. The resulting deficiency (final net loss) would have been still larger, and it would still have been shared at the 1:1:1 ratio.

Average Capital 

In some partnerships the partners may contribute and withdraw substantial amounts of capital during a fiscal period to meet their firm's fluctuating need for funds. A partnership that is engaged in the contracting and construction business, for example, occasionally needs extensive funds, whereas during slack periods it requires a relatively smaller supply and the partners invest or withdraw capital accordingly. In such cases if interest is to be allowed on capital accounts, or if the entire net profits are to be shared in the capital ratio, it is desirable that the average capital of each partner be determined and used as a basis. The use of the amount in the capital account either at the beginning or at the end of the fiscal period would simply not coincide with the actual contributions to the welfare of the business. 

A satisfactory (although not the only) method of determining the average balance of an account over a fiscal period is (1) to multiply the amount of the opening balance by the number of days that elapsed to the next entry in the account, (2) to determine each new balance and multiply it by the number of days to the next change in balance, and so on to the end of the period, (3) to total the resulting day-dollars, and (4) to divide this total by the number of days in the fiscal period. The result is the average

PARTNERSHIP ACCOUNTING 

capital. An illustration showing the calculation of average balances over a three-month period, June 30 to September 30 (92 days), serves to clarify the procedure. 

Assume that the Capital accounts of A and B appear as follows: 19XX 9/27 

A, Capital 

19XX 

B, Capital 

19XX 19XX

6,000 6/30 Bal. 10,000 7/15 5,000 7/27 The accounts indicate that A's opening balance is $10,000 and B's is $20,000; A's closing balance is $9,000 and B's is $15,000. 

The calculation of A's average balance is made in the following manner: 

Opening balance, unchanged to July 27. 

July 27 balance, unchanged to Sept. 27.. 

Sept. 27 balance, unchanged to Sept. 30.

$735,000 These average capitals may be used as a basis for calculating interest at the agreed rate or as a basis for determining the capital ratio. For example, if it is desired to divide a three-month profit of $6,000 between A and B in capital ratio, the calculation is made in the following manner: 13,336.96 

X $6,000 $3,752.29, A's share. 21,326.09 7,989.13 21.326.09 × $6,000 = $2,247.71, B's share.

The denominator, $21,326.09, is the total average capital ($13,336.96 plus $7,989.13).

CHAPTER III  

THE THREE remaining situations which are unique in partnership accounting are discussed in this chapter, namely, the admission of additional partners, loans to partners, and the dissolution of the partnership. Although these situations are not encountered with great frequency, each of them is concerned with basic business and accounting relationships.

Admission of Additional Partners 

Legally the addition of one or more new partners to an existing partnership terminates the old partnership and creates a new one. 

Obviously, all the provisions of the original agreement concerning capital contributions must be rewritten in the new partnership agreement. Very likely the management duties will be redivided. 

The income-sharing provisions must be amended to reflect the new partner's shares. Possibly the provisions of the prior agreement with regard to drawings need revision or strengthening. It would be very unwise to drift into what is technically a new contract with the vague hope that the old agreement will cover the situation adequately.

In spite of what has been said about the need for an entirely new partnership contract to provide for all legal details, the accounting for the admission of new partners is usually handled on the books as though the old partnership were continuing, with certain changes. The following illustrations are built on this basis. 

In general there are two ways in which a new partner can enter an existing partnership. He may enter as an additional partner, or he may buy out (that is, take the place of) one of the old partners. In either case careful attention should be given as to the accuracy of the asset and liability valuations on the books of the old partnership. If all of these valuations are not adjusted to the mutual satisfaction of all concerned, inequities will probably arise in the division of the profits, as well as at the time of the subse- 47

quent sale of specific assets or at the final liquidation and dissolution of the firm. 

The statements of condition in Figure 10 are used as a basis for our discussion of partnership accounting.

NELSON AND LEE Statement of Condition January 1, 19XX ASSETS Current Assets 

$ 45,000 Liabilities

Fixed Assets 

60,000

Proprietorship:

H. Nelson, Capital.. $50,000

The Admission of a New Partner 

A new partner, W. After these adjustments, Payne will have a one-third interest in the partnership by contributing $46,500 in cash to the business. The entries to be made and the resulting statements of condition are as follows:
