FIGURE 10B. STATEMENT OF CONDITION OF A PARTNERSHIP AFTER ADMISSION OF A NEW PARTNER 

It should be noted that after Payne is admitted to the partnership, his capital constitutes one-third of the total capital. It does not necessarily follow that he will receive one-third of the subsequent profits. The latter point should be determined by the partners when they arrange their new income-sharing agreement, in which all the factors must be taken into consideration. 

It should be noted also that the revaluations made preparatory to Payne's admission increased the Capital accounts of the two old partners by $18,000. This increase made it necessary for Payne to pay $46,500 to acquire a one-third interest in the partnership, whereas he could have acquired his one-third interest for $37,500 if the valuations of the original statement of condition had been accepted as correct. Against this background, note that before the new partner is admitted, it is to the old partners' advantage to write up the assets (and write down the liabilities) whereas it is to the incoming partner's advantage to write down the assets (and to write up the liabilities). An appreciation of these accounting relationships may save many dollars for the businessman involved in such a situation whereas blundering into a reorganization without understanding may prove to be very costly to him. 

The Purchase of a Partner's Interest 

If, instead of becoming a third partner, Payne simply buys out Lee's interest, we have different entries. Since the cash is paid by

Payne directly to Lee, the cash transaction is not reflected on the partnership books at all. Before deciding the amount to be paid to Lee, Payne and Lee should discuss and agree on the revaluation of the old partnership assets and liabilities. As we saw in the previous illustration, the revaluations resulted in a credit to Lee of $9,000, so that the proper purchase price for his equity would be $34,000.

The best plan is to make the revaluations before and then to show the change in the partnership by the entry: 

D. Lee, Capital W. Payne, Capital 

34,000 34,000

There is, however, no particular pressure to recognize the revaluations on the partnership books. The entry for the sale of 

Lee's equity to Payne is often made by the simple debit and credit: 

D. Lee, Capital W. Payne, Capital 

25,000 25,000

This simple entry might, however, prove to be a real hardship to Nelson if the new profit-sharing agreement gives him a lesser share than the old one gave him. Any subsequent sale of the fixed assets, for instance, would probably involve the sharing of a profit -part or all of which accrued during the lifetime of the old Nelson and Lee regime. If the new Nelson and Payne profit agreement gives Nelson less than one-half, he would lose part of the profit to which he is legitimately entitled. On the other hand this arrangement would benefit Payne unfairly, since he would receive an improper share of the profit accrued before his admission. 

A somewhat similar situation is encountered when a partnership is incorporated. In this case new capital usually comes into the business through the sale of stock to new investors while the former partners take stock in exchange for their equity in the business. Although the situation is basically similar to that occurring on the admission of new partners, the basic forms of corporate accounting must be studied before these entries can be developed fully.

Partners Loans 

Occasionally, a partner may make an advance of cash to the partnership rather than have the business borrow from an outside

source. Sometimes such borrowings are evidenced by promissory notes signed by all the partners. In such cases it is clear that the transaction should be treated as entirely distinct from capital. 

The note should be shown among the liabilities in a separate account, for example, Loan from Partner A. Any interest paid or accrued on such a loan should not be confused with provisions for interest in the capital accounts. Such interest should be debited to interest expense and credited to cash, if cash is paid, or to the partner's drawing account if he wishes to withdraw the cash at a latter time.

Theoretically, it is also possible for the partnership to make a loan to a partner, although the provisions of the agreement concerning withdrawals normally cover this situation. If such a loan is made, it should be evidenced by a note and treated as to accounting, interest, and collection just the same as any other note due. 

The preceding statements regarding loans to and from partners describe the situation from the standpoint of a going concern. In event of bankruptcy, the courts would probably require that the loans be treated as capital transactions rather than as strictly armslength, outside transactions. 

Dissolution of the Partnership

Although the dissolution of a partnership is not a very common phenomenon, it is instructive to study the entries necessary to reflect such final breakup. Technically, when a new partner is taken in or when an old partner withdraws from the business there is a dissolution of the old partnership. These situations are usually handled by the accountant as reorganizations rather than as dissolutions. In this section we are interested in those dissolutions which result in the final liquidation of the partnership business. 

Final liquidation might result in a profit, for instance, when the liquidation is caused by the selling of a going concern to a business chain. In this case there is a final net income, perhaps very sizable, which must be paid to the partners under their rules for sharing the profit.

The more typical dissolution, however, involves the sale of business assets at sacrifice prices with the result that a considerable loss must be distributed before final payments can be made to the partners. It should be obvious too that these large losses usually

more than absorb any credit balances which the partners have in their drawing accounts. The distinction between the drawing accounts as temporary capital and the capital accounts as permanent capital becomes academic since on dissolution the whole situation is fluid and temporary. Thus it is usually advisable to close any debit or credit drawing account balances into the capital accounts as soon as dissolution is decided upon. As long as there is any possibility that a partner's capital account may not be large enough to stand his share of the losses, it would be bad practice to pay to him any amount apparently due him in his drawing account or in a loan account. Certainly no such payments should be made to any partner until creditors' claims are settled. 

Partners' income-sharing agreements involving salaries or interest on capital accounts are based on the concept of normal fiscal periods, and it is doubtful whether they should be considered to be in force during a period of liquidation. Certainly if an outside administrator undertakes to handle the liquidation, partners' salary arrangements should usually be considered terminated. 

These matters may very easily become the subject of litigation, in which case the decisions of the court must be followed. 

To make the problem simple and to bring out the basic relationships, assume that the liquidation takes place a very short time after the closing of a normal accounting period for the business. Thus the partners' salary and interest arrangements may be disregarded.

Though there my be some overlapping of these steps in actual practice, the following steps, taken in the order shown, are normal in the typical dissolution. 

1. The sale of assets for cash with the recognition of the loss (or profit) thereon usually in a special Liquidation Profit and Loss account. 

2. The payment of all liabilities. However this step may have to wait for steps three and four if sufficient cash to pay the liabilities is not realized in step one. 

3. The division of the net loss (or profit), as shown by the 

Liquidation Profit and Loss account, among the partners' accounts in their loss-sharing ratio. The debit may be made to the partners' drawing accounts to the extent of any credit balances therein and to their capital accounts for the remainder.

4. The collection of cash from any partners whose capital accounts have developed debit balances after the losses and any loan balances have been closed to capital accounts. If such balances cannot be collected because of the personal insolvency of the partners, these amounts must be regarded as additional losses to be debited to the remaining partners in their loss-sharing ratio. This step is actually an implementation of the unlimited liability provision of partnership law. At worst, it may cause some one partner, who is personally solvent, to stand the losses for all the others who are actually insolvent or who succeed by devious methods in hiding their assets from the hands of the law. 

5. Assuming that step one or step four or both have produced enough cash to pay all liabilities and that some cash is still available to the partners, step five results in the distribution of all remaining cash to the partners in accordance with the credit balances in their capital accounts. (See also statements regarding partners loans earlier in this chapter.) It is sometimes stated that this final step comprises the payment of cash to the partners in their capital ratio, but this is an unnecessarily involved concept. All that is really happening is that each partner is paid. the exact credit balance of his capital account, and there is no point in regarding this step as based on a mathematical ratio. 

If entries are made properly to implement these steps, all accounts balance completely and the books are finally closed. 

Finally if it is impossible to collect enough cash from any of the partners to satisfy the creditors completely, the cash that is available is paid to the creditors on a pro rata basis.¹ 

Thereafter, the debit balances in partners' capital accounts will be equal to the credit balances in the creditors' accounts. 

A final entry may be made closing these balances. This entry will reflect the final assumption of loss by the creditors. 

The steps normally involved in a dissolution may be clarified by an illustration. Assume that the statement of condition, Figure 11, represents the status of Brown, Miller, and Wills, a partnership, at the close of a year of poor business which causes them to decide to liquidate and dissolve.

SUMMARY 

3,200 2,550 5,750 In accounting for the partnerships, it is essential to understand their unique aspects, and to give effect to the provisions of the law and to the provisions of the specific partnership agreement. 

The formation of a partnership, its reorganization because of the admission of a new partner or the release of an old one, the distribution of current profits and losses, and the final liquidation and dissolution are the principal occasions on which unique problems are encountered. Routine accounting for the large volume of current business transactions is no different for the partnership than for the single proprietorship or the corporation. 

An additional significant and fairly common problem is the incorporation of a going business previously organized as a partnership. A discussion of this problem must await until we have studied basic accounting for corporations.

CHAPTER IV CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

THE LEGAL background and status of the business corporation are portrayed in Chapter I. The economics of the corporation and the resulting accounting and business steps that are taken in organizing the corporation are presented in this chapter. 

Some Advantages of the Corporate Form of Organization By virtue of the ability to issue stock, the corporation can break down its proprietary equity into a large number of shares. Thus it is enabled to gather contributions from an indefinitely large number of owners. This advantage which it has over the single proprietorship and partnership results in the corporate form being the predominant form used for the organization of large businesses. 

A second, closely related advantage is the ability of the corporation to issue two or more different kinds of stocks; thus it appeals to persons with widely differing investment requirements. Every business corporation issues common stock. The owners of the common stock are most nearly in the position of partners in that they are the organizers, the active managers, and the recipients of the residual profits. The corporation may issue preferred stock whose holders usually have no voice in its organization and virtually no voice in its management but do have a right generally to share prior to the common stock in earnings usually limited in amount. 

All limitations and preferences attaching to the preferred stock must be specifically provided for in the charter. Thus the common stock appeals to persons who want to take a more active part in management and who are willing to take more risk for a possibly larger income. The preferred stock appeals to those who seek greater safety of principal and are content with a lower rate of return. In fact the corporation can issue several kinds of perferred stock with varying degrees of safety and varying rates of return. 

In addition to tapping several sources of proprietary investment, 62

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

63 the corporation can issue bonds, a liability, to obtain funds from investors who wish for still greater security and who generally are willing to take a still lower rate of return. This flexibility of appeal to investors is not only an advantage to the corporation but is very beneficial from the social standpoint in making a wide range of investment securities available to investors with differing requirements.

From the viewpoint of the organizers who hold common stock, there is a very real income advantage in having sizable proportions of the senior securities draw low rates of return from the business. 

If the business as a whole can earn 10% but must pay only 5%, 6% or 7% on the senior securities, the rate of return to the common stockholder will be better than 10%. This principle is known as the principle of leverage or trading on the equity. 

Financing a Corporation--a Practical Illustration 

Assume that a group of people find a business opportunity requiring an estimated $450,000 of capital. Between them they can invest $150,000; they believe that several hundred persons in their business community would be willing to invest $300,000. Since the partnership form of organization is obviously not feasible for so large a group, the organizers decide to incorporate. Before applying for their charter, they must formulate and carefully describe their proposed plan of financing.

The organizers can, of course, capitalize the company entirely with common stock, that is they can obtain permission to sell 4,500 shares of common stock at $100 per share (or 45,000 shares at $10 per share). If they used this method of capitalization and sold all the stock, however, they would lose control of the company since their own investment of $150,000 would constitute less than onehalf of the common stock.

They might of course obtain permission to issue $150,000 in common stock, which they would buy themselves, and $150,000 each of preferred stock and of bonds for sale to outsiders. However, they know that the senior securities should not constitute too large a proportion of the entire financing because the financial structure of the company would become top-heavy and thereby fail to appeal to investors seeking safety. Probably the organizers would decide to issue $250,000 of common stock, and $100,000 each of preferred

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

stock and of bonds. Their own $150,000 block of common stock would still retain control of the corporation safely since it is over one-half of the common stock. The sale of an additional $100,000 of common stock would provide an opportunity for other investors who were willing to take some risk. At the same time the $250,000 total of common stock would reassure the prospective preferred stockholders and bondholders since the common stockholders would have furnished over one-half of the total investment. 

Whether these exact proportions would be adopted in any particular set of circumstances would call for seasoned judgment. The principle of balance would be the essential factor, however. The advantages and disavantages of the several suggested plans would have to be weighed carefully against one another. One important advantage of issuing bonds is that interest paid to the bondholders is deductible for taxes, whereas dividends paid to stockholders are not deductible.

The Rates of Return on Preferred Stocks and on Bonds 

Then the organizers should decide the terms as to rate of return and other rights to be written into the charter concerning the preferred stock and bonds. Commonly preferred stock is given an annual rate of return in the range of 5% to 7%, whereas the bonds may be expected to sell with a rate of 3% to 6%. The rates would depend on many factors-the nature of the proposed business, the supply of investment money in the area, the competing opportunities for investment, and finally on the business reputations of the organizers themselves. If the rates are set too high the securities will sell easily, but the insiders will in effect be giving away a larger share of the profits then is necessary. If the rates are set too low, the organizers will have difficulty in selling the securities and may jeopardize their whole project.

Other Provisions Concerning Preferred Stock 

There are other provisions that can be included in the charter to make the preferred stock more attractive. For instance, provision may be made for cumulative dividends on the preferred stock. Thus if in any year the corporation does not pay the preferred dividend, that dividend accumulates and must be paid before any dividend may be paid on the common stock. This pro-

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

65 vision strengthens the position of the preferred stock, especially since the common stockholders are in control of the board of directors that determines the dividends. Without such a cumulative provision the stock would be called noncumulative preferred. Noncumulative preferred stock is such a poor investment that comparatively little of it is actually sold. 

Still another way to make preferred stock attractive is to make it preferred as to assets. This phrase means that, in the event of dissolution the preferred stockholders are entitled to receive the face value of their stock before any return is made to the common stockholders. Most purchasers of stock in a new corporation certainly do not anticipate dissolution or bankruptcy, but the provision preferred as to assets shows good faith and confidence on the part of the organizers who will hold the common stock. Hence, the preferred stock becomes both more attractive and much easier to sell.

Occasionally, the preferred stock contract goes as far as to make the preferred convertible into common stock, either share for share or in some other ratio, at the option of the preferred stockholder. This provision makes the preferred attractive, but it also poses the threat of a shift in the control of the corporation since the preferred stockholders by the exercise of the conversion privilege and by joining the previous "outside" common stockholders might outnumber the organizers in terms of common shares held. 

A provision with a contrary effect is sometimes written in the contract whereby the preferred stock may be callable at the option of the corporation. Under this provision, the preferred stockholder does not know how long his status can be maintained. Callable preferred stock is less attractive, but the common stockholder retains a much stronger position. 

Many other provisions can of course be written into the charter. 

But the provisions noted are especially significant in terms of their impact on corporate accounting.

In the preceding discussion, it is assumed that all the stock authorized by the charter is subscribed for and issued as soon as the organization is completed. Often, however, the organizers contemplate future expansion of the business and obtain authorization in the charter for considerably more stock than they intend to issue immediately. It is sometimes suggested that the fact of

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

authorization should be made the basis for a formal journal entry as follows: 

Unissued Capital Stock Capital Stock Authorized 

10,000,000 10,000,000 (100,000 shares par $100 authorized by charter)

Such an entry employs debit and credit language to record an important practical and legal fact, which does not create any real asset or equity. At best these two accounts could be considered only mutual contra accounts. At worst they might cause confusion and obscure the fact that the first real assets and equities come into existence with the acceptance of subscriptions. The use of these entries is not recommended, but a memorandum should be made in the capital account to record the total amount of the authorization.

Other Provisions Concerning Bonds 

Bond contracts can likewise be made more attractive to the investors, depending on their specific provisions. Mention has been made of the rate of interest to be paid. In the case of bonds, the contractual amount of interest must be paid as specified (that is, annually, semiannually, or quarterly). Hence there is no problem parallel to the problem posed by the cumulative or noncumulative dividends on preferred stock. Unlike the preferred stock contract, which is normally perpetual, the bond contract terminates in a specified number of years, at which time the principal sum must be paid. Bonds may be made (1) callable at the option of the corporation, (2) payable serially (a) with specified portions of the issue to be paid in specified years or (b) with portions of the entire issue callable by lot in certain years, or (3) they may be convertible. 

The major difference in bonds concerns the kind of property pledged to secure their payment. Customarily, the property of the corporation is mortgaged to secure the debt. A trustee may be empowered to act for the bondholders and in the event of default (nonpayment of interest or principal) to foreclose the mortgage if necessary to obtain payment. Other bonds, known as collateral trust bonds, are secured by the deposit of stock or bond investments with a trustee. Bonds issued without such specific security

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

67 provisions, that is, on the reputation of the issuing corporation, are known as debenture bonds. 

Sale of Securities Through Investment Bankers

The sale of the stock and bonds of the newly organized corporation may be handled directly by the organizers in the case of the relatively small corporation, but in the case of larger issues when the funds of hundreds or even thousands of investors must be attracted, the sale is usually arranged through an investment banker or a syndicate of bankers. This method relieves the corporation of the bothersome details involved in advertising and selling the securities. The investment bankers are compensated by discounting the securities; they give the corporation a sum somewhat lower than the price paid by the actual investors. 

Theoretically the price paid to the corporation may be either above or below the par value (stated or face value) of the stocks, although in many states it is now illegal to issue stocks below their par values. Therefore, the corporation must issue securities that are sufficiently attractive to sell at least for par, or perhaps above par, at a premium. To avoid the difficulties and potential confusion of par, discount, and premium, corporations in most jurisdictions may issue no-par stock.

Bonds may be sold at par or above or below par value, the actual price usually being determined through competitive bidding by investment bankers who have studied the bond provisions thoroughly and who have expert knowledge about the market in which they will retail the bonds. In the determination of the price of bonds, the principal factor is the comparison of the rate of interest promised by the bonds with the going rate of interest in the market for bonds of the same maturity and the same degree of safety and desirability. If the bond rate is higher than the market rate, the bonds will sell at a premium; if lower, the bonds will sell at a discount. The size of the discount or premium will depend on the disparity between the bond rate and market rate and on the maturity of the bonds. 

Accounting for the Sale of Securities 

The accounting entries for the sale of stocks and bonds result from the application of normal debit and credit techniques to

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

the various facts previously discussed. For the illustration discussed earlier in this chapter the entries recording the sale of stocks ($100 par value) and bonds (at face value) would be: Cash 250,000

Capital Stock, Common (2500 shares) 

250,000 Cash 100,000 

Capital Stock, Preferred (1000 shares) 100,000 Cash 100,000

Bonds Outstanding 

100,000

Although the sale of stock at a discount is illegal in most states, the entries are instructive. The following entries might result when there is no restriction with respect to the price obtained on the sale of stock with par value. Cash 237,500 

Discount on Common Stock 

12,500 

Capital Stock, Common (2500 shares) 

250,000 Cash 110,000

Capital Stock, Preferred (1000 shares) 

100,000 

Premium on Preferred Stock 10,000

Cash 98,000 

Discount on Bonds 2,000 

Bonds Outstanding 

100,000

The accounts Capital Stock, Common; Capital Stock, Preferred; 

and Bonds Outstanding are obviously control accounts since many separate investors are represented in each account. If the stocks and bonds are being sold through investment bankers the detailed information which supports the control accounts must be supplied by the bankers. In large corporations, especially those whose stocks are listed on the stock exchanges, the detailed records regarding the stockholders, with the thousands of changes caused by subsequent sales of the stocks between investors, are kept not by the corporation itself but by some bank or trust company that acts as the corporation's stock transfer agent and specializes in this task. Whenever the corporation needs the detailed information regarding its stockholders (for instance the names of the stockholders and how many shares each one owns) the transfer agent furnishes a certified list. Such information is required for the payment of dividends (quarterly in the vast majority of cases), stockholders' meetings, the issuance of rights to subscribe to new issues of stocks, and so forth.

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

69 2 00 Corporations whose stock is listed on the stock exchanges must also employ independent agents to act as their registrars. The function of the registrar is to act as a check against the transfer agent in keeping the record of stock trading, and more especially to prevent an overissue of the particular stock, that is an issue beyond the amount properly authorized in the charter. By putting the responsibility for the detailed records in the hands of transfer agents and registrars, the corporation's own accountant is relieved of this detail, but the corporation's officers are not relieved of the technical responsibility for the proper keeping of those records. 

They are obligated to make certain that their agents function properly.

When the detailed records are kept by the outside agents, the control accounts for the various issues of stocks and for bonds outstanding are the only formal ledger records kept by the corporation.

The Keeping of Detailed Records by the Corporation 

When the corporation keeps its own stock records, some interesting problems arise. Sometimes the stock purchase agreement provides that the stock may be paid for in instalments. The dates when the instalment payments are due may be fixed in advance, or the payments may be subject to call at such times as the corporation requires funds for its expanding construction. The agreement may provide for the issuance of the shares when the subscription is received or when a certain percentage of the purchase price or the entire amount has been paid. The same persons may be subscribers for both common and preferred stock. Some subscribers may pay some of the instalments and then fail to make subsequent payments. Obviously, the corporation must establish a carefully controlled record of its relations with each subscriber. 

The heart of such a record is a special form of receivable. The amount which the subscriber promises to pay to the corporation becomes the original debit to the account and the successive collections are credits. The legal document behind the account is the subscription agreement signed by the subscriber and stating clearly the number of shares subscribed, the subscription prices, the downpayment, any agreement with regard to instalments, and when and in what form the actual stock certificate is to be issued.

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

The general ledger account to control these receivables is known by various names: Subscribers' Accounts Receivable, Subscriptions Receivable, Due from Subscribers to Common (or Preferred) 

Stock, and so forth. The form of the detailed record depends on the agreement and the intention with regard to instalments. 

Entries on the Corporate Books 

The following illustrative entry might be made in the books of a corporation at the effective date for the subscriptions assuming that the sale of the stock is not illegal in the corporation's home state.

Subscriptions Receivable-Common Stock Discount on Common Stock 

237,500 12,500 

Common Stock Subscribed 

250,000

Subscriptions Receivable-Preferred Stock 

110,000

Preferred Stock Subscribed 

100,000

Premium on Preferred Stock 

10,000

These entries assume the subscription for 2,500 shares of $100 par common stock at a 5% discount and 1,000 shares of preferred stock at a 10% premium. As noted earlier, the sale of stock at a discount is illegal in some states. However, it is instructive to note the nature of the entries that would be made, and therefore they are included here for that reason. Observe that the amounts shown as receivables are the actual amounts promised to be paid by the subscribers to the common and preferred stock. Subscriptions receivable are legally and in fact accounts receivable and should be thought of and shown in the statements as such, although they should, of course, be differentiated from trade accounts receivable, in the same way that other accounts receivable of a specialized nature are differentiated. The par values of the shares for which he has subscribed may be shown as an explanation in the detailed account for the subscriber, but the actual amount which he agreed to pay is the important element and should be debited to his account. The debits to the individual accounts must agree with and support the amount here shown in the control account, Subscriptions Receivable. There should be a separate control for each class of stock.

Note that these entries did not credit the actual capital stock accounts but rather the temporary accounts Common Stock Sub-

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

71 scribed and Preferred Stock Subscribed. By this technique attention is directed to the fact that the stock certificates have not been paid for in full. When the certificates are issued entries are made as follows: 

Common Stock Subscribed Capital Stock, Common Preferred Stock Subscribed Capital Stock, Preferred 

250,000 250,000 100,000 100,000

Some accountants consider this phasing of the entries an unnecessary refinement; they would credit the formal Capital Stock accounts as subscriptions are received, and they would make no further entry to record the issuance of the stock certificates. This pattern of entries parallels the legal principle that the subscriber is a stockholder even though the certificates have not been issued. 

The more roundabout method of making the entries used in the illustration emphasizes the practical point of view, the fact that a considerable pressure is exerted on the subscriber when the stock certificates are not issued until his instalment payments are completed.

The collection of cash from the subscribers is similar to the collection of any accounts receivable. Assume that a $10 downpayment was received for each share at the time the subscription was taken. The entry for this transaction should follow immediately after the entry for the subscription.

Subscriptions Receivable-Common Stock ... 

Cash 25,000 25,000

Cash 10,000

Subscriptions Receivable-Preferred Stock ... 

10,000

Call No. 1 Receivable on Common Stock .... 

50,000 50,000 50,000 50,000

Whether such detailed phasing is better accounting or whether it represents mere make-work activity on the part of the accountant is difficult to determine. In any specific case it should be apparent whether the more detailed entries aid management in controlling or understanding the situation. 

Forfeited Subscriptions 

If a subscriber who has made a downpayment and perhaps several additional payments is unable to complete his payments, several possibilities are open. He may secure financing from a friend, complete the payments, and then transfer his shares to his friend. He may arrange with the corporation to transfer the subscription to his friend's name. These possibilities present no accounting problems. However, the subscriber by law may forfeit the amounts he has paid. In this case the corporation has a special kind of a gain which should be shown clearly labeled as a gain. 

Assuming that Jones, a subscriber to 10 shares of common stock (for which he agreed to pay $950), has made a downpayment of $10 per share and has paid the first call of $20 per share, his account in simple form would appear as follows:

Subscriptions Receivable-Common Stock, Jones 

10 shares at 95 per share 950 Downpayment 

First call 100 200 Remember that Jones' subscription also is included in the Discount on Common Stock account for $50 and in the Common 

Stock Subscribed account for $1,000. The forfeiture of his subscription and the removal of the amounts applicable to him from the accounts require the making of the following entry: 

Common Stock Subscribed Discount on Common Stock 

1,000 

Subscriptions Receivable-Common Stock Surplus from Stock Forfeitures 

50 650 300

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

73 333 ​T 1 Some state laws, however, protect a forfeiting subscriber by requiring the corporation to offer the forfeited stock for resale and to reimburse the original subscriber for his payments minus the losses and costs incurred by the corporation on the resale of his shares. It might be possible to reduce his subscription to the number of shares for which he has paid and to issue this lesser number of shares to him.

These illustrations indicate that before actual accounting entries are made the accountant (1) must have a clear understanding of the particular facts of the situation and (2) must apply the basic accounting concepts and techniques to the particular facts of the situation.

Entries for No-Par Stock 

The nuisance and possibly misleading representations surrounding the discount or the premium on stock constitute a practical reason for the issuance of no-par stock. Generally speaking, no-par stock may be issued at any price. Subsequent issues may be sold at varying prices. The entry to be made for a sale has the simple effect: Cash

Capital Stock-no-par 

250,000 250,000

This entry may represent the sale of 25,000 shares of no-par stock at $10 per share, or 62,500 shares at $4 per share, or any other combination of factors. For this reason, it is considered necessary to state the number of shares involved in every transaction, every account, and every statement showing no-par stock either as a parenthetic part of the account title or as explanation accompanying the entry.

Some states permit the directors to credit only a portion of the contribution received by a corporation for its no-par shares to the Capital Stock account, the excess over this amount is credited to Paid-In Surplus (to be discussed later). The amount credited to the capital stock account under such provisions is known as the stated value of the stock. In some states a minimum stated value to be used for no-par issues is fixed by statute. These statutory requirements are fraught with potential disadvantages and misunderstandings similar to those involved in par stocks. It should be clear that, even if part of the original contribution of the

CORPORATIONS-ORGANIZATION 

stockholder may be legally credited to a surplus account, such amount should be segregated from the retained income account. 

When no-par stock is preferred as to dividends, it is necessary to state the dividend preference in terms of dollars and cents per share since there is no value available to use as a basis for a percentage description of the dividend. Similarly, if the no-par preferred stock is to be preferred as to assets in event of dissolution, that preference will have to be stated in terms of dollars. For instance, a corporation might be authorized in its charter to issue a preferred stock paying a dividend of 70¢ per annum. If this stock is sold for $10 per share, the investor would receive, in effect, a 7% rate of return. If later, when the corporation is better established, subsequent sales of the same no-par stock are made for $11.67 per share, the new buyers would receive 6%; if it sells for $14 per share, the rate of return would be 5%, and so forth. 

The provisions concerning preference as to assets in the event of liquidation would probably be fixed at $10 per share, the original issue price. Investors who later pay higher prices for the stock would receive only the same $10 preference in event of liquidation. Since the several issues will be mingled when the stock is traded, it would be impossible to have the asset preference other than such a single amount. It should be remembered that this preference merely places claims of the preferred stockholders ahead of those of the common stockholders; all creditors' claims have to be fully settled before even the preferred stockholders receive any payment in liquidation. 

Current Record of Stock Ownership 

The kind of current records kept for stock ownership depends largely on the number of shares outstanding and on the expected volume of sales to be recorded. We have already noted that the record of stocks listed on the exchanges, where the volume of trading may run into hundreds of transactions per day, are kept by the stock transfer agents and the registrars. These companies usually mechanize their routines by using tabulating techniques. 

At the other extreme we have the very small corporation that expects its ten or twenty stockholders to remain with it. For this reason, an adequate record could be kept on the stubs of the stock certificate book, as illustrated in Figure 12. Note that the stub

CHAPTER V  

IT IS VERY important that the banker and the businessman understand clearly the accountant's concept of surplus and all its ramifications. Many significant decisions which the businessman must make concerning dividends, financing, expansion, price level changes, and so forth are all based on an appreciation of the significance of the representations which the accountant makes regarding surplus.

Unfortunately the term surplus is much misunderstood partly because the word is used also in other fields and therefore it enters the accounting field with a variety of preconceptions attached to it. Further misunderstanding is caused by the fact that the concept of surplus is quite complicated and cannot be defined adequately in a single sentence. Various single sentence definitions which can be found in accounting tracts are not actually incorrect. 

They define the genus, surplus, properly enough, but the fact is that the important problem is the clear understanding of the various species of surplus, for that is where the confusion and the practical problems lie. Therefore the aim of this chapter will be not so much an attempt to define, but rather to set up a series of observations of the various aspects of surplus against the shifting business scene, so that the student may build his concept of surplus with a realistic appreciation of its complexity. 

GENERAL CONCEPT OF SURPLUS 

An interesting sidelight on the problem of surplus is the decision of the American Institute of Accountants; they have substituted the term retained earnings for the term surplus. This decision is recognition of the fact that the term surplus is misconstrued to such an extent that it had best be abandoned. The new term retained earnings describes clearly the principal origin of the "surplus,” and at the same time does not prejudice the pos- 81

sibility of recognizing restrictive conditions which must later be applied to its subdivisions. The new term is gaining rapid acceptance among the leaders in large industries, as shown by their published reports. For the next five or ten years, however, most businesses will still use the term surplus with its specially modified forms. Therefore, the student should be familiar with both terminologies. 

Capital and surplus are subject to exacting definitions in state statutes that prescribe the rights and duties of boards of directors and of officers with respect to dividends and other corporate financial decisions. Since the corporations are created under state statutes, in many practical situations the legal definitions are the controlling ones. Accounting concepts in general must be reconciled with the legal requirements even though the backgrounds and viewpoints of those who wrote the legal language of the statutes are sometimes quite different from the viewpoint of the accountant.

Legal Requirements for Capital Maintenance 

The basic legal requirement is that the corporation distinguish between its legal capital and its surplus. The value attributed to the shares of capital stock represents the amount of legal capital of the corporation, and it is the measure of the amount of corporate assets that by law must be maintained and must not become impaired, except as impairment results from operating or other losses. If the net assets of a corporation, that is, total assets minus total liabilities, are no greater than the legal capital, then in most states it is illegal for the corporation to pay any of the assets to its stockholders either in exchange for their stock or in the form of dividends, because to do so would impair the capital. It is primarily as a protection to the creditors that the law requires that the capital of a corporation be maintained. 

This rule seems reasonably clear and sensible, but its practical implementation is often far from simple. For instance, under certain circumstances, some states permit the payment of dividends from the current year's net income even though the capital may be impaired as a result of losses in prior years. Does such a payment constitute a violation of the basic purpose of the law? Would it make any difference in our judgment whether we were referring

to dividends on common stock, on cumulative preferred stock, or on noncumulative preferred?

Fortunately, the purpose of the legal provisions regarding the maintenance of capital can be accepted fully by businessmen and accountants. In fact most businessmen and accountants tend to be more conservative than the law requires in implementing the basic philosophy of capital maintenance. In a period of general price increases, for instance, a businessman might easily maintain the dollar amount of the original capital by the observance of the letter of the law, but since the dollars buy less, corporate capital might still be impaired in terms of physical units or in terms of purchasing power. Accountants are trying to reformulate the accepted rules of accounting in order to attain real capital maintenance in spite of inflation. Boards of directors generally are aware of the problem and restrict their dividend payments far more tightly than the law requires. 

Source and Status of Surplus 

To know how to handle any particular element of surplus, the accountant must realize that two sets of factors must be considered. 

The first factors are those surrounding the origin of the surplus item. When we talk about earned surplus, surplus net profits, paidin surplus, donated surplus, surplus from appreciation of land, and so forth we are concerned with the origin of the particular item of surplus.

The second factors are those surrounding the present status of the surplus item. When we talk about surplus reserves, available surplus, free surplus, capital surplus, and so forth we are concerned with the present status of the item, especially with its status as to possible use for cash dividends. The two sets of factors are not unrelated, however. Sometimes the nature of the origin of the surplus, as for instance, paid-in surplus, in effect determines our judgment as to the propriety of its use. In other cases, the condition of its origin, for example surplus net profits, establishes the initial presumption of the availability for dividends. Subsequently however, the action of setting aside reserves negates this initial presumption. The mere fact that a profit or surplus credit is realized in the form of cash does not necessarily cause it to be available for dividends. For instance if a section of plant site land

is sold for cash at a profit but is to be replaced by a new site, the purchase price of which is equally costly, the transaction does not yield a surplus that is available for dividends in any prudent sense.

In contrast, surplus from appreciation of land that initially was not available for dividends, actually may become available for dividends when the land is sold for its appreciated value. 

Both origin and present status factors must be carefully considered in appraising any surplus situation, as is emphasized by these illustrations.

Problems like these which are involved in the capital-surplusreserves-dividends situation are highly individual. No set rules, simple or complex, can control all situations adequately. The accountant, the businessman, and the banker should understand the basic concepts and relationships. They must be equipped to trace any proposed course of action to its full consequences, and exercise a high degree of informed judgment on every problem that arises.

The Significance of Surplus 

The best approach to the problem in this chapter is to study the origin and significance of surplus under relatively simple and "clean" assumptions in order to be sure of our basic concepts, and then later to add the special conditions and circumstances which cause the real difficulties.

Perhaps there actually has never been such a perfectly simple set of conditions, but for purposes of discussion we may visualize a trading or manufacturing and selling business that is operating in a period of stable prices and which is neither expanding nor contracting. In these circumstances capital would be maintained in both the legal and practical sense if the annual net income were paid out in full as a cash dividend. If normal accounting procedures are followed, if there is no expansion of plant, and if all assets, such as receivables and inventories, and all liabilities are kept at the same amounts, then there would be enough new cash at the end of the year to permit the payment of the cash dividend to the approximate amount of net income without impairing the capital and even without changes in the working capital. This amounts to saying that in these very simple circumstances the

year's transactions leading to net income could be summarized into the very simple journal entry:

Cash 

Surplus 

58,695 58,695 Under similar perfectly simple conditions, the year's operations for the single proprietorship or partnership could be summarized by an entry showing the credit to capital or drawing accounts instead of to surplus. This consideration reveals the true nature of earned surplus. Surplus is capital. Surplus is that part of capital that has been created by operations. At any one time surplus is that part of the total capital that is in excess of the capital stock. 

The declaration of an annual dividend would be journalized as follows: 

Surplus Dividend Declared 

58,695 58,695 Obviously, the payment of the dividend would complete the cycle of entries and leave all other accounts untouched: 

Dividend Declared 

Cash 58,695 58,695 The ramifications of the declaration and payment are presented in greater detail later in this chapter. 

Accumulated Surplus 

Most corporations even though operating under fairly stable price conditions and though not formally planning to expand, actually do not pay the full income in dividends each year. Companies commonly point with pride to their unbroken dividend records. They conservatively hold part of the income as surplus in order to be able to keep the dividends level if the operations of following years should happen to be less profitable. The mere fact that it is customary to pay dividends in even amounts or percentages usually results in keeping a balance in the surplus account.

If the company of our simplified illustration had capital stock of $500,000, the actual dividend paid would almost certainly have been an even 10% (or $50,000). Thus, $8,695 would have remained in the surplus account. 

The failure to pay out every penny of net profit in dividends

is our first departure from the assumptions of complete simplicity. 

At this point the corporation is likely to move unwittingly into a form of expansion. The use of surplus for dividends requires specific action by the board of directors, but there is no restriction on the use of any available cash. There is a strong tendency to use the extra cash for businesslike purposes, such as increasing the inventory of merchandise, stocking supplies more generously, or paying current liabilities more promptly. These uses of the cash, however, are all aspects of expansion. Once the cash has been used in these ways and the business management has become accustomed to the new status, the cash is more or less permanently tied up. Legally, the surplus may still be used for cash dividends, but practically speaking it is no longer actually available since the cash, originally in excess, has now been used for other purposes. 

If in the next year the net income should fall $8,000 short of the hoped for 10% (or $50,000) the summary entry would be: Cash

Surplus 

42,000 42,000

The surplus account would now have a balance of $50,695. The directors legally may declare a $50,000 cash dividend. To pay out $50,000, however, would require draining cash from the increased inventories of merchandise and supplies and increasing the current liabilities to their original amount. Although these steps could be taken, it is much more likely that the board of directors would prefer to cut the dividend to 8% (or $40,000), to be on the conservative side. Thus another $2,000 would be added to the balance of the surplus account and probably would result in a further informal expansion of operations such as that previously described.

The fact is that such automatic expansion with the accumulation of surplus is customary. Gradual expansion is far more common than the condition of no expansion which was assumed in our original simple visualization. The declaration of dividends that are equal to net income is extremely rare in actual business. 

Under the widely accepted notion that "if you do not move forward you go back," or, "you cannot stand still in business," the unintentional expansion merges over into an aggressive and planned expansion.

When management is clearly aware of what happens in the cash-

surplus-dividends-expansion relationships, it refers to the situation as plowing the profits back into the business. Many present-day corporate statements of condition show surplus so much larger than cash there can be no possibility of paying cash dividends to the full extent of the surplus. The managements of these corporations realize that the surplus consists of two parts: one part, recent profits still backed by available cash; and a second far greater part, the accumulation of previous profits that have been thoroughly plowed back into plant equipment, inventories, and receivables. 

SURPLUS RESERVES 

Expansion also may be financed by the sale of additional stocks or bonds. Such transactions and the entries for them do not concern us in this discussion. At present we are pursuing the study of expansion out of earnings.

The Formal Expansion Program 

Some boards of directors explicitly plan their company expansion out of earnings. They want to see their intentions recognized in the accounts. For instance, they may pass a resolution to set aside $20,000 per year out of earnings in order to finance the construction of a $100,000 addition to the plant five years hence. 

To give effect to this resolution the accountant makes the following annual entry:

Earned Surplus (Retained Income) Reserve for Plant Expansion 

20,000 20,000

This entry does not actually change the basic nature of the surplus. It does earmark the surplus as no longer being available for dividends but segregated for the specific purpose named. Although this entry earmarks the surplus, it does not prevent the use of the cash for any legitimate purpose within the business. If the $20,000 of new cash annually available is devoted to increasing receivables and inventory and decreasing current liabilities, the corporation may still find itself without ready cash to pay for the new plant, when the time comes, although the financial situation should be so improved that no real difficulty would be encountered in the attempt to finance by borrowing.

Funding the Expansion Program 

Rather than obtaining the necessary funds by borrowing or by issuing stocks the board can supplement its order establishing the $20,000 annual reserve by requiring the treasurer to invest $20,000 each year in high grade securities that also are earmarked for the expansion program. The entry for this transaction would be: 

Expansion Fund Securities Owned 

Cash 20,000 20,000

With the creation of such a special fund, the reserve becomes what is commonly known as a funded reserve. It is especially important to recognize the fact that this device still leaves the Reserve for Expansion an integral part of Surplus and that it should be shown as such in the statement of condition. 

Reserve for Retirement of Bonds 

A corporation having an issue of bonds outstanding may adopt a systematic savings plan directed toward the payment of the debt when it falls due. The process is similar to the system used in formalizing the expansion plan. If the purpose is to pay a $100,000 bond issue at the end of ten years, the directors will order the earmarking of the requisite amount of surplus annually by the following entry:

Surplus Reserve for Retirement of Bonds 

10,000 10,000

This entry has the significant effect of decreasing the amount of surplus that might otherwise be distributed as dividends. The building of the reserve formally expresses the directors' renunciation of the temptation to pay dividends. The same effect could be obtained simply by not declaring dividends and by allowing the surplus to accumulate in the Surplus account itself. However corporate saving is made easier when formalized in a special reserve even as individual saving is made easier when formalized through the bond-a-month plan. 

The Reserve for Retirement of Bonds may be backed up by the setting aside of specific securities, that is, by "funding the reserve," if the directors care to do so. Such specific securities, earmarked as part of a debt retirement plan, are often characterized as a

sinking fund, though the term may not seem particularly descriptive. The annual entry would be: 

Sinking Fund Investments 

Cash 10,000 10,000

Sometimes the reserve when thus reinforced by a fund is termed the Sinking Fund Reserve. The title previously used, Reserve for 

Retirement of Bonds, is preferable, however, since the use of the words sinking fund on both sides of the statement of condition is confusing. 

In some cases the bond indenture requires the corporation to establish a reserve for retirement of bonds. Sometimes the indenture also requires the funding of the reserve. Such contract provisions must be recognized as binding on the corporation. They constitute a second kind of legal restriction on dividends, that is, in addition to statutory restrictions. 

Reserve for Contingencies 

Occasionally corporate directors when faced with a possible loss, such as a suit for damages or a war risk to foreign investments, may think that a conservative dividend policy is advisable to keep the corporation in a position which is strong enough to meet the contingency. Of course the directors either could refuse to pay dividends or could pay only small dividends. If, however, they wish to formalize their conservative judgment they may order the earmarking of a certain portion of the surplus by an entry similar to the following:

Surplus Reserve for Contingent Loss on Damage Suit 

50,000 50,000 If the contingency is one that grows greater, successive entries may be made so that the reserve may, to the extent feasible, measure the risk of loss. It is difficult to measure such risks, however, and the directors merely select a rather conservative figure. 

Sometimes the contingency itself is rather indefinite, and the reserve may have a general title such as Reserve for Contingencies. 

A vague title invites suspicion; perhaps the directors are trying to hide surplus in order to keep the stockholders from clamoring for more dividends.

When a specific loss occurs, it should be charged to an account

with a properly descriptive title and closed into Expense and Revenue at the end of the fiscal period. At the same time the reserve for the contingency, if no longer needed, should be closed into Surplus. By following this technique, the accountant charges the loss against income in the period of its actual determination. 

This procedure follows the best accounting practice as well as the income tax deduction rule, and still secures the benefit of most conservative guidance of dividend policy.

To illustrate: In July 19X5 the company is sued for $150,000 on a claim for breech of warranty on its product. An entry is made at that time:

Surplus 

Reserve for Contingent Loss in Breach of

Warranty Suit 

150,000 150,000 

While the suit is in process the December 31, 19X5 and 19X6 statements of condition would show the reserve in the proprietary section. In March 19X7 the suit is concluded and $115,000 damages are assessed against the company by the court. The entry would be:

Loss in Breach of Warranty Suit 

Cash 115,000 115,000 

Reserve for Contingent Loss in Breach of

Warranty Suit 

150,000

Surplus 

150,000

Assume that the books are closed annually on a calendar year basis. On December 31, 19X7 the loss account would be closed to 

Expense and Revenue by the following entries: 

Expense and Revenue Loss in Breach of Warranty Suit 

115,000 115,000

The $115,000 loss would appear in the income statement and the tax return for 19X7. The dividends would have been restricted by $150,000 in 19X5, with a net restoration to surplus available for dividends of $35,000 in 19X7.

Reserve for Self-Insurance 

A special kind of contingency reserve is the reserve for selfinsurance which some large companies create rather than buy insurance for such hazards as fire and tornado from commercial

insurance companies. Whereas the small company with one or two plants could not take such a risk, a large company with widely scattered properties may justifiably take advantage of the laws of probability. An entry is made annually for approximately the amount which a commercial insurance company would charge for the hazards to be covered in the form: 

Surplus Reserve for Self-Insurance 

375,000 375,000

Whenever a loss does occur it should be charged to a properly named loss account, which would be closed to Expense and Revenue at the end of the accounting period. At the same time an equal amount of the reserve should be closed into the Surplus account as follows: 

Loss by Fire 

Plant (or other asset)

Reserve for Self-Insurance Surplus 

200,000 200,000 200,000 200,000

The reasoning behind this pattern of entries is similar to the reasons previously outlined for the contingency reserve for loss on a damage suit.

It should be noted also that until the loss occurs any balance in the Reserve for Self-Insurance is surplus and should be shown in the proprietary section of the statement of condition. Over the years the reserve should not grow excessively since its basic purpose is to spread the cost of the hazard against dividend payments equitably among the years. If the reserve grows much beyond a reasonably conservative figure, it is evident that the annual credits for self-insurance are too great and that a surplus that can be made available for dividends is being hidden in the reserve.

Summary: True Surplus Reserves 

The reserves discussed in this section are all illustrations of true surplus reserves. In each case, surplus which had originated in earnings was segregated to keep it from being used for dividends. Legally, each surplus could have been used initially for dividends. As far as the law is concerned these reserves could be closed into surplus and used for dividends whenever the directors

92 

so desired. However good business practice recognizes other determinants than the law and would not justify such dividends. This discussion is simply a part of the whole surplus problem. Other ramifications of the surplus problem are presented in following sections. 

PAID-IN SURPLUS In Chapter IV, Corporations-Organization, an entry for the sale of par value stock above par credited the excess to Premium on Capital Stock. We may now study the concept of paid-in surplus more critically in relation to the entire problem of corporate surplus.

Paid-In Surplus and Stock Issues 

By the legal definition of capital in most states, the premium is in excess of capital in the legal sense, and therefore it is legally surplus and may be used for dividends. Regarded from the standpoints of business and accounting, a premium on stock certainly is not earned income. If the corporation were to use the premium on stock for dividends, it would be returning to the stockholders cash that they themselves had paid in. The best accounting thinking is that premium should not be considered as surplus available for dividends (in spite of its legal availability), but should be kept permanently on the books, clearly identified as premium on capital stock.

If we were to ask whether premium is surplus, the answer would be, "Yes." But premium is a surplus that should be distinguished from a surplus arising from net income. Premium is one kind of paid-in surplus, and like other kinds of paid-in surplus it must not be used for dividends.

The excess of paid-in capital over the stated value of no-par stock is related closely to premium surplus. Regardless of the legal status of such paid-in surplus, it should be distinguished from surplus arising from net income. The American Institute of Accountants suggests that the descriptive phrase "Capital Contributed in Excess of Par or Stated Value" be used to replace the traditional terminology of Paid-In Surplus or Premium-Capital Stock or similar wording.

Surplus from Assessments on Stockholders 

Another variety of paid-in surplus originates in assessments on stockholders. If the corporation finds itself seriously in need of funds the stockholders can, by special action, assess themselves for contributions of additional cash in proportion to their stockholdings. Such surplus should not be used for dividends, and the credit to Paid-In Surplus (or to Capital Contributed in Excess of Par or Stated Value) expresses this conviction as indicated by the entry: Cash

Paid-In Surplus 

100,000 100,000

OTHER TYPES OF SURPLUS 

Related to paid-in surplus but different enough to require further careful analysis are the varieties of surplus discussed in the following paragraphs.

Surplus from Appreciation of Fixed Assets 

The interplay of economic forces often causes the value of fixed assets such as land, buildings, patents, and leaseholds to rise considerably. To recognize such changed values in the accounts is usually unnecessary and serves no immediate purpose. Accountants prefer to leave such matters alone and ignore them. Under certain circumstances, however, it becomes necessary to enter such increased values on the books, and this recognition provides another type of surplus.

For instance, a corporation owns a piece of land that was purchased years ago for $10,000. This land is now conservatively valued at $150,000. The corporation issues $100,000 of bonds with a mortgage on this land as the security. Without the recognition of the increased value of the land, the statement of condition would show disproportionate liabilities. This situation can be remedied by showing the appreciation in the value of the land by the following entry:

Land 

Surplus from Appreciation of Land 

140,000 140,000

Proprietorship has increased. Certainly by basic definition, the credit element is a kind of surplus. Yet, this surplus is not available for dividends. It belongs to the general category of capital surplus, but it is quite different from paid-in capital surplus. The use of a fully descriptive title such as the title used in the accompanying journal entry is the only way of handling the situation adequately and honestly.

Surplus from Donated Assets 

If a municipality, in its campaign to attract industry, presents a corporation with a piece of land or a plant as an inducement for moving to the town, we have a typical donated asset situation. 

The corporation appraises the asset value conservatively and makes an entry similar to the following:

Land (or Plant) Surplus from Donated Assets 

200,000 200,000

Assuming that the gift has no strings attached, it certainly represents another type of surplus. Legally, this surplus probably would be judged as available for dividends, but practically, it should not be so considered because of the nature of the asset which backs it up. Generally, it is undesirable to pay a dividend from donated surplus even if cash is available. 

The asset donated, however, is rarely free and clear. More often than not such donations do have conditions attached, which may alter the situation materially. For instance, land donated by a municipality to a manufacturing corporation may be subject to the stipulation that the title does not actually pass until the corporation shall have constructed a half-million dollar plant on the land or until the corporation shall have employed at least a hundred people for five years. Such conditions may be only matters of form if fulfilment is a virtual certainty, and management may wish to ignore them in the making of the accounting entries. 

Technically, the entries should be set up with descriptive account titles fully disclosing the contingent nature of the items, for example: Land-Contingently Owned Under Donation Plan or Contingent Surplus from Donated Assets. When the contingencies have ceased these account titles would be changed by journal entries to the permanent titles, such as Land and Surplus from 

Donated Assets.

Surplus from Reorganization 

Reorganization is a very broad term. Its meaning ranges from a very profitable consolidation to a recapitalization scaling down proprietary equities to wipe out the deficit of a nearly defunct corporation. Often it is necessary to credit some surplus account in the process. Examination of the circumstances ordinarily indicates that such a surplus credit would not be available for dividends. Here is another type of capital surplus. Its identifying title might be Surplus from Reorganization, unless there are other pertinent factors that should be recognized specifically in the title. 

A common type of reorganization situation is graphically illustrated in Figure 14. The Jones Corporation, after some difficult years, faces the following statement of condition:

JONES CORPORATION Statement of Condition 

December 31, 19X8 

ASSETS EQUITIES Current Assets 

$170,000 Liabilities $250,000

FIGURE 14. STATEMENT OF CONDITION OF THE CORPORATION PRIOR TO REORGANIZATION 

The preferred stock is 6% cumulative and has three years' dividends in arrears ($36,000). A joint committee of preferred and common stockholders have worked out a reorganization plan subject to the approval of all the stockholders as follows: Fixed Assets are to be written down $50,000. The preferred stockholders renounce their claims to cumulative dividends in arrears and turn in their $200,000 par of preferred stock for $150,000 par of common stock. The original common stockholders donate threequarters of their stock to the corporation for cancelation.

The journal entries necessary to effect this reorganization would 

Surplus Fixed Assets Capital Stock, Preferred Capital Stock, Common Surplus Capital Stock, Common Surplus Surplus from Reorganization 

50,000 50,000 200,000 150,000 50,000 75,000 30,000 45,000

In effect these transactions and entries would accomplish three purposes (1) reduce the overvalued fixed assets to a reasonable figure, (2) correct a badly unbalanced capital structure, and (3) wipe out the accumulated debit balance in the surplus account. 

Note that the third entry might have been made with a credit of $75,000 to the regular Surplus account. This would have left a $45,000 balance therein resulting from the sacrifices of the stockholders. The possible subsequent confusion of this balance with credits to surplus for operating income is nicely averted by earmarking the $45,000 under its own special title; the new statement of condition would be:

JONES CORPORATION Statement of Condition 

December 31, 19X8

ASSETS EQUITIES Current Assets 

$170,000

Liabilities 

$250,000

Fixed Assets 

300,000
