$470,000

Total Equities 

$470,000

FIGURE 15. STATEMENT OF CONDITION OF THE CORPORATION AFTER PROPOSED REORGANIZATION 

Note that the $45,000 credit is actually surplus, as may be seen by returning to fundamentals. The assets now correctly valued at $470,000 exceed the liabilities of $250,000 by $220,000. This amount, $220,000, is proprietorship. The formal capitalization is

now $175,000 of common stock and the difference is surplus by basic definition. In actual practice for the purpose of full disclosure, footnotes to the statement of condition would explain the surplus item.

THE CLEAN SURPLUS PROBLEM 

In this section we are concerned with the Surplus account (or the Retained Earnings account). This is the account which is credited with the period's net income. It is the account from which the dividends are usually declared and from which the surplus reserves are taken. 

In addition to these normal uses the Surplus account has in the past also been debited and credited for losses and gains that are apart from the normal business operations and for adjustments in proprietorship resulting from previous years' errors in the determination of income. This use of the Surplus account is aimed to make the net income as determined in the Expense and Revenue account measure the results of each period's operations as such. This use of the Surplus account is understood as the current performance concept. Under this thinking a loss by fire or embezzlement or the loss of an overseas branch of the company through war was deemed not a proper charge against the efficiency of the operation of one particular period. Likewise the profit on the sale of a piece of land that had been held for years, or the receipt of compensation through the winning of long-fought patent infringement suit was considered not properly creditable to the efficiency of one period. Generally, according to past reasoning, the best way to keep such items from obscuring the measurement of the particular period's success was to dump the item into Surplus. Adjustments for depreciation allowances and for revaluations of inventory often represent the recognition of accumulated errors from previous periods. In the past these errors have been run directly into the Surplus account in order to avoid distortion of the results of current operations as shown in Expense and 

Revenue. 

One of the primary functions of a good accounting system is to present a clear picture of each period's operations to management. 

The use of the Surplus account for extraneous items accomplishes

this management objective; therefore, the practice of using the surplus account for this purpose will persist for a long time. 

However, the practice of dumping such odd and end transactions into the Surplus account has led to abuses. In some instances it became a way of hiding unfavorable facts from the stockholders. 

Although the stockholders might study the income statement carefully, they were rarely even interested in the supposedly technical adjustments in the Surplus account. With this in mind many present-day accountants espouse the clean surplus concept which insists that all losses and gains must be run through the Expense and Revenue account and that they not be entered directly into Surplus.

Does the use of the clean surplus concept destroy the possibility of presenting a clear picture of each period's operations? The answer is "No." In the process of closing the accounts and, even more importantly, in drawing up the income statement an accountant should be certain to develop and identify the net income from operations before adding and subtracting extraneous items. 

If these representations are handled properly, management will be served as well by the clean surplus technique as by the older method (the current performance technique), and a potentially serious source of misunderstanding will be removed. 

Undoubtedly the accountant for some years in the future will continue to encounter the use of the Surplus account for extraneous items so he must be prepared by a full understanding of the shift in thinking to cope with either practice. 

DIVIDENDS 

A dividend is a distribution to stockholders of cash or other assets reducing the available surplus element of proprietorship. 

Dividends usually are paid in cash. In rare instances the dividends are paid in other assets, such as shares of stock in other companies bonds, subdivision lots, or cases of whisky.

A scrip dividend is a dividend initially paid in scrip (or promissory notes) and finally paid in cash at the due date of the scrip. 

While the scrip is outstanding, it must be recognized as a liability closely similar to a commercial note payable.

A stock dividend is another type of dividend. It is discussed later in this section. 

Declaration and Payment of Dividends 

The decision to pay a dividend is the prerogative of the board of directors. Most dividends are paid quarterly, although semiannual and annual dividends are not uncommon. If the stock of a corporation is active, the timing of the declaration and payment of dividends should be handled in such a way that persons who are in the midst of buying and selling the stock suffer no injustice. 

For instance, the declaration of dividends may be made at the directors' meeting on January 10 as payable on February 15 to stockholders of record at the close of business January 26. Buyers of the stock between January 10 and about January 23 may know of the coming dividend and have sufficient time to become the recorded owners of the shares by the close of business January 26. 

The rules of different exchanges vary in details but in every case there is a specific critical date, generally, three or four days before the date of record. To continue the illustration: On January 23, 24, 25, and 26 the buyers no longer can become registered in time to reap the dividend. No harm is done, however, as long as both buyers and sellers understand the situation. On the morning of 

January 23 the price of the stock should drop by the amount of the anticipated dividend. The stock is now selling ex-dividend, which means that the old stockholders receive the dividend and that the buyer is aware that he is buying ex-dividend (or without the dividend). When the stock is quoted ex-dividend, the stock price quotations are identified with the symbol xd.

The entries for a $50,000 dividend for the given dates would be: 

January 10 Surplus 50,000 

Dividend Declared 50,000 

February 15 

Dividend Declared

Cash 50,000 50,000 The terminology is not rigid. The title used for the dividend liability may be Dividend Payable or even Dividend Declared Payable.

The action of the directors in declaring a dividend converts

a portion of the surplus into a liability. The corporation is thereafter obligated to make the payment; any stockholder could sue for the payment if the corporation failed to meet this obligation. 

If the corporation has several kinds of stock outstanding the dividend must be fully identified by particular stock issue as for instance, Dividend Declared-First Preferred Stock, Dividend De clared-Common Stock, and so forth. No entry need be made on the corporate books for the passing of the date as of which dividends are payable, although the person who is responsible for the detailed records should make a notation of the date carefully when he lists the stockholdings on which the dividend checks are to be figured.

The entries for dividends other than cash would be subject to the same careful arrangements concerning the date of payment and would parallel those for cash dividends. 

When the corporation has preferred stock outstanding, the declaration of dividends must respect the fine print in the preferred stock contract and the status of the preferred stock with respect to dividends in arrears, if any. 

The rights with respect to arrearages are quite simple. As a rule, the cumulative preferred stockholder must have received all the dividends to which he is entitled before the common stockholder receives any dividends. In years of tight cash it is proper to pay partial dividends on the preferred stock. Thus in subsequent years the preferred stock may have whole dividends plus partial years' dividend arrearages. Such cumulative dividends in arrears are not a liability to the corporation. They are not subject to recognition in book entries but should be mentioned in footnotes to statements of condition. If the preferred stock is noncumulative the situation is simpler from the accounting standpoint, but more delicate as a policy problem in a year when cash is tight and when the directors are considering the passing (nonpayment) of a preferred dividend.

Although it is not very common, preferred stockholders may have the right to participate in dividends beyond the basic stated rate for their stock. Such participation attracts investors. The investor and his accountant should read the provisions of participation very closely. Usually the common stock must receive a basic percentage after the preferred has received its regular divi-

dend before the participation goes into effect. The participation may be at one rate for the preferred stock and at a different rate for the common stock. If either or both of the stocks are no-par, the participating provisions must be stated in terms of dollars and cents per share rather than in percentages. 

For example, we have the "X" Corporation whose capitalization is $100,000 ($100 par) preferred stock and $100,000 (10,000 shares of no-par) common stock. The dividend contract states: "In each year the preferred stock shall receive a basic dividend of 6% (cumulative as to years in which dividends are not paid) and shall participate in equal dividends per share with the common stock after the common stock shall have received a basic dividend of $6 per share." Note that the participating feature does not go into effect until after the basic $6,000 dividend to the preferred, and after the common shall have received a dividend of $60,000 or 60%. Although a preferred stock technically may have participating features, in actual practice it would seldom benefit from its participating features unless the business were unusually profitable.

Stock Dividends 

The word dividend, as used in the expression stock dividend, refers to a basically different situation. Although the phrase "paying a stock dividend," is commonly used, the accurate terminology is "issuing a stock dividend," in order to contrast this form of payment with the payment of a cash dividend. In recording the issue of a stock dividend the corporation debits surplus and credits capital stock. The stockholders received additional shares of capital stock. The issuing of a stock dividend is best understood as the final step in the process of plowing profits back into the business. The issuance of a stock dividend out of accumulated surplus is final recognition of the permanency of that part of the surplus. The entry for the stock dividend is closely comparable to the partnership entry for closing amounts from the drawing account into the capital account to express the intention of leaving such amounts permanently in the business. 

It is instructive to visualize the stock dividend from the investor's viewpoint. The accumulation of net profits in the surplus account is one determinant of the market price of the stock. When

a corporation has accumulated surplus equal to its original capital stock the market price of its stock becomes two or three times its original issue price. At such high prices the market tends to become sluggish. Under these conditions the issuance of a 100% stock dividend which doubles the number of shares in the hands of stockholders should reduce the market price by 50%. However the publicity attending the issuance of the stock dividend plus the greater activity of the lower-priced shares frequently result in an increase in market price, which makes everybody happy. 

To the stockholder who expects to keep his total holdings and who therefore is not particularly interested in market prices, the 100% stock dividend may be compared to the process of changing his $20 bill into two 10s. Occasionally it is handier to have two tens than one twenty, and the stockholder who wishes to realize some cash on his investment may find that the stock dividend is some slight advantage in the selling of a few shares. 

The stock dividend is not limited to 100%. If circumstances indicate the advisability thereof, the corporation may issue stock dividends of any percentage above or below 100%. The two statements of condition and journal entries in Figure 16 demonstrate a 200% stock dividend issued by a corporation which has been expanding its business out of earnings.

COOPER CORPORATION Statement of Condition 

(before stock dividend)

ASSETS EQUITIES

$380,000

200,000

To record the issuance of 2,000 shares of stock for $100 par

FIGURE 16B. STATEMENT OF CONDITION FOR A CORPORATION AFTER ISSUING A 200% STOCK DIVIDEND 

The statement of condition before stock dividend shows the typical large surplus that resulted from expansion out of earnings without the formal recognition of the fact in a Reserve for Expansion. If such a reserve had been formalized the situation would have been as suitable for issuing a stock dividend as the situation shown in Figures 16A and 16B. The entry for the declaration of the stock dividend would then have debited the Reserve for Expansion instead of the Surplus account; or the reserve might have been closed into an account called Capital Surplus and the declaration made from that account. 

A stock dividend also may be declared from the Reserve for 

Retirement of Bonds, Surplus from Appreciation of Land, or 

Surplus from Reorganization. Any one of these surpluses and surplus reserves or a combination of several of them may be used for the declaration of a stock dividend. Since these surpluses typically are not available for cash dividends, the directors clarify the situation by their action in declaring the stock dividend, thus eliminating the various surpluses and combining them with the permanent capital.

If a corporation has preferred stock outstanding and wishes to pay a stock dividend on the common stock, the directors should make certain that the equities of the preferred stockholders are respected properly. If the preferred stock is nonparticipating and has received all the dividends to which it is entitled to date, the entire surplus or any portion thereof may be issued to the common stockholders as a stock dividend. If the preferred stock has participation rights, however, it would have rights in a stock dividend

similar to those that it has in a cash dividend. This consideration might render a stock dividend impracticable. 

The illustration of a 200% stock dividend in Figures 16A and 16B is typical. The stock dividend amounts to two new shares for each old share held. Stock dividends are not always in terms of round amounts. A stock dividend of 50%, 25%, or 7% is possible, in spite of the fact that the number of shares to be issued to some stockholders will be fractional. In these cases the number of whole shares payable to each stockholder are issued as regular shares. 

The excess to which each stockholder is entitled is given to him in the form of fractional share "warrants," which develop a market price of their own and are marketable. Interested buyers are able to collect many small fractional warrants and present them to the corporation in exchange for whole shares. Sometimes however, most of the stock is held in large blocks, and the fractional amounts are small, in which case the corporation may pay these amounts in cash instead of issuing fractional share warrants. 

Some corporations have the routine practice of issuing small percentage stock dividends in addition to cash dividends. For instance, a corporation may pay a 2% quarterly cash dividend plus a 5% extra stock dividend at the end of the year. These corporations pay the resulting fractional amounts in cash or take steps to facilitate the sale of the fractional warrants on behalf of their stockholders.

STOCK SPLITS 

A stock split is the process by which the corporation in effect calls in all the old shares and issues a greater number of new shares to replace the old shares. The splits resemble stock dividends in two ways: (1) they increase the number of shares outstanding with a consequent reduction of the market price per share and (2) they do not change the stockholders' proportions of ownership and control, except for the fractional shares. Unlike stock dividends, splits do not convert surplus into capital stock. 

The stock split usually is accomplished by reducing the par value per share and increasing the number of shares correspondingly. For no-par stock with a stated value, the stated value is decreased and the number of shares is increased. When no-par

stock does not have a stated value the old shares need not be called in, all that is necessary is the issuance of additional no-par shares. In practice, the corporation merely issues certificates for the new shares and stickers to be attached to the old certificates. 

If the par or stated value is changed, the entry made is a debit to the old capital stock account and a credit to the new capital stock account, the amounts being equal. 

It should be noted that, whereas the stock dividend converts surplus into capital stock and to that extent eliminates the possibility of cash dividends, the stock split does not affect the surplus account. Therefore after the stock split there still may be a possibility of cash dividends if the cash is available.

SUMMARY 

This chapter may be summarized by a consideration of F. J. 

Lasmer Corporation's statement of condition and income statement as shown in Figures 17 and 18. Each item in this illustration should be considered in the light of the discussion in this chapter. 

It is instructive for the student to think through the debit and credit entries represented by each item and the entries that may be made subsequently. From the viewpoint of the common stockholder, what is revealed about policy by the actions of the board of directors with respect to the year's net income?

CHAPTER VI 

 IN CHAPTERS IV and V situations are presented that are encoun tered commonly in the life of an active business corporation. 

Earlier it was pointed out that normal business activity may be expected to produce available cash in approximately the same amounts as the increase in surplus from net income. Several of the limitations to this expectation have already been presented in the previous chapter. The situation to be discussed here is one in which cash becomes available in excess of the net income, and it therefore presents a problem quite in contrast to the problems previously discussed in Chapter V concerning surplus, surplus reserves, and dividends.

Special Reductions of Corporate Capital 

The purpose of this chapter is to present five additional situations under the headings: Depletion; Treasury Stock; Interest, Discount, and Premium on Bonds Outstanding; Incorporation of

Single Proprietorship or Partnership; and Dissolution of a Corporation. These situations are known among accountants as special reductions of corporate capital. 

Situations involving certain corporate financial and accounting problems are encountered less frequently than the problems discussed in the corporation chapters. Nevertheless, every businessman should learn how each of these situations is properly reflected on the books of the corporation in order to keep the facts of the business so clear that he may be guided by them in planning the future development of the business. 

Every banker should understand these problems in order to have a well rounded appreciation of the corporate dilemmas facing modern business managers. Obviously, the banker must be able to comprehend and analyze the financial statements submitted by businessmen who are seeking to obtain bank loans. 118

DEPLETION 

The depletion phenomenon as a corporate financial and accounting problem results from the consumption of a natural resource or wasting asset, such as timberlands or mineral rights, by the business. The very nature of the business operation converts the natural resource asset into cash through the sale of the product. If this cash can be used more or less currently to replace units of the depleting asset by purchases of additional similar units, there is no special problem. Usually such replacement is not possible, and the resulting accumulation of excess cash becomes a very special problem that requires careful consideration by management.

The Funding of the Depletion 

If the amount could be determined, the cash resulting in each period from the conversion of the natural resource could be funded by investing it in earmarked securities. Thus the fund would equal the original cost of the natural resource when that resource is consumed completely. At that time the corporation would be faced with the question of the disposition of the fund. 

If it could be used to buy additional amounts of the natural resource, the whole process could be repeated. If the fund could not be so reinvested and if there were no other desirable investment opportunity, the fund could be converted into cash, the cash could be returned to the stockholders, and the corporation could be dissolved.

The Periodic Return of Cash to Stockholders 

The question quite naturally arises: If eventually the cash is to be returned to the stockholders, can it not be returned to them period by period when their original investment in the natural resource is converted into cash? The answer is in the affirmative, with the caution, however, that the situation must be very carefully explained to the stockholders to prevent them from confusing the return of depletion money with their dividends from profits. In other words, any such payments represent a return of capital previously invested.

Entries for Depletion 

The entries for depletion, both the lump sum return of the investment and the period-by-period return as the depletion results in available cash, may be clarified by an illustration. 

Assume that the Swenson Lumber Company begins business in January 19X1 with $800,000 invested in stumpage rights (that is, the right to cut the timber in a certain area) in addition to other investments of approximately $100,000 in plant, equipment, and so forth. The following tabulation presents the pertinent data from the company's experience over the eight-year period during which the timber is harvested. Per cent of Total 

Depletion

Expense 

(Per cent X cost) 

Net Income 

After All

Depletion Expense Allowance for Depletion of Stumpage. 

56,000 56,000 The Depletion Expense account would be closed to Expense and Revenue with all other expense accounts for the period. It is actually a substantial element in the cost of goods sold. The 

Allowance for Depletion of Stumpage is a contra or valuation account against Stumpage Rights. It is shown next to the Stumpage Rights account in the ledger and is subtracted from the Stumpage Rights item in the statement of condition.

An entry is made, similarly, for each year's depletion. The 

Allowance for Depletion of Stumpage accumulates each year until, at the end of 19X8, it would equal the $800,000 in the Stumpage Rights account. Assuming the simple condition that there is no expansion or contraction and that the total net income is paid out in cash dividends each year, we would find that the cash available each year equals the growth in the account, Allowance for Depletion of Stumpage. This depletion cash may be accumulated until the end of the project by an annual investment in securities for which the entry would be as follows:

If this pattern of transactions and entries is extended to December 31, 19X8, the net book value of the stumpage should be zero, and the investment of the stockholders should have been reduced to a net of $100,000 which is presumed to be the investment in assets other than stumpage. 

In actual practice the periodic return of their investment to

Surplus

Cash 25,000 25,000 25,000 56,000 81,000

In the interest of economy a single check may be mailed to each stockholder. The check is accompanied by a letter explaining how much of the check comprises a dividend and how much represents the return of original investment, which is made possible by the depletion of the wasting asset, stumpage rights. Each stockholder would reduce the cost of his stock by the amount of the investment returned, whereas the distribution out of earnings is clearly income to him. 

Calculation of Depletion 

The calculation of depletion on standing timber, shown in the illustration, is comparatively easy because the total of the available timber in the area concerned can be estimated closely (usually in terms of thousands of board feet) by the skilled timber cruiser; it is also comparatively easy to obtain figures for the annual production of board feet. When the wasting asset is coal or other minerals or natural gas or oil, the estimate of the total amount available is often much less accurate than in the case of standing timber. The accountant must be guided by the best expert advice that he can obtain initially, and he must be prepared to change his accounting if the experts later change their estimates of the total amount available. Necessary allowances must be made for those parts of the resource that, although known to be in existence, cannot be extracted at a reasonable cost. Coal and mineral veins may be too thin; oil may be too deep for economical pumping; and even standing timber may be located high on inaccessible mountains. Common sense enters each calculation in order to spread the cost of the wasting asset equitably over the actual units produced.

TREASURY STOCK 

A corporation may reacquire some of its own stock either by purchase or by donation. In some states stock may be reacquired only for the purpose of cancelation, which would reduce the size of the corporation. In other states reacquired stock may be kept alive in the corporation's treasury, that is, the corporation may own its own stock. If such stock had been fully paid for, that is, up to its par or stated value, the reacquired stock is known as treasury stock.

If the corporation sold new, previously unissued, stock below par or stated value, the buyer of the stock would be subject to assessment for the deficiency. Treasury stock, having been fully paid, may be resold by the corporation at a price below its par or stated value without the buyer's incurring a risk of additional assessment. If the stock was not fully paid, it may still be subject to assessment and is distinguished from treasury stock by being referred to merely as "reacquired stock." The fact that treasury stock is nonassessable permits the corporation some freedom in its use, which is often advantageous. 

Purchased Treasury Stock 

In small closely held corporations, the death of a stockholder creates a situation quite similar to that created by the death of a partner in a partnership. If the stockholder has no heirs to carry on his active participation in the affairs of the corporation and if no other acceptable purchaser stands ready to take the stock, the corporation itself may buy the stock of the deceased stockholder. (Sometimes the stockholder or his estate may be required to offer the stock to the corporation.)

If the corporation has unneeded cash, the repurchase may result in an actual permanent cancelation of the stock. The entry to reflect this purchase would be:

Capital Stock Surplus 

Cash 10,000 2,500 12,500 In this situation the price, $12,500, is determined by negotiation. 

The debit to Surplus represents the deceased stockholder's equity

in the accumulated surplus. If there were a deficit on the corporation's books, the price paid probably would be less than par and would necessitate a credit to the Deficit account instead of the debit to Surplus. The question of the proper valuation of the corporation's assets (including goodwill) and liabilities is involved in the proper valuation of the stock and the resulting entry. If we could assume that all of the valuations on the books were entirely correct (which of course is very seldom the fact) the repurchase would be exactly at book value. Any discount or premium on the books, which attaches to the retired stock, should be proportionately written off in the entry for such a retirement of stock.

If the corporation is not in a position, cash-wise, to retire the deceased's stock, it may still repurchase the stock from any funds available with the intention of reselling the stock later. In this case the stock is said to be kept alive in the treasury and so is considered treasury stock. The following entry would be made:

Treasury Stock 

Cash 12,500 12,500

When this stock is subsequently resold, a profit or loss may be realized on the sale. The entry for the sale of the treasury stock for $12,900 would be: Cash

Treasury Stock Profit on Sale of Treasury Stock 

12,900 12,500 400 If a statement of condition is drawn while the treasury stock is on the books, the cost of this stock is shown as a deduction from the total proprietorship as follows:

Capital Stock Surplus Less: Cost of Treasury Stock Total Stockholders' Equity 

$100,000 

50,000 $150,000 12,500 $137,500

Since the purchase of the company's own stock is in some respects similar to the purchase of any other investment, there is some danger that the treasury stock may be confused with such bona fide assets. Treasury stock is not an asset; therefore, to show such stock as an asset, either by itself or lumped with other investments, inflates both sides of the statement of condition.

If a stockholder in the closely held corporation wishes to retire, the corporation may repurchase his stock. The entries for this transaction parallel those for the repurchase of the stock of a deceased stockholder, although the practical conditions surrounding the determination of the price to be paid are quite different. 

Here, again, the repurchased stock may be canceled or may be kept alive as treasury stock with the intention of subsequent resale. 

Under some conditions, a corporation may use its own funds to buy and sell its own stock for the purpose of making a profit. This device may, of course, be open to serious abuse when a group of insiders who have privileged information about the profit prospects use the corporation's own money to buy from and sell to those who do not have privileged information.

Treasury Stock and Employee Stock Ownership Plan 

Many corporations have become aware of the advantage of selling stock to their employees. It usually seems desirable to offer the stock at a bargain price to attract the employees. New issues sold below par or stated value would not appeal to employees because of the liability to assessment as previously explained. The corporation may resolve this dilemma by buying a sizable block of its own shares in the open market at a time when the price is low and then selling these shares to employees over a period of time after the market price has recovered.

Usually a stock purchase plan is offered to employees on an instalment basis. The plan may be facilitated by arranging for the employee's payments to be deducted regularly from his wages. 

Assume that under the plan of the Paton Chemical Company the company purchases 10,000 shares of its own common stock in the open market at an average price of $60 per share; of these shares 3,000 are sold to employees on a payroll-deduction plan at $60 per share with 40 weekly instalments. Collections through payroll deductions are shown for two weekly periods in Figure 20.

Treasury Stock Subscribed Treasury Stock 

54,000 54,000 The account for the subscriptions receivable is, of course, a control account, and it is supported by detailed entries in the employee stock subscription ledger. The individual accounts in the subscription ledger would not be complicated. They would have to show debits for the original and for any subsequent subscriptions, and credits for the payroll deductions. As the shares are issued, a memorandum entry recording the fact of issuance would probably be made in the account. 

The general ledger entries shown in Figure 20 were made on the assumption that the subscriptions resulted from a particular campaign. If instead the opportunity to subscribe for stock is open at all times, the entries should be made from day to day or week to week in smaller amounts, but they do not differ essentially. 

The method of showing treasury stock and employee subscriptions on the statement of condition is interesting. The treasury stock itself is not an asset but should be shown as a subtraction in the stockholders' equity section. Any amounts which the company

has withheld from the employees, pursuant to the plan, comprise a current liability. With the accounts used in our illustration, the liability immediately after the collection of the second $4,500 instalment would be shown by treating the balance in the Subscriptions Receivable account as a contra item to the Treasury Stock Subscribed account in the current liability section. The equity side of the pro forma statement of condition would appear:

The showing of the liability to the subscribers may seem somewhat awkward and circuitous because the accountant insists on making a debit-credit entry to record the subscription by the employees. The subscription could then be regarded as the basis for memorandum and not as the basis for a formal entry. In this case, the first formal entry would be made for the deductions from the payroll, and the amount of the deduction would be established as a current liability entitled, Liability for Withholdings Under Em ployee Stock Subscription Plan. When specific subscriptions are fully paid, the issuance of the stock would be entered as a debit to this liability account and a credit to Treasury Stock. 

INTEREST, DISCOUNT, AND PREMIUM ON BONDS OUTSTANDING In Chapters III and IV, the economic, legal, and accounting aspects of corporate bond liabilities were presented. In this section bond interest, discount, and premium are explained.

Bond Rate, Effective Rate, and Price

In Chapter III we mentioned the important relationship between the rate of interest promised by the bond and the rate of interest which investors expect to receive and which they are in a position to demand because of conditions in the money market. 

If the bond rate is below this effective market rate the bonds will sell at a discount, the amount of the discount being in proportion to the disparity of the rates. Similarly a bond rate above the effective market rate will result in the sale of those bonds at a premium.

Since at its maturity the bond will be redeemed at par, the corporation's total interest burden is not merely the series of interest payments during the lifetime of the bond but the total of these payments plus the amount of the discount or minus the amount of the premium. Figure 21 illustrates the prices that would be paid for 10-year, 20-year, and 50-year bonds which pay 6%, 4%, and 3% at the varying rates shown in the column listing the effective rate.

It should be noted that the bond rate and the effective rate relationships result in discounts and premiums in accordance with the expected pattern. In each case when the effective rate equals the bond rate, the bond sells at par regardless of its maturity; in each case in which the effective rate is lower than the bond rate the bond sells at a premium and the premium is larger for the longer-lived bonds. When the effective rate is higher than the bond rate, the bond sells at a discount, which is larger for a bond of longer term.

Examine closely the situation of the corporation which issued the 10-year bond listed on line 6 of Figure 21. This bond, with a $10,000 par, pays 4% or $400 per year to the buyer. The buyer is in a position to demand 5%, the effective market rate. Therefore, he can purchase the bond at $9,228 at a discount of $772. When the bond is due, the corporation must pay the full $10,000 or $772 more than it received. This discount of $772 plus the $400 per year interest for 10 years is the total interest burden on the corporation, and it is the total interest income to the bondholder. 

The $4,772 of total interest works out at 5% per year of total interest compounded annually, as any mathematician could verify.

Bond Effective 

Years to Maturity 

Line 

Rate Rate 

10 20 50 123 6% 5% 10,772 11,246 11,826 2 6% 6% 10,000 10,000 10,000 6% 7% 9,298 8,941 8,620 456 789 4% 3% 10,853 11,488 12,573 4% 4% 10,000 10,000 10,000 4% 5% 9,228 8,754 8,174 3% 2% 10,898 11,635 13,142 3% 3% 10,000 10,000 10,000 3% 4% 9,189 8,641 7,752 

FIGURE 21. PRICES PAID FOR A $10,000 BOND UNDER Straight-Line Amortization VARYING CONDITIONS 

The accounting entries made by the issuing corporation to portray the relationships outlined above are illustrated in journal entry form for the $10,000, 10-year, 4% bond issued at an effective rate of 5% for the price of $9,228 in the following illustration. As the discount is written off or amortized over the life of the bond, the net carrying value of the bond increases. At maturity, the discount has disappeared entirely, and the par value shown in the bond account constitutes the then true liability. 

For years controversy has raged around the proper method of showing the bond discount in the statement of condition. Accounting theorists agree that the balance of the discount on bonds should be shown as a contra account against the par value of the bonds in the fixed liability section of the statement. However, the majority of practicing accountants still show the discount on the asset side of the statement, usually in the final section, under a heading, Other Assets. Actually it would be difficult to stretch the definition of the word asset to include discount on bonds. As long as the current amortization entries are properly made, however, there is no particular harm in following the common practice. 

It should also be noted that the amortization of the discount in the illustration is computed for each year as one-tenth of the total. This method is known as the straight-line method of amortization. It is simple to calculate and apply, and it is sufficiently accurate for most purposes. Under this method the relation of the

interest expense to the net liability shown in any year is only approximately the effective rate since the total annual interest remains the same while the net liability is increasing. 

At the time the bonds are sold: Cash

Discount on Bonds Bonds Payable 

At each annual interest payment date:

Amortization by the Actuarial Method 

10,000.00 10,000.00

Amortization of bond discount by the method that is actuarially correct requires a separate calculation for each year's interest expense. Under this method the effective rate is applied each year to the net liability as shown at the beginning of that year. In our previous illustration the interest expense on the bond for the first year would be .05 $9,228 or $461.40, instead of the straightline amount of $477.20. The entry would be:

Interest Expense 

Cash 

Discount on Bonds 

461.40 400.00 61.40 

In the second year the liability would be $9,289.40 (that is, $9,228.00+ $61.40). The interest expense for the second year would be .05 × $9,289.40 or $464.47, and the entry would be:

Cash 

Interest Expense Discount on Bonds 

464.47 400.00 64.47 

A comparison of the two methods of amortizing the discount reveals that, under the actuarial method, amortization begins at an amount lower than under the straight-line method, but that it increases each year so that during the later years of the bond's life it is greater than the straight-line amortization.

Often bonds are sold at an effective rate which is not an even percentage but a multiplaced decimal. Therefore the calculations for discount amortization would have to be carried to many decimal places if the final amortization is to come out exactly even. 

This degree of accuracy is usually unnecessary, and the accountant may be content with an accuracy to the nearest dollar, or the nearest ten dollars, in the annual amounts. The amortization for the last year would take up the slack for the accumulated errors. 

To be certain that such an error is not cumulating to an unreasonable amount, the accountant should prepare the entire table for the amortization schedule in advance and preserve it as a guide in making his annual entries. Such a table for the bond previously discussed would appear as follows:

Note that the interest as calculated for the tenth year (.05 X $9,905.03) amounted to $495.25. This amount if used as the annual entry would have provided amortization of $95.25 or 28¢ more than the discount remaining on the bonds. Thus 28¢ is the amount of the cumulative error, and it is so small an amount that it may be disregarded. The final year's entry would be $494.97, or exactly the amount needed to amortize the remaining discount.

Accounting for More Frequent Payments of Interest 

Most bonds pay interest semiannually or quarterly instead of annually as in the illustration. The frequency of the interest payments does not alter the basic relationships between the interest rates and the prices of bonds nor does it affect the method of accounting for the discount nor the way the discount is shown in the statement of condition. If the 10-year bond paid interest quarterly, it would require forty entries for interest and amortization. Under the straight-line method of amortization, one-fortieth of the discount would be amortized with each interest payment. 

Under the actuarial method of amortization, both the bond rate and the effective rate would be considered on a quarterly basis, and the calculations would be made with interest compounded quarterly instead of annually.

Amortization of Bond Premium 

The basic thinking behind the amortization of bond premium is the same as that behind bond discount. The straight-line method is used in the great majority of cases, especially when the amounts are small and the bonds are relatively short-lived; the correct actuarial method is reserved for cases in which the amounts are high and the bonds are long-lived. 

Since the premium account has a credit balance whereas the discount account has a debit balance, the entries for the amortization of premium are the reverse of those for the amortization of discount, and the effect of the amortization is to reduce the annual interest burden rather than to increase it, as in the case of the discount.

The 10-year 4% bond with an effective rate of 3% which sells for $10,853 (line 4 of Figure 21) would require the following entries to be made:

Cash 

Bonds Payable Premium on Bonds 

10,853.00 10,000.00 853.00

On the date of each annual interest payment (assuming the straight-line method), the entries would be: 

Bond Interest Expense Premium on Bonds 

Cash 3,914.70 85.30 4,000.00

The balance in the premium account is shown as an addition to the par value of the bonds in the statement of condition. Ten of these interest and amortization entries would reduce the premium on the bonds to zero by the end of the tenth year with the result that the net liability is $10,000 at the redemption date. 

A partial table for the calculation of premium amortization by the actuarial method is shown in Figure 23, together with the interest and amortization entries for the first two years.

FIGURE 23. AMORTIZATION OF BOND PREMIUM BY THE ACTUARIAL METHOD INCORPORATION OF SINGLE PROPRIETORSHIP OR PARTNERSHIP 

After a period of growth, many businesses which were originally begun as single proprietorships or partnerships find it advantageous to incorporate. Sometimes the determining factor is the protection given to stockholders by the limited liability feature of the corporation, although more often the greater opportunity for expansion through stock issues is the advantage sought.

Business Aspects of Incorporation 

After incorporation the business usually proceeds much as before. The individuals who were previously the direct owners or partners are now the principal stockholders. Customarily they are also members of the board of directors and more often than not the principal salaried managers. To the public, their customers, the change may not be very apparent, but legally the old single proprietorship is gone, the old partnership is dissolved, and a new legal person, the corporation, has come into existence. 

From the general creditor's or banker's viewpoint only the assets of the corporation henceforth will stand behind the credit of the business, although of course any debts incurred prior to incorporation are still a claim against the former proprietors as individuals.

The accounting for the incorporation of the business should follow the legal aspect. The accountant recognizes the problem as twofold. The first step is the sale of the assets, the payment of the liabilities, and the winding up of old business. The second step is the organization of the new corporation, its purchase of the assets, and sometimes its assumption of the liabilities of the former business. Thus the former books are technically closed and a new set of books is opened, although of course some of the detailed records, such as the customers' subsidiary ledger, may be continued in the new system.

Generally it is desirable to revalue the assets and the liabilities of the former business before incorporation, especially if the corporation is to sell stock for cash to newcomers. Without such revaluation, the interest of either the old owners or the new investors might be seriously disadvantaged. Actually the revaluations might be the result of long bargaining sessions between the partners and some of the new investors. Some of the values might be agreed upon only after consulting disinterested outsiders, such as certified public accountants or appraisers. 

Entries for the Incorporation of a Partnership 

Assume that the partnership of Briant and Burns, whose statement of condition is shown in Figure 24 is to be incorporated as the Excello Corporation, with the sale of additional stock to out-

siders to provide cash for expansion. (Specific additional assumptions are posed for each entry that is made.)

FIGURE 25. STATEMENT OF CONDITION OF A CORPORATION AFTER THE INCORPORATION OF A PARTNERSHIP Variations in Arrangements and Entries 

The preceding incorporation of a partnership was planned as simply as possible. Common variations from this simple plan which are made to accommodate special circumstances need

cause no difficulty in the making of entries if the facts themselves are clearly understood. For instance, the partnership cash or certain other assets may be divided between partners before the business is incorporated. The amount of cash or the value of assets received by each partner would be debited to his Capital account. The corporation would issue that much less stock to each partner in the final settlement. In addition, the partners might use some of their cash to pay certain liabilities before transferring the business. In the illustration they might well have paid the two accrued items since they had sufficient cash. 

The corporation may have a more complicated capital structure comprising preferred stock as well as common stock. The partners may subscribe for a certain amount of the preferred and for some of the common. The corporation may issue no-par stock or it may issue both common and preferred stock at a discount or at a premium. In such instances it would be necessary to assume that the partners subscribed for these stocks on the same basis as the outsiders, otherwise either the partners or the outsiders would pay an unfair price.

The partners might turn their accounts receivable over to the corporation with a guarantee of their collectability. In this case the corporation would probably hold back some of the stock to be issued to the partners until the acounts were collected. 

The corporation might not want to assume some of the partners' old liabilities. If so, the liabilities would remain the personal liabilities of the partners who would get additional stock to compensate them for the higher net values given the corporation. 

There is no limit to the variations in the arrangements between the partners and the corporation. However, the variations previously discussed may be considered the most probable. The student would be well advised to visualize these entries in order to have a practical command of the situation. 

DISSOLUTION OF A CORPORATION 

Many of the basic facts of corporate finance and accounting are highlighted by the problems arising at the time of dissolution of the corporation. Therefore, although such circumstances are rare, it is desirable to study the accounting for dissolution.

Profitable Dissolution 

The process of consolidating businesses into larger units goes on constantly and causes many individual corporations to pass out of existence. This process necessitates a liquidation of the corporation just as truly as if the dissolution had been caused by insolvency. The entries to be made for such a profitable dissolution are interesting.

To study a clear-cut case assume that the corporation sells all its assets for cash and fully pays its liabilities in cash. Only one asset would remain, namely, the amount of cash to balance the entire stockholders' equity. If the capitalization was simple with only common stock outstanding at par, the equity would consist of only the capital stock account and a sizable credit balance in the surplus (or retained earnings) account. A liquidating dividend would be declared to the full extent of the remaining cash. An entry to record this liquidating dividend would close the books and adequately record the dissolution of the corporation. 

Capital Stock Surplus¹ 

Cash 

The Pattern of Dissolution 

XX,XXX X,XXX XX,XXX Assume a more complex situation, one in which the corporation has several issues of stock outstanding, both preferred and common, and possibly one or more surplus reserves. The basic pattern of dissolution involves the same three steps: 

1. All of the assets are converted into cash, profits and losses being recognized and closed into surplus. 

2. The cash is used first to pay all liabilities. 

3. Liquidating dividends are paid to the full extent of the remaining cash.

Types of Surplus and the Dissolution Problem 

It should be apparent that after steps one and two, the previously important distinctions between the types of surplus (such as: available, nonavailable, from appreciation, from reorganization, and from appropriations) and the types of surplus reserves 1 

¹ Surplus may have to be credited if a deficit results from the liquidation.

(such as: for expansion, for retirement of bonds, for self-insurance, for contingencies, and so forth) lose their significance and all types become unrestricted surplus. Each type of surplus could be analyzed individually. However, a discussion of three examples serves to demonstrate the general pattern of analysis. 

If, at some past time, the corporation had recognized the appreciation of land value from $10,000 to $60,000, the resulting $50,000 credit to Surplus from Appreciation of Land certainly was not available for dividends. When that land is sold in step one of the dissolution procedure, however, the increased value of the land is converted into cash and the amount of $50,000 does become available for distribution. If the actual sale price is more than the anticipated $60,000, the excess is recognized as additional available surplus; if it is less, the loss on the sale is recognized as a reduction of surplus.

If the corporation has been building up a reserve for expansion, the amounts therein, previously held out of dividends by specific action of the directors, now are restored to the available category by the more basic decision to dissolve, which of course overrules any plans for expansion. 

If the corporation has earmarked reserves for self-insurance against fire, tornado, or the risk of other property loss, such reserves are no longer needed since all the property has been converted into cash. Similarly the reserves for other contingencies revert to available surplus when all normal business is suspended by the dissolution process. These statements are conditioned upon the full implementation of step two, namely, that all liabilities have been paid. If any contingent liabilities (such as damage suits against the corporation) remain unsettled, the final step of dissolution should be suspended voluntarily or it would be held up by court injunction.

A reserve for retirement of bonds becomes available surplus if step two has actually been accomplished by the retirement of the bonds. Presumably any sinking fund investments would have been converted into cash to help finance the paying of the bonds. 

Also, bonds presumably would have to be retired at par although unamortized discount still appeared on the books. Some bond contracts provide for retirement before maturity at a specific premium price. Whether, in the absence of such a provision, bonds

that had been issued at a premium could be retired before maturity at par would be a fine legal question. The refinements of legal action that might be necessary to retire bonds before their normal maturity date is beyond the scope of this text. The payment of the bond liability pursuant to step two actually might be assumed by the new corporation, resulting from the consolidation. This decision would have to be effected with the consent of the bondholders acting through their trustee, and probably with specific court approval. A discussion of this technicality also is beyond the scope of this text but deserves mention. It is one of many situations in which the accountant depends on legal advice. 

Stock Discount and Premium in Dissolution 

Discount and premium on common and preferred stocks require careful consideration. Although these items most properly are shown as permanent adjustments of par values when the company is a going concern, they should probably be considered as minus and plus surplus elements at the time of dissolution. 

This treatment might not seem entirely equitable if the preferred stock had been issued at a sizable premium and the common stock at a sizable discount. Application of this rule would result in retiring both the preferred stock and the common stock at par, in spite of the fact that the original investments were not at par. 

Since the issuance of stock at a discount is quite rare (and illegal, in many jurisdictions), the practical situation is more likely one in which one issue is sold at par and the other at a premium, or at different premium rates. Even in these cases if the premiums are closed into surplus and the stocks retired at par, which seems to be the rule, there is an unavoidable element of inequity.

Disposition of Surplus Calculated as a Final Dividend 

Any discussion of surplus available for dividends at time of dissolution leads to the main question, namely: How is the distribution determined as between the several stock issues? We are still concerned with the profitable dissolution situation. The answer is that such a distribution of surplus is subject to exactly the same rules that would govern a dividend, of the same amount, paid under normal going-concern operations. All provisions with

respect to preferred cumulative dividends in arrears and with respect to any participating rights also must be taken into consideration. After such a dividend has been paid, sufficient cash should remain to pay the par amounts of the several stock issues. 

Although the amounts to be paid to the several classes of stockholders must be calculated under dividend rules, such amounts are not considered dividend income to the recipients for income tax purposes. As long as the payment is made in a bona fide liquidation, the tax status of the individual stockholder will be determined by the basis on which he holds the stock. For those who have purchased their shares at a high price, there may be an actual tax loss; for others, there may be a tax gain that in most cases would be a capital gain in the income tax sense. 

No-Par Stock in Dissolution 

To this point our discussion has been restricted to par value stocks, although most of the foregoing applies equally well to the corporation whose stock or stocks are no-par. The complications of discount and premium relating to par value stock have their parallel in the stated value aspect of no-par stock. When two kinds of no-par stock have been issued and the capital stock accounts are credited for stated values that vary as to the actual percentage of investment, as between the several issues, another potential inequity exists. The inequity arises from the fact that any excess over stated value is technically surplus to be distributed as a dividend probably in some proportion other than the proportion in which it was received from the several classes of stockholders. 

Understandably, the problems of dissolution are not usually in mind at the time of organization, but the accountant should be fully conscious of the possible situation when advising the organizers as to the ramifications of the stated value problem. 

Paying Off in Stocks

There is another circumstance, related to profitable dissolution, that opens a new range of practical difficulties without altering the basic facts. Frequently, instead of receiving cash for its assets, the corporation, dissolving as a result of consolidation, receives its compensation in the form of stock of the resulting or successor corporation. The dissolving corporation's stockholders would

have the same rights in the distribution of this stock as they would have had in a final cash distribution. The main problem is how to effectuate such a stock distribution since it might involve the issuance of fractional shares. The situation might be quite awkward if many stockholders were involved. 

Dissolution with Heavy Losses 

When business losses are so heavy that the corporate stockholders' equity is wiped out entirely and the creditors suffer the loss of part of their claim, the settlement is usually placed in the hands of a court. The accounting in this case is dictated by special legal rules arising from the complications of technical bankruptcy and is beyond the scope of this text. Our present study, however, is concerned with the cases in which the losses caused by unsuccessful operation and by the process of liquidation are large enough to cause a problem in the settlement as between stockholders but do not lead to the complications of technical bankruptcy. 

The pattern of procedure parallels steps one and two described earlier for profitable dissolution. The procedural steps may be outlined as follows: 

1. All the assets are converted into cash, the profits and losses being recognized and closed into surplus (which in this case presumably develops a large debit balance, or deficit). 

2. The cash is used first to pay liabilities. 

3. The surplus (as adjusted by the closing of all reserves and the recognition of all losses) now shows a debit balance. The important question at this point is how the loss shall be shared by the several classes of stockholders. This decision must be reached before step four can be taken. Step four is the final paying of the remaining cash to the stockholders. 

The Meaning of the Term, Preferred as to Assets The answer to the question of the sharing of the losses, which is the essence of step three, lies largely in the reading of the fine print of the preferred stock contract. If there is a provision in the charter which declares the preferred stock to be preferred as to assets, it will be found, on careful reading, to mean that the common stockholders must absorb the losses to the full amount of their equity before any loss shall be charged to the preferred

stockholders. Thus these words which often pass almost unnoticed while the business is operating smoothly become extremely important in event of dissolution with heavy losses.

The measure of the amount of such preference as to assets usually is the par value of the preferred stock, regardless of whether it was issued at a discount or at a premium. In the case of no-par preferred stock the preference as to assets is presumably its stated value. If there is no stated value as such, the terms of the charter should specify the amount per share of the asset preference.

Cumulative Dividend and Asset Preferences 

If the preferred stock is noncumulative as to dividends, its dividend status (whether the dividends are current or in arrears) is not significant at the time of final dissolution. On the other hand, if the preferred stock is cumulative, it may or may not have a claim for unpaid dividends at time of dissolution. Its depending on the exact provisions of the charter. For instance, if the preferred stock is preferred as to assets and has specified rights to cumulative dividends, these provisions simply increase the share of the losses that must be borne by the common stockholders. 

Actually, any rights of the preferred stockholders to arrears of cumulative dividends at time of dissolution must be interpreted as part of their share in the preference as to assets since no dividends can be distributed when there is no surplus. In the case of very severe losses the asset preference of the preferred stock may wipe out the common stock equity completely, leaving the preferred equity still protected. In still worse cases the preferred stockholders also may lose heavily in spite of their asset preference. 

When the interpretation as to the sharing of losses has been made, the accounting should be rather obvious. There will be a credit to the Surplus (Deficit) account and debits to the several stock accounts to show the sharing of the loss. The entry for the final sharing of the cash is merely a paying of the remaining balances in the two capital stock accounts. 

Complicated Capital Structures and Dissolution 

Sometimes there is more than one class of preferred stock as well as several kinds of common stock. These circumstances

further complicate the final settlement but do not change the basic principles. A careful scrutiny of the charter provisions and a reference of all doubtful or ambiguous points to the court which has jurisdiction in such cases is the only possible procedure. Above all, the accountant needs to realize that two problems are involved. 

The first problem is the determination of the rights of claimants. 

The second, and far easier problem, is to decide upon the debits and credits to be made which will adequately reflect the facts of the situation.
