CHAPTER VII  

IT IS EVIDENT that the current assets have a special significance for business management because management has its greatest flexibility with respect to the current assets. Cash is the most general of all assets. A business manager can use the cash of the business in an unlimited number of ways—to pay debts, to buy merchandise or other current assets, to buy plant or other permanent assets, or to hire any kind of services.

Other assets, such as the receivables and inventories, which will be converted into cash relatively soon in the normal course of business manifest this desirable flexibility in a lesser degree. By their relative nearness to cash, however, these assets still place the business manager in a position that is strong because of its flexibility. 

It is this desirable flexibility of position that really lies behind the importance to be attached to the total amount of current assets, to the excess of current assets over current liabilities, and to the current ratio. The investment of money in plant and other fixed assets is necessary of course, but such investments commit the funds for protracted periods; at any one time there is little that management can do to alter its previous decisions regarding investments.

The purpose of this chapter and Chapter VIII is to examine more closely the actual conditions surrounding the management of these current assets and the techniques for reflecting the pertinent facts in the accounts. 

CASH 

We have been regarding cash as the one asset which is understood and accounted for most easily. By and large that attitude toward cash is justified, but other aspects of cash still need careful consideration. 154

Borderline Items 

Should undeposited checks of customers be included in cash? 

Should postal and express money orders and traveler's checks endorsed to the firm be included? Should the IOU's of its officers and employees? Postage stamps? Christmas seals held for sale to em ployees? Postdated checks? Checks marked N. S. F. (Not Sufficient 

Funds) returned by banks? Savings account balances? Cash in closed banks? Funds in escrow? Certificates of deposit? All of these and many other items have been found included in cash, and the answer cannot safely be too categorical because circumstances bearing on the decision vary widely. 

As a general rule only the items unquestionably and promptly available for expenditure may be included in cash. This decision permits the inclusion of such items as newly received customers' checks, money orders, and traveler's checks if they are bona fide and if they will be deposited in the normal course of business and thus become in fact and promptly a part of expendable cash. Savings account balances and certificates of deposit are deemed to be cash even though notice of withdrawal may be required. They should be identified in the accounts by their special titles, but they may be included, at least for certain purposes, in the general term cash. For instance, in a summarized statement of condition issued to stockholders as part of the annual report no good purpose would be served by differentiating the several conditions of the cash account. 

With regard to cash in escrow, postdated checks, N. S. F. checks, officers' IOU's, cash in closed banks and other similar items, it is necessary to examine the particular circumstances very closely. 

Generally the accountant should be very cautious about these items. Although it is possible to imagine special circumstances that might make each of these items qualify as cash, it is usually better to identify them specifically and to treat them as special receivables. 

If postage stamps and Christmas seals are being used, they are office supplies; if they are held for resale, they are technically merchandise, although seals costing a few dollars may be carried in petty cash without serious infringement of principle. Often they are carried in petty cash merely to avoid unnecessary record keeping.

Reconciliation of the Bank Balance 

By far the largest element of cash in the typical business is the commercial deposit or checking account commonly known as Cash in Bank. The problem here is to determine the exact amount that properly may be shown as Cash in Bank on the particular date of the statement of condition. 

The company's account, Cash in Bank, and the bank's account with the depositor are reciprocal accounts in the sense that all proper debits and credits to the one account sooner or later must become credits and debits in the other. The difficulty is created and explained by the time-lag which causes the lack of direct agreement in the reciprocal accounts. Checks drawn by the company do not reach the bank until a day or several days later; the bank's charges and credits for services, collections, and so forth often are not recorded in the company's account until the bank statement is received several days after the end of the month. 

Hence, in an active commercial account it is very unlikely that either the bank statement balance or the cash account balance represents the real contractual or true cash balance as of any particular date.

There is no need for either the bank or the depositor to be concerned with regard to the depositor's true balance on a day-today basis.

However, it is good sense to prove, not less frequently than once a month, that the two records in fact may be reconciled. If the accountant is to prepare a statement of condition, it is desirable to reconcile the two records in a form that reflects the true cash balance to be shown in his statement. 

An example of a reconciliation which develops the true balance of cash in bank appears in Figure 26. Note that it consists of two sections. The first section begins with the balance as shown on the bank statement. To this balance is added the amount of a deposit made on the last day of the month too late to be included on the statement. From the balance is subtracted the amount of the company's outstanding checks. These two corrections adjust the balance on the bank statement to $5,260.14 or the amount for which the company properly could write checks at that moment.

MARSALA CORPORATION 

Reconciliation 

March 31, 19XX 

Balance per bank statement, March 31

Add: 

Deposit made after banking hours on March 31 $6,333.70 1,658.72 $7,992.42

Deduct: 

Checks outstanding, March 31 #2681 $500.00 #2684 628.71 #2693 718.11 #2694 62.62 #2699 822.84 2,732.28 

Corrected bank statement balance. $5,260.14 

Balance per Company's account $5,138.68

Add: 

Bond coupons clipped by bank ... $12.00

Proceeds of sight draft on Smith collected for company by bank 497.54 509.54 $5,648.22

Deduct: 

Collection charges on out-of-town items $14.95 

N. S. F. charge-back, Jones 363.13 ...

Credit inquiry fee 

10.00 388.08 

$5,260.14

Corrected balance per books 

FIGURE 26. TWO-PART OR TRUE BALANCE FORM OF BANK RECONCILIATION 

The second part of the reconciliation begins with the balance of the company's Cash in Bank account and makes corrections for the two types of items which the bank has recorded but which are not yet reflected on the company's books. This part of the reconciliation frequently has more items than the first part. Again the addition and subtraction of the items lead to the corrected balance for Cash in Bank. If the situation is in fact in balance, the two corrected figures will be equal. If these figures do not agree, it may indicate that some item necessary to the reconciliation is still missing or that there is a shortage in the company's cash.

In the actual use of this type of reconciliation, it is advisable to prepare the form with the two balances and with plenty of space provided in the add and deduct section under each. When the items in the reconciliation are studied, two questions are asked: "Which of the balances is wrong with respect to this item?” and "Is the balance too large or too small with respect to this item?" The most complicated items developed in the experience of business become quite obvious when they are handled in this way. The two questions point directly to the place in the reconciliation where the item must be entered. 

In a textbook problem the necessary items for a reconciliation must be presented to the student in a rather obvious form. In actual practice the most difficult phase of the reconciliation may be the ferreting out of the items to be used. 

For instance, to discover which deposits were too late for credit, the accountant must check the deposits shown to have been made by the company's books with the deposits credited on the bank's statement. This operation is not only useful to identify the late deposit but is extremely valuable in verifying the fact that all deposits presumed to have been made during the period were in fact made. This verification would uncover any theft of funds received and recorded but stolen before they get to the bank. To facilitate this verification, the company should have a strict rule that the exact total of each day's cash receipts be deposited at the close of the day's business or the following morning. If this rule is observed, it is much easier for the company's own auditor or for an outside auditor to follow the "cash trail" and to guarantee that the cash has been accounted for honestly. When deposits are made irregularly and in amounts not corresponding to day-by-day receipts, the auditor can compare the total month's receipts with the total month's deposits (making due allowance for undeposited amounts at the beginning and end of the month). This type of checking fails to protect against the possibility that employees may "borrow" funds early in the month and "repay" them before the end of the month without detection. An airtight rule with regard to daily deposits avoids this possibility and is one of the very important parts of an internal control system. 

The determination of the amount of outstanding checks involves the comparison of the checks returned by the bank with

the record of the checks written. The actual process may involve a comparison of the returned and canceled checks with the check stubs in the checkbook, with the cash disbursement book record of checks, or even, with a duplicate file of dummy checks (as is done in punched card systems). Great care must be exercised to see that checks outstanding at the end of the previous month and still outstanding are not overlooked. Checks which have been voided for any reason and checks upon which payment has been stopped should not be included as outstanding. Their amounts should be added back to the balance in the Cash in Bank account and to the running balance on the stubs if such a balance is carried thereon.

Most of the items for correction in the second part of the reconciliation are discovered by a study of the bank statement. For instance the two items bond coupons clipped by bank, and proceeds of draft collected for company by bank in Figure 26 would be discovered in the bank statement column for deposits. The three items deducted likewise would have been discovered by a careful scrutiny of the checks and Other Charges column of the bank statement. Usually these items are identified by some code symbol. Generally, the company would have been notified of most of these charges and credits when they were made by the bank. If the company's bookkeeper had made the proper entry in the cashbooks when so notified, the items would not be involved in the reconciliation at the end of the month. 

One of the considerable advantages of the reconciliation in the form of Figure 26 is that the second section practically indicates what entries the bookkeeper has to make to bring the books into accord with the facts. The situation in Figure 26 would require entries to be made to correct the company's Cash in Bank account as follows: 

Cash in Bank

Bond Interest Revenue Account Receivable, Smith Collection Department Expense Account Receivable, Jones. 

Credit Department Expense 

Cash in Bank 509.54 12.00 497.54 14.95 363.13 10.00 388.08 

In practice these entries would be made in the cash receipts and

cash disbursements journals respectively, but for our purposes the effect is shown more clearly in these general journal entries. 

Multiple Cash Accounts 

The simplest and clearest cash and bank situation occurs when the company has separate accounts for Cash (or Cash on Hand) and Cash in Bank. This kind of a Cash in Bank account is contemplated in the previous illustration. 

Sometimes, however, a company may have only a single account called Cash which embraces cash on hand and cash in bank. In this case the stubs in the checkbook may carry the record of cash in bank. If so, it is the check stub balance which must be reconciled with the balance on the bank statement. A careful inspection of these arrangements is necessary in preparation for the task of reconciliation, since otherwise one may be trying to reconcile figures that cannot be expected to agree. 

Another common situation occurs when the company has two or more bank accounts, each of which must be reconciled. This situation introduces another type of adjustment resulting from a confusion between the accounts on the company's books. If, for instance, a deposit is actually made to account "A" but the bookkeeper adds the amount to the stub balance in checkbook “B,” each balance will fail to reconcile with its respective bank statement. If both accounts are in the same bank, it is possible for the bank to credit or debit the wrong account which introduces still another interesting element. Usually multiple reconciliations can best be prepared in parallel columns. If the two-part true balance form is used properly, complicated situations can be reconciled by anyone who faces the situation with understanding and a minimum of real care.

Sometimes a company has several checking accounts in the same bank each of which has a special purpose. For instance the company might have its general checking account, a special payroll account, and a special dividend payments account all in the same bank. Experience indicates that such a divison of functions often simplifies the problem of bank relationships. Customarily, the checks for payroll and dividends are quite different from each other in appearance (color, wording, and so forth) and from the general account checks. These differences aid both the bank and

ཕྲ the company in avoiding confusion between the accounts. In spite of these precautions, however, errors may still creep in which are discovered and corrected only at the time of the attempted reconciliation.

Internal Control System To Protect Cash 

Any fairly large company can protect its cash effectively from potential dishonesty as well as from common everyday errors by an internal control system. This means the organization of the accounting system and the laying down of such rules for the handling of cash and all related transactions that no one person could steal cash and cover up the defalcation. If the system is properly designed, the work of each employee is more or less automatically checked by the work of other employees with the result that three or four persons would have to conspire to conceal any misappropriation. The mere existence of these controls is a safeguard against any fraud.

Several important features of internal control already have been provided in our earlier discussion. The petty cash system 

(described in Accounting I, Chapter X, The Voucher System) is a standard feature of internal control. The rule stated earlier in this chapter that each day's receipts shall be deposited in full is very important. The systematic reconciliation of every bank account at the end of each month and the use of special bank accounts for special purposes described previously are internal control devices. Some other useful procedures which aid in internal control are mentioned in the following paragraphs although the list is by no means an exhaustive one. 

1. The person opening incoming mail should make a list in duplicate of each check or other cash item received. One copy of this list is taken to the cashier with the cash items, the other copy is delivered to the company's internal auditor to use later in verifying the deposits acknowledged by the bank. 

2. The cashier who makes deposits has no authority to indorse checks for cashing but only to indorse them for deposit to the company's account. 

3. The cashier who makes deposits has no authority and no duties with respect to the writing of checks for the company. 

4. The person or persons who have authority to sign checks or

who handle or receive cash for the company should have no access to the journals and ledgers. 

5. Any credits to customers' accounts (or notes), other than for cash received (such as sales returns and bad debt write-offs), must be initialed by some responsible person other than the cashier. 

6. All persons handling cash should be bonded.

Many other more detailed provisions can be established further to safeguard cash. The nature of these provisions depends on the specific business conditions, the personnel, the physical layout of the office, and many other practical considerations. It is intended in this text only to present a few of the main devices which constitute internal controls.

It is much more difficult to provide a system of internal controls in the small business than in the larger business. With fewer employees in the office it is not possible to divide the duties adequately. This disadvantage is offset partially by the fact that in the small business the owner (owners or top managers) is better acquainted with each employee and can personally observe dayto-day activities.

ACCOUNTS RECEIVABLE 

The accounting for accounts receivable is outlined generally in previous chapters. The basic fact is that accounts receivable represent a heavy volume situation. Even a small business may have several hundred charge account customers, and a large department store may have hundreds of thousands of these customers. The characteristic feature of accounts receivable accounting is the control account in the general ledger and the agreement between the control account and the subsidiary ledger in which the detailed accounts are carried. 

Sectional Controls 

In the large volume situation, this control usually is subdivided into intermediate controls by alphabetic or geographic classification of customers. Thus many department stores use the technique of having a single master control account for all customers which directly controls the alphabetic section control accounts. Each of the section accounts in turn controls the detailed accounts for the

customers in its group. Each alphabetical section may consist of from 1,000 to 2,500 individual accounts, as this is a conveniently sized unit to use in checking the accuracy of the daily postings. 

Some businesses may have accounts receivable of several quite different categories as for instance, wholesale, retail, and instalment. In this case it is usually wise to treat them as independent systems, each category having its own control account and subsidiary ledger. Accounts receivable due from a company's own officers and employees usually are segregated from regular customers' accounts. Accounts receivable arising from occasional sales of fixed assets, deposits with public utilities, amounts due from the government for tax refunds, and other incidental receivables should be kept separate from accounts due from regular customers. 

The reason for segregating these items is that all the routines of credit granting and collection which are organized for regular receivables do not apply to these irregular items, for example, accounts receivable from employees. The irregular items need special attention which they might not receive if they were not shown separately on the records. 

The Problem of Bad Debts 

In spite of careful credit granting and diligent collection procedures, every business which sells on account must expect some accounts to prove uncollectable. Percentagewise the bad accounts may vary from a small fraction of one per cent of sales in good times to two or three per cent in periods of depression, and of course the percentages vary widely in different businesses even under similar basic conditions. 

Although the failure to collect any particular account is clearly a loss to the business since it brings in no attendant advantages, the overall phenomenon of uncollectable accounts is considered an expense. There are definite advantages to a business in selling on account and the inevitable failure to collect a certain percentage of the resulting accounts receivable is regarded as a reasonable price to pay for these advantages. Bad debts therefore may be considered an expense rather than a loss as long as the percentages are held to reasonable limits. 

Management certainly must take into consideration the probable loss or expense of bad debts when it prices its product or service.

In this respect bad debts are strictly similar to other expenses, all of which normally must be covered by the sales revenue if the business is to continue.

If we could disregard the necessity of determining net income by accounting periods, there would be no special accounting problem in the bad debts situation. Each account determined to be uncollectable would be written off by the simple entry: 

Bad Debts Expense Accounts Receivable, Jones 

150 150 This simple technique (sometimes referred to as the specific, or direct, charge-off method) results frequently, however, in charging the bad debt to the expense account of a period later than the one which received the benefit of the related sale, and thus distorts the net income of both periods. Furthermore, the accounts receivable balance in the statement of condition always would be overstated by the amount of the uncollectable accounts lurking undetected in it. If the sales volume and the bad debts percentage remained the same, the distortion of income in successive periods would balance out, but there would be no such automatic correction of the overvaluation of the accounts receivable balance in the statement of condition.

The Allowance for Bad Debts 

The use of an estimated allowance for bad debts corrects both the error in income and in the statement of condition, whether experience remains level or whether it changes. If the accountant carefully watches the shifting of underlying factors, he also can adjust the allowance to accommodate changing percentages of bad debts to sales, and thus correct the income for both factors which previously caused net income distortion. 

A small department store which handles its bad debts write-offs by the allowance, or reserve, method might show the following situation in its December 31 statement of condition. 

ASSETS Current Assets: 

Cash $12,600 

Accounts Receivable 

$48,700 

Less: Allowance for Bad Debts 

4,200 44,500 

Merchandise Inventory 

152,300 

Other Assets 

XX,XXX

The figures shown indicate that the business has legal claims against its customers for $48,700 but that in the accountant's judgment $4,200 of this amount may not be collected; therefore, the realistic value to be attached to the accounts receivable is the net of these two figures or $44,500. The $48,700 may be due from several thousand customers. The accountant does not know which of these accounts will be uncollectable, but his careful study of the company's experience and his judgment of present financial and credit conditions enable him to estimate the total that will be bad within a fairly narrow margin. This method of carrying the estimated minus element in a contra account permits the accounts receivable account to remain in direct control of the customers' subsidiary ledger.

As specific accounts are determined to be uncollectable, they are written off against the allowance for bad debts in entries similar to the following:

January 5 

Allowance for Bad Debts

Accounts Receivable, Jones 

32 32 The credit is posted both to the control account and to the Jones account in the customers' ledger.

Note that the Jones account may have originated in a sale at any time in the past year or even earlier. It is clearly not an expense of the month in which it is written off. By the use of the allowance method, the Jones account is in effect charged to expense in the month in which it originated. That month received the benefit of the revenue from the sale to Jones, and at the end of that month an allowance for its expected bad accounts was established by a debit to Bad Debts Expense and a credit to Allowance for Bad Debts based on the percentage of the month's sales. 

The total value of accounts written off in any month may vary a great deal depending on the diligence of the collections department, the legal department, the season of the year, and to some extent on random factors. The fact that the amounts written off vary from month to month, however, does not distort the net income for any month because the net income is affected by the estimate of bad debts for the month rather than by the write-offs themselves. If sales are level, month by month, the bad debts expense will be level; if sales are predominantly seasonal, the bad

debts expense based on them may be expected to follow the seasonal trend. 

Assume that in January the additional accounts written off as bad amounted to $395. The following entry summarizes these write-offs:

Judging the Adequacy of the Allowance The best approach to the difficult problem of judging the adequacy of the balance in the Allowance for Bad Debts account is known as aging the accounts receivable. This technique is based on the assumption that the older an account is the more likely it is to be finally uncollectable. The usual method of procedure is demonstrated by the tabulation of Figure 28. 

This tabulation uses 30-day, age intervals up to 120 days and gathers together all accounts older than 120 days. Note that some accounts are contained entirely in one column and that others are broken down into more than one column. This analysis obviously requires a close inspection of the accounts and is a fairly arduous task, although machine-accounting systems can simplify it somewhat.

The absolute dollar totals of the columns are made more meaningful when they are reduced to percentages, and they are still more meaningful when the percentages of comparable earlier periods also are shown. The comparative percentages in Figure 28 indicate that the situation shows improvement over that of a month ago since the percentages in the first two columns containing the newer, younger accounts are now relatively larger. However, the situation is not, on the whole, as good as it was a year ago when 82% of all the accounts were in the three oldest age groups as against the condition in this period with 77.8% and 7.2% in those groups.

The aging of the accounts receivable with the comparative analysis of percentages provides a clue as to whether the situation is improving or deteriorating, but it does not of itself test the adequacy of the allowance for bad debts at the particular date. 

Such a judgment requires a considerable amount of additional data regarding the bad debt experience of the business. From his

It would indicate that the $4,058 balance in the allowance account is more than adequate. Generally the accountant and businessman prefer to be conservative in matters of this kind, and therefore, they would permit the allowance to remain at the present amount. 

If the accountant made a similar calculation in succeeding periods and if he found the allowance was becoming higher and higher in relation to the calculated figure, it probably would indicate that the 3/4 of 1% of sales used to calculate the monthly allowance was too high. He might conclude that the experience of the company was improving as a result of more effective credit and collection procedures or as a result of changing economic conditions. He would then probably change the percentage applied to sales to ½ of 1% for 6 or 8 months and again watch the results period by period as he had done previously.

Basic Study of Bad Debt Experience 

The basic data regarding the bad debts should be studied most carefully when a firm converts from the direct write-off basis to the allowance basis. Such a firm presumably would have a file of uncollectable accounts receivable which have been written off in

the past. These old accounts may be filed alphabetically, they may be filed by the years in which they were written off, or in still other ways. In any case these old accounts are the raw material for the study.

All accounts which have been written off must be analyzed and recapitulated by the year of their origin. It is important to realize that this recapitulation is not by the year in which the accounts were written off, but by the year of origin. The mechanics of this task probably would be handled by setting up a multicolumnar sheet with columns for each year of origin within the period available for study. Figure 29 illustrates such a form. 

The totals of the columns divided by the sales on account for each year give the percentage of accounts of that year which eventually had to be written off as uncollectable. In Figure 29 this percentage dropped rather rapidly in the first four years of the study and leveled out in the fifth year at almost the same percentage as the fourth year. This study indicates that the firm has improved its credit and collection procedures and that its loss experience is now a more or less steady ¾4 of 1% of sales. If this study is made at the end of 19X6, the figures for 19X6 would not yet be available. However unless there is some special reason to expect a change it would be reasonable, on the basis of the experience shown, to adopt 3/4 of 1% of sales on account as the percentage to be applied to 19X6 and later years.

Figure 29 reports the number of accounts involved in each year's write-off. Observe that this number decreased steadily until the fifth year, even though the sales volume was increasing. By dividing this figure into the amount written off for each year, we obtain the average size of the accounts written off. 

With a little more work in the original tabulation, we could have recorded the age of each account at the time it was written off. The average age of the accounts at time of write-off for each year might be a significant indication of changing collection procedures and therefore worth the effort involved in obtaining it. 

The average time for write-off also may be significant when judging the adequacy of the allowance for bad debts at any particular time. All other factors being equal, the allowance must be larger of course for a longer write-off period than it need be for a shorter one.

NOTES RECEIVABLE 

Although notes and drafts are approached here as receivables much of the discussion is also significant from the viewpoint of the other interested party, that is the party to whom these documents are payables. Often it is instructive to visualize the accounting entries to be made on the books of both parties. 

In earlier problems, the entries have involved notes taken on account, given on account, or given to banks for loans. Usually, these problems have dealt only with the face of the note or with the interest payment in its simplest form. It now becomes necessary to examine the accounting for notes under certain variations, namely: (1) the form of the note, (2) the occasion for the note, and (3) the valuation basis of the note.

Interest- and Noninterest-Bearing Notes 

Time drafts originate in a manner different from promissory notes, but once they have been accepted, they are in effect identical with promissory notes. For this reason we can discuss both classes of instruments together as notes. 

Every promissory note contracts to pay a specific principal amount at a fixed or determinable future date. Some notes promise to pay an additional amount as interest. The interest provision usually is stated as an annual rate applicable to the principal of the note. Thus the total amount promised, that is the maturity value consists of principal plus interest in the case of the interest-bearing note.

There are various methods of calculating the interest, but they are all variations of the basic formula: 

Principal Rate X Time = Interest. 

Since the rate is expressed as a per cent per year, the time must be expressed in terms of fractions of a year. In some instances when the note runs for a certain number of months, the fraction may be in even twelfths of a year, but the formula is equally adaptable to situations requiring a more accurate fraction expressed in terms of days. Theoretically the fraction should use the exact number of days the loan runs as the numerator and 365 days as the denominator (366 in leap years). Banks commonly calculate interest by using

the exact number of days the loan runs as the numerator and 360 days as denominator. However, many calculations use other approximations to make the calculation easier. For instance the assumption is often allowed that all months have an even 30 days as well as that the year has 360 days. Except when the loans are for large amounts, the interest ascertained by such approximate calculations is not substantially different from the amounts ascertained by the most exact method. The degree of accuracy to be employed should be determined always by consultation between the accountant and the employer. The accountant might well set up some sample calculations to compare the results of accurate with those of approximate calculations as the basis for such consultation. 

The fact that some notes are formally noninterest-bearing does not indicate that the loan is really a free loan but rather that the borrower is receiving less cash or credit than the face amount of the note. In fact, the discount rate used in the calculation usually applied to the amount on the face of the note, which amount is also is its maturity value, with the result that an interest cost to borrower in reality is higher than if the same rate had been the applied as the stated rate in an interest-bearing note.

En ries for Recording Simple Loans 

The following notes were received by the Smith Company on account from customers. The Jones note bears interest at 4% a year. The Hansen note is noninterest-bearing but was accepted by

When the notes are due and the cash is collected, Smith Company makes the following entries: 

September 8, 19XX Cash 5,033.33 

Notes Receivable (Jones) Interest Income 

5,000.00 33.33 

September 8, 19XX Cash 5,000.00

Notes Receivable (Hansen) 

5,000.00

The interest was calculated with the exact number of days (60) over 360 days as the denominator. If the notes had been for two months the fraction would have been 62/360, since both July and August have thirty-one days and the due date, two months after July 10, would have been September 10. Note that on the interest-bearing Jones note, the interest was recognized at the time the note was collected, whereas on the noninterest-bearing

Hansen note, the interest was recognized at the time the note was received. This pattern of entries results from the custom of always showing the face or principal amount of the note in the notes receivable account.

Note also that, although the amount of the interest was $33.33 in each case, this amount was earned in the case of the Jones note when credit of an even $5,000 was extended for 60 days, whereas in the case of the Hansen note the credit extended was only $4,966.67. Thus the loan on the noninterest-bearing note which was calculated on a 4% discount basis earned a higher rate of return than did the loan on the interest-bearing note. The student should visualize the corresponding entries to Accounts Payable, Notes Payable, and Interest Expense as they would appear on the books of Jones and of Hansen. He should realize that from Hansen's viewpoint the 4% discount cost was slightly higher than a true 

4% interest.

Discounting Notes Receivable 

Frequently, a creditor owning customers' notes finds it necessary to convert them into cash before they are due by turning them over to a bank. This process is known as discounting the notes rather than selling them mainly to recognize the contingent liability which the business incurs under the Negotiable Instruments Act when it endorses the notes. This indorsement not only guarantees the validity of the note but also guarantees its payment. 

The transaction is thus not properly described as a sale of the notes, even though in fact it actually does contain most of the elements of a sale.

The most interesting problem concerning the discounting of a note receivable is the amount of money to be received. This amount is determined by the rate of return which the bank is to receive and by the length of time remaining to the maturity of the note.

The usual method of calculating the value at which the bank will discount a note is to apply a discount rate to the maturity value of the note for the number of days which the note still has to run, that is, its discount period. If the bank used this method with a 4% discount rate, the calculation of the maturity value, the discount, and the "proceeds" for the Jones note on July 25 (45

days after the signing of the note) would have been made by the bank in the following manner: 

(1) To calculate the maturity value: 

60 $5,000.00+ (5,000.00 × .04 × -)=$5,033.33 360 (2) To calculate the discount: 45 $5,033.33 .04 X = $25.17 360 (3) To calculate the value on July 25 or the "proceeds" $5,033.3325.17 $5,008.16 The entry to be made on Smith Company books to reflect the value on July 25 would be: Cash

Notes Receivable Discounted (Jones) 

Interest Income 5,008.16 5,000.00 8.16

The Smith Company, of course, is realizing a little less than the the 4% it expected to get on the Jones note. Some theorists would say that the Smith Company had realized the 4% and had paid out 4% discount. The two interpretations are not far enough apart to warrant discussion. 

The credit in the above entry is made to Notes Receivable Discounted rather than to the Notes Receivable account as a way of recognizing the contingent liability on the discounted note. Notes 

Receivable Discounted is a contra account to Notes Receivable and is deducted from that account in the statement of condition. 

When the due date of the Jones note, September 8, has passed, and when Jones has paid his note at the bank, the contingent liability is relieved and the Smith Company makes the entry to record this relief as follows: 

Notes Receivable Discounted (Jones) Notes Receivable (Jones). 

... 5,000.00 5,000.00

Of course the bank's discount rate might be considerably higher than the interest rate on the note, if so, the net interest earned by the Smith Company would be lower still. If the interest rate on the note were 6% and the bank's discount rate 4% the same type of calculations used for the Jones note would produce an effective

rate of interest to the Smith Company of more than 6% for the period during which it held the note. 

In working problems or in handling actual transactions in this area, one must read the conditions of the problem or interpret the intentions of the parties very, very carefully. When the conditions or intentions are observed with care, there is no particular mystery or difficulty in the situation.

The calculation of the discounted value or proceeds for a noninterest-bearing note is the same as that for the interest-bearing note illustrated previously, in three steps except that step one, the calculation of the maturity value, is unnecessary since the face of the note shows the maturity value. The discount rate used by the bank bears no necessary relationship to the rate used in the original discounting operation.

Dishonored Notes Receivable 

If a note discounted at the bank is dishonored by the original promissor when presented by the bank, the indorser (the Smith Company in our illustration) must pay the full maturity value of the note because of its indorsement. The full amount paid, of course, becomes a claim against the original debtor. On September 

8, the following entry is made if Jones refuses to pay his note and 

Smith Company pays it for him:

Dishonored Notes Receivable (Jones) 

Cash 

Notes Receivable Discounted (Jones) Notes Receivable (Jones) . 

5,033.33 5,033.33 5,000.00 5,000.00

The $5,000.00 journal entry records the cancelation of the contingent liability that no longer exists against the original asset (Notes Receivable) because the note now has been converted into 

Dishonored Notes Receivable. 

The Smith Company should collect the amount, $5,033.33, due from Jones, with interest at the full legal rate. If $5,033.33 is collected six months later with interest at the rate of 6% per annum, the entry would be as follows:

Cash 

Dishonored Notes Receivable (Jones). 

Interest Income 

5,184.33 5,033.33 151.00

If it should prove impossible for the Smith Company to collect, this claim would be written off as any other bad debt. Assuming the company used an Allowance for Bad Debts, the entry would be:

Allowance for Bad Debts Dishonored Notes Receivable (Jones) 

5,033.33 5,033.33 

The charge against the allowance is only proper when, as in this case, the original note grew out of an account receivable that would have been a proper charge to the allowance had it proved uncollectable. Purists might argue that it would be better practice to charge the $33.33 against interest income or against some special loss account since the interest was not a part of the original account receivable for which the allowance was set up. 

Because the accounts receivable ledger often is used as a source of credit information, many accountants prefer to close the dishonored note receivable to the customer's account from which it originated and then to write it off as a bad account from that source. This procedure would eliminate the Dishonored Notes Receivable account but care would be required to safeguard the relationship between the accounts receivable control account and its subsidiary ledger. This variation of running the dishonored note through the account receivable does not affect the entry to close the Notes Receivable Discounted account and the Notes Receivable account to express the disappearance of the contingent liability.

Any company that has more than a very few notes receivable probably will prefer to establish a formal register in which to record the details of each note received. The Notes Receivable

Since the facts about each note are most clearly in mind at the time the note is received, the total interest and the interest per month should be calculated, checked, and extended across the register at that time. At the end of each month, the totals of the monthly interest columns would provide the total interest and discount earned on the notes receivable during the period.

Some notes receivable registers omit this feature of interest accrual but do show all the other data included in Figure 30. 

Either form of register can be considered a subsidiary ledger controlled by the notes receivable account in the general ledger. 

Each line of the register is in effect an account in the subsidiary notes receivable ledger. At any time, the balance of the notes receivable control account should be reconcilable with the total of the face amounts of notes still held as shown on the register. 

Notes Payable Register 

A register similar to the notes receivable register can be set up to show the details of the notes payable when a company has numerous notes payable outstanding. The calculation of the interest accrued payable month by month would be handled and exhibited in the same way that the interest earned is exhibited in Figure 30. 

Interest Income Accrued and Deferred

The general pattern of interest entries on interest-bearing and noninterest-bearing notes should throw light on the necessity for making adjustments if the term of the note extends beyond the end of an accounting period. In the case of the noninterest-bearing note, the interest income for the full amount of the prepaid interest is credited at the time the note is received. In this case if the end of the accounting period comes, or if interim statements are to be drawn up before the full term of the note has run, there is an element of unearned interest in the interest income account that must be removed to a deferred interest income account among the current liabilities in preparation for the closing entries. The note is shown at its face value among the current assets, and the deferred interest income is shown among the current liabilities. 

It might be more correct from a theoretic standpoint to show deferred interest income as a deduction from the face value of the note thus producing a net asset value closer to the actual present value of the note. If the amounts involved are very large, this more correct showing might be of practical significance, but it is seldom used in actual practice.

In the case of the interest-bearing note receivable, the need for accruing interest to the date of closing or of drawing up interim

statements should be quite clear. The note is earning interest every day and the proper recognition of this fact in the statements provides an asset element, interest accrued receivable among the current assets in the statement of condition, and additional interest in the income statement. 

Generally speaking, the treatment outlined for notes receivable would be used in a manufacturing or merchandising business in which the notes are a more or less incidental part of the business. 

A commercial credit company or a bank, in which the ownership of notes becomes a major aspect of the business, might well wish to treat these assets and their income in a different pattern. For instance, it is not uncommon for a bank to accrue interest daily on the total amount of interest-bearing notes receivable. If this routine is the daily pattern, no special entry would be made at the end of the period since the interest accrued receivable and the interest income are kept up to date each day. In this case at the time the note is collected the credit for interest received is made to the account, Interest Accrued Receivable.

The parallel procedure for noninterest-bearing notes receivable would be to credit the deferred (or unearned) interest income account for the full amount of the discount at the time the note is received. This deferred (or unearned) interest income then would be debited on a daily basis, with the credit going to interest income. This procedure again puts the earnings on a day-to-day basis and the need for any special end-of-the-period adjustment is obviated.

INVESTMENTS 

Many investments in stocks and bonds are highly liquid since they may be readily sold on the open market. Such liquidity, however, does not mean necessarily that they should be treated as current assets. If these securities are held in pension funds or in sinking funds or are held for other long term purposes, it would be improper to treat them as current in the statement of condition. 

Under these conditions, their liquidity or cashability is of no particular consequence. 

This chapter is concerned primarily with accounting for highly liquid assets. However, since the problems involved in the valua-

tion of and in accounting for investments are similar for both short and long term investments, the latter are also discussed. 

Investments in the Statement of Condition 

Note that some statements of condition may be prepared with three major asset headings as follows: 

Assets Current Assets: 

Fixed (or Plant) Assets: 

This arrangement seem to imply that the accountant was not quite sure whether the investments were current or fixed, and doubtless such confusion is sometimes present. Unless the detailed titles under the heading, Investments, are clear, this arrangement may leave considerable doubt in the reader's mind as to what the total current assets really are.

A clearer alternative is to show the Investments as the third division as follows: 

Assets Current Assets: 

Fixed (or Plant) Assets: 

When this form is used, any investments that actually are liquid and are intended to be used as liquid assets should be shown under the current assets heading. Then the formal heading, Investments, would be reserved for such long term items as pension and sinking funds, stocks owned for the control of subsidiaries, and for similar purposes not related closely to actual current operations. The detailed titles within the Investments section must be specific concerning their real content.

It is not the function of this book to set up arbitrary rules in controversial areas, such as the form of the statement of condition. 

It is important, however, that the student visualize some of the practical problems confronting accountants upon which they do not agree completely.

Accounting for Investments 

Generally speaking, stocks, bonds, mortgages, and other investments are accounted for at cost, although certain exceptions are

noted subsequently. Cost is interpreted to mean the amount paid to the previous owner plus all normal and necessary incidental costs involved, such as brokerage fees, transfer taxes, and so forth. 

Subsequent assessments by the issuing corporation properly required to be paid on stock owned should be added to the investment account as additional cost.

If very few investments are owned, a separate account in the general ledger may be set up for each. If there are many investments, it is necessary to establish one or more control accounts for them in the general ledger with detailed accounts for each investment carried in a subsidiary ledger. 

Interest or dividends received on investments are recognized normally as income when received, although of course, interest as accrued may be shown as income and dividends declared may be shown as receivables with a credit to income on the record date. 

Deviations from these simple entries for investments will be considered in the following sections dealing with long term investments in bonds.

Long Term Investments in Bonds 

In Chapter VI, Other Corporation Problems, we studied the phenomenon of amortization of premium and discount on outstanding bonds. From the viewpoint of the long time investor in these bonds, the amortization of discount and premium is frequently disregarded entirely, although theoretically it is as significant to the investor as it is to the issuing corporation. Whereas the issuing corporation usually shows the principal, or the face value, of the bonds in the Bonds Outstanding account with a contra account for the discount on bonds or an adjunct account for the premium, the investor, if he recognizes amortization at all, usually shows the carrying value in the investment account itself and makes the amortization entries directly by adding to or subtracting from that account. Here again, the simple straight-line method of amortization generally is considered adequate, unless the amounts involved are very large or unless greater accuracy is required for other reasons. In such cases the actuarial compound interest method should be used. Thus as a general rule, long term investments in bonds should be valued at cost plus or minus the amortization of discount or premium.

Long Term Investments-Controlling Interest in Stocks 

The carrying of long term stock investments at cost and the recognition of dividends as income when received may be thought of as expressing the legal relationship between the owner and the corporation. When the investor, whether he is an individual or a corporation, holds a controlling interest in the stocks of a corporation, he may find it desirable to go beyond this legal relationship to show more completely his entire economic situation with respect to his investment. He reflects his situation by carrying the investment account at cost plus his proportionate share of the income of the corporation or minus his proportionate share of its net loss and dividends received. Since he has control of the corporation (that is, he owns over half of its stock) he can in general control the corporation's dividend policy and thus convert his share of its earnings into cash at will. He then is placed in a position quite different with regard to the earnings of the corporation from that of an investor who owns only a small percentage of the stock. This reason is considered sufficient justification for the difference in the basis on which he carries his investment. 

To illustrate: Suppose that on January 1, 19X1 Jones buys 90% of the stock of the X Corporation for $128,000 at a time when the corporation's statement of condition shows a stockholders' equity comprising $100,000 of $100 par capital stock and $30,000 of retained earnings. Jones would make the following entry to record his purchase:

Investment, X Corporation 

Cash

To record the purchase of 900 shares $100 par 128,000 128,000

Note that he has paid $128,000 for 90% of the stockholders' total equity, the book value of which is $117,000. If he has made this purchase intelligently, we may assume that some of the corporation's assets-probably including its goodwill-are undervalued on its books so that the price paid correctly reflects the value of the stock purchased. When the corporation subsequently shows income flowing into the retained earnings (surplus) account, it is logical to visualize Jones' investment account increasing correspondingly. 

If the corporation reports net earnings of $40,000 for the year,

Jones will recognize his 90% share of the earnings by the following entry: 

Investment, X Corporation 

36,000 

Income on Investments 36,000

If the corporation reports a net loss of $20,000, Jones will recognize his 90% share by the entry: 

Loss on Investments

Investment, X Corporation 

18,000 18,000 If, after the recognition of the profits, the corporation pays a dividend, Jones will have to record the dividend as a reduction of his investment. A 10% dividend paid by the corporation on its $100,000 stock would result in a $9,000 dividend check to Jones, for which he would make the following entry:

Cash 

Investment, X Corporation 

9,000 9,000

There are, of course, many ramifications in accounting for stock ownership which are too complicated to be considered in this text. 

The purchases and sales of successive lots of the same stock, the receipt of stock dividends or stock rights, and stock splits, all require quite intricate accounting, especially when the need to consider the income tax implications is taken into account. 

Market Price and Long Term Investments

The methods of valuation for long term investments in stocks and bonds discussed previously are all basically cost methods. It should be quite apparent that, in general, no good purpose would be served by recognizing strictly temporary market fluctuations in the case of these long term investments. The accountant, however, is not unaware of the fact that the market prices of these securities may be of some interest or importance, especially when they are considerably above or below the cost basis. To recognize the market price in these cases without formally breaking the accounting rules outlined, the accountant often finds it desirable to add a properly referenced footnote to the statement of condition showing the market valuation as of that date.

In addition, most accountants would agree that, when there has been a very great drop in market valuation under conditions that indicate little hope of recovery, the loss should be formally recog-

nized by crediting the asset and charging the loss to a properly labeled account. If stock is owned in the corporation which has suffered bankruptcy, it would be proper to recognize the fact. If the corporation has been reorganized with the result that the value of its stocks have been scaled down formally, it would certainly be desirable to reflect this fact on the books of the investor. 

Securities Owned as Current Assets 

Short term investments in stocks and bonds, properly classified as current assets, may be encountered under two general situations. 

On one hand, a dealer in securities to whom the investments are in effect merchandise would regard them as current assets. On the other hand, investments made in stocks or bonds as a temporary disposition of cash would be a fully legitimate current asset. 

In both of these cases, cost is probably the most acceptable basis for valuation. However a great many accountants prefer to be conservative; they would value these short term investments at the lower of cost or market. The controversy over cost versus cost or market as applied to current investments parallels the controversy regarding the valuation of merchandise inventories discussed in 

Chapter VIII. 

The special problems regarding the amortization of premium and discount on bonds, the reflection of a controlling interest in stock, and so forth, which had to be considered in connection with long term investments, need not be of concern when stocks and bonds are owned as temporary investments. The amortization on bonds and the changing fortunes of the corporation whose stock is owned will be reflected adequately in the profit or loss recognized when the temporary investment is converted into cash. If a bond. is purchased at a discount, held for three months and then sold for slightly more than it cost, the increase, or profit, is the result of theoretic amortization of discount plus or minus some actual change in the market price. The net change could be separated into its elements, but no good purpose would be served to justify the work involved. Similarly if a stock investment is held for a few months, the gain or loss on it may be partly a reflection of the earnings or loss of the issuing corporation during that period and partly the result of other market factors. In this case analysis would be virtually impossible as well as purposeless.

Interest Accrued at the Time of Purchase or Sale 

One further detail requires study in connection with the purchase and sale of bonds between interest payment dates. In determining the total amount paid for a bond the owner must take into consideration the interest accrued on it at the date of sale. If the accrued interest is not isolated properly at both the purchase and sale dates, the profit or loss on the temporary investment would be reflected erroneously.

Assume that a $10,000, 5% bond with quarterly interest payment dates on January 31, April 30, July 31, and October 31 is purchased on January 19 for $10,811.11 and sold on May 5 for $10,756.94. Quarterly interest of $125 is received on January 31 and April 30. If no attention is given to the interest accrued either at the time of purchase or at the time of sale the entries for this investment would be:

January 19 Investment in Bonds 

10,811.11 Cash 

10,811.11

January 31 

Cash 125.00 

Interest Income 

125.00 

April 30 Cash 

125.00 

Interest Income 

125.00 May 5 Cash 10,756.94

Loss on Sale of Bonds 

54.17 

Investment in Bonds 

10,811.11

Note that these entries show interest income of $250.00 and a loss of $54.17 on the sale of the bond. If the loss is noticed and the interest forgotten, the conclusion might easily be drawn that the best judgment was not used in selecting the investment. 

If these same facts are properly accounted for, the following entries must be made:

Investment in Bonds Interest Accrued on Bonds 

Cash 

January 19 

10,700.00 111.11 10,811.11 (Interest accrued on $10,000 bond at 

5% for 80 days)

Cash January 31 125.00 111.111 13.89 

Interest Accrued on Bonds Interest Income 

April 30 Cash 125.00 

Interest Income 

125.00 May 5 

Cash 

10,756.94

Interest Income 

6.94 

Profit on Sale of Bond 50.00 10,700.00 

Investment in Bonds 

(Interest accrued on $10,000 bond at 

5% for 5 days)

This format for entries results in showing $145.83 interest income for the period the bond was owned and a profit of $50.00 on the sale of the bond. The interest income is the amount earned on a $10,000 bond at 5% for the three and one-half months (approximately) that the bond was owned; the $50.00 profit reflects correctly the judgment or luck or both of the person responsible for the particular investment. These figures interpret the situation much more satisfactorily from the management viewpoint since they can be readily grasped.

CHAPTER VIII INVENTORIES 

THE TERM inventories as used by accountants embraces goods held for sale by merchants, raw materials, goods in process and finished goods of manufacturing industries, and supplies of various kinds owned by any business. In general the factors involved and the rules for the evaluation of items in any of these categories are similar. The term inventories generally does not include plant assets, investments, receivables, or cash. Occasionally, it may be used in the broader sense in which it covers the entire concept of assets. Some years ago, for instance, the United States Navy "took an inventory of all of its plant assets." This is the broad use of the term. In this chapter, we adhere to the more commonly used narrower concept of inventories.

Cost as the Basis of Inventory Valuation 

Basically, goods and supplies are considered to be worth what they cost the owner, although this basic concept is amended in various ways to reflect special circumstances. 

Cost must be interpreted to mean not only the price paid to the previous owner but also all necessary and normal outlays incidental to the purchase. These outlays may include such costs as freight, cartage, import duties, special packing or unpacking, and assembly. The phrase necessary and normal, properly interpreted, is an effective guide to use when unusual circumstances arise and unusual items have to be handled. If purchasing officials spend wastefully in the process of acquiring merchandise, or if certain unusual circumstances cause incidental expenditures that are rarely required, such purchases or incidental expenditures should be considered unnecessary or not normal or both and should be charged as losses rather than as inventory costs. 

This problem of the inclusion of incidental costs obviously arises at the time of purchase but it is no less a problem at any time when goods are being inventoried. The proper application 201

INVENTORIES 

of freight and other incidental costs to the inventory often is handled negligently in the typical small business. 

Inventories and the Accounting Period 

The characteristic problems in this area, like the problems of income accrual, are caused by the necessity of breaking the history of a business into time segments. If the time segments were twenty or twenty-five years in length a mere comparison of purchases and sales might present a fair picture of the success of the business. 

But when management requires an appraisal of its success on an annual or even a monthly basis, the value of goods and supplies on hand at the beginning and at the end of each period becomes significant. 

Two conditions of inventory need to be differentiated. In largeunit merchandising the record of merchandise on hand can be currently recorded. This method is known as the perpetual inventory method. Also kept currently are the inventories of many manufacturing concerns where all changes in the cost of raw materials, goods in process, and finished goods are continuously reflected in the accounts. In the typical merchandising operation where it is not feasible for the accounts to follow the merchandise on hand currently, the problem becomes one of taking a physical inventory periodically. In this situation, the inventory problem is seen most clearly, although the basic factors of inventory valuation are present under both sets of conditions. 

Physical Inventory Methods 

The problem of taking the physical inventory is to establish such routines as will assure completeness and accuracy of the actual count. If any merchandise or other materials are either overlooked or are counted twice, all the careful accuracy of subsequent steps cannot produce an inventory total that is meaningful to management. Some inventories can be taken in one big effort after the close of business on the last day of the year; some, especially in manufacturing enterprises, require the closing of the business for a week or more. It is also fairly common to take inventory over a period of several days, or a week, while the business is in full operation. This kind of inventory is accomplished by attaching to every item a special inventory tag upon which

INVENTORIES 

203 employees record all ins and outs during the taking of the inventory. Then, when the actual count is made, the quantity on hand as of the exact inventory date may be calculated. Although this technique involves careful preparation and training of employees, it does permit the taking of inventory without the expensive shutdown, which might be necessary otherwise. 

The exact format of inventory sheets may vary greatly, but it should provide, with respect to each item, for the unit (such as pounds, tons, ounces, dozens, or dollars), the description in sufficient detail for certain identification (including size, style, and color), the number counted, and one or more columns for unit price and extension. In some cases it may be desirable to fix responsibility by requiring the initials of the inventory taker on each line; in other cases it may be sufficient to require only the initialing of each sheet. Each sheet should be headed to indicate the general area or category of items covered. The sheets should be numbered in advance so that no whole sheets will be omitted in the final summary.

The taking of a physical inventory of goods in process in a manufacturing business, with goods in many different stages of completion, may be extremely difficult. In some cases, actual physical inspection may enable the inventory taker to determine the amount and value of the raw material which has gone into these partially completed goods. In other cases, this type of evaluation is impossible; even if the raw material content may be determined by physical inspection, the labor and factory costs that have been applied rarely can be so determined. Practically speaking, the taking of a physical inventory of goods in process is a misnomer. 

Usually the most valid way of arriving at the amount and value of goods in process is to accept the cost accountant's bookkeeping figures for the costs involved. If the inventory taker understands accounting and can discover flaws in the cost accounting techniques used to develop the book figures, he might be justified in challenging these figures. He should be able to trace the methods of pricing used in charging raw materials to goods in process. If he discovers that the methods used do not agree with the methods that are supposed to control the inventory pricing (such as first-in, first-out; most recent cost; or cost or market, whichever is lower), then the cost accounting figures should be adjusted. A study of the

INVENTORIES 

intricacies of cost accounting is required before this problem can be discussed fully.

Likewise the valuation of the inventory of finished goods in a manufacturing establishment depends largely on figures that have been accumulated by the cost accountant. Possible adjustments in these figures would follow the same procedure as in the case of the valuation of goods in process.

Pricing the Inventory 

The subject of the pricing of inventories bears an important relationship to the recognition of income. A major controversy in accounting rages around this topic. An overstatement of ending inventory increases the net income in the period just closing and automatically decreases the net income of the following period. 

This results from the formula for cost of goods sold, namely: 

Beginning Inventory + Purchases - Ending Inventory = Cost of Goods Sold 

Thus, although the net income is not increased or decreased in the absolute sense, it is shifted as between periods. This increase or decrease of net income may be very important in terms of possible impact on management policy, taxes, and dividends. 

In inventory discussions much emphasis usually is placed on the relative conservatism of the several methods. In this context conservatism means two things: (1) the showing of lower incomes and (2) the more modest showing of inventory value in the statement of condition.

In general, bankers favor such conservatism on the part of borrowers as being basic to the protection of loans made by the banks. The businessman with a conservative attitude is less likely to indulge in careless expenditures, unwise withdrawals in the form of partners' drawings or corporate dividends, and unjustified and often dangerous plans for expansion. 

In auditing the business records of borrowers or prospective borrowers, certified public accountants are aware of the interest of the banks and can be relied on to insist upon proper conservatism in inventory valuation as well as in other matters. 

In the absence of an opportunity for close verification of inventory values, the gross profit method is sometimes useful as a check. 

In most businesses, the percentage of gross profits to sales is fairly

INVENTORIES 

205 stable year after year. in the gross profit percentage often indicates an overvalued ending merchandise inventory and is the signal for further checking of the inventory.

Under such circumstances a sudden increase Under comparatively simple merchandising conditions, the pricing problem usually is to decide whether to use the straight cost basis or the basis of cost or market, whichever is the lower basis. The straight cost basis certainly is the easier to use, but the majority of merchandising businesses use the cost or market basis. 

The practical argument in favor of the cost or market basis has been stated effectively:

Of the two bases mentioned, the second (cost or market) is generally considered preferable to cost, on the theory that it is more conservative, that any loss because of market decline is thereby recognized in the period in which such decline occurred, and that the showing of the inventory at the lower value enables the management to face the next period with merchandise carried at a price comparable to that which can be acquired by competitors, thus insuring a clearer and more practicable sales policy.¹

The newcomer in accounting often objects to the cost or market, whichever is the lower, rule. He objects to its inconsistency. "If market is important when prices drop why should we not recognize market when prices go up?" he asks. The best answer to this question is that our cost or market rule is inconsistent, but that consistency is not our only criterion. Emerson once wrote that foolish consistency is the hobgoblin of little minds. In accounting, this quotation is aptly illustrated by our present quandary. It is even possible that management might be badly misled by the consistent use of market price, whereas the inconsistent cost or market price generally leads to intelligent decisions that are safe. 

The implementation of the cost or market, whichever is the lower, rule admits a certain latitude of interpretation in actual practice. The most conservative application of the rule consists of examining the cost and market prices for each item in the inventory and to use the lower price for each item. The next most conservative technique is to examine the cost and market prices for each group, or category of items, and to use the lower total for each group. The final interpretation is to price all the items at 

1 Inghram, Howell A., Accounting II, p. 228. American Bankers Association, New 

York, 1947

INVENTORIES 

cost and at market and then to take the lower of the two grand totals as the amount to be used for accounting purposes. 

Whichever interpretation is used, it is very desirable to place the facts with regard to the inventory situation clearly before management. The manner of presenting this important difference in the income statement is discussed subsequently in this chapter. 

THE MEANING OF COST AS APPLIED TO INVENTORY 

It has been pointed out that cost should include necessary and normal incidental costs. Beyond this, the principal problem is how to reflect costs when the quantity of any item on hand represents purchases made at various times and at different prices. The true cost of inventory would of course require that the particular items on hand be identified with their own actual purchase prices. Where this can be done it is known as the specific identification method. 

Except for large-unit items, such as pianos or threshing machines, however, this method is usually not feasible, and some approximation must be used. The price at which the most recent lot was purchased may be used. If the most recent purchase was made near the end of the period, and if prices have been falling, this interpretation of cost approximates the lower of cost or market. 

If, however, prices have been rising, this latest price method produces a nonconservative valuation that is higher than the actual cost for some of the units in the inventory. 

First-In, First-Out Method 

Another interpretation of cost is based on a series of prices, pricing a quantity equal to the most recent purchase at the most recent price, a quantity equal to the next most recent purchase at the next most recent price, and so forth until the actual quantity on hand is covered. This method is sometimes described as going back on the invoices. Practically, it involves an examination of the invoices for recent purchases to check the quantities and prices. It is a tedious method to apply, but it is generally considered the best possible interpretation of cost. It should be noted that this method operates on the assumption that the units on hand are those units which were most recently acquired, although actually they may not be. This method of pricing is called first-in, first-out

INVENTORIES 

207 cost. The name is often abbreviated, and the method is referred to as fifo cost.

THE MEANING OF MARKET PRICE 

Market price, in connection with the pricing of inventory, means the price at which the goods in the inventory could be repurchased at the inventory date. The repurchase price must be interpreted to mean the price of the customary quantities from the normal sources of supply. It would be unreasonable, for instance, to apply carload lot prices as quoted in California to a retail store in Detroit, which usually buys in case lots from local wholesalers. In addition, the market price must include the normal and necessary incidentals at their market prices as part of the market price to be used in valuing the inventory.

The application of this concept of market price is sometimes easy and sometimes quite difficult. Market prices for many standard items can be read directly from the current price lists of the normal suppliers. Other items may have been obtained from suppliers on the basis of competitive bids. It may be virtually impossible to ascertain the replacement cost of such items. In these cases the cost would have to be accepted unless the general price movements in the category gave evidence that the item was available at a lower market price. Frequently the only practicable guide to market price is the price of some actual purchase shortly before or after the inventory date.

For either inventory cost or market price, the method of applying the incidental costs varies according to circumstances. Consider one case in which the incidental cost element is very significant. 

For instance, in the case of freight charges on coal or iron ore, the freight should be figured as a specific element for each classification of the coal or ore in the inventory. If freight rates have changed since the actual purchase, the new freight rate as of the inventory date should be considered in determining the market price inventory. The new freight rate is the rate that would be effective if the coal or ore were to be replaced at the inventory date.

For most other merchandise inventories (either cost or market), however, the freight element is proportionately much less significant. Usually, instead of determining the actual freight on each

INVENTORIES 

item it is satisfactory to add an amount for freight to the total inventory on a percentage basis for both the cost and cost or market inventories.

Perpetual (or Book) Inventories 

Manufacturing firms commonly find it advisable to establish detailed accounts to maintain a continuing record of the quantity and value of every item of raw materials. In effect this involves the creation of an account for each of the thousands of items of raw materials. These accounts constitute a subsidiary ledger that supports the control account, Raw Materials, appearing in the general ledger.

The format of these accounts varies greatly according to special circumstances and according to the mechanical bookkeeping equipment that is available. The forms employed in Figures 31 and 32 may be accepted as representative of the basic relations. The columns headed In, Out, and Balance also might have been headed Acquisitions, Requisitions, and On Hand, or, Purchases, Issues, and Balance on Hand.

The First-In, First-Out (or Fifo) Method of Pricing Requisitions The transactions shown in Figure 31 read as follows: On 

January 1 there were on hand 650 units of raw material #225.13 at $5 per unit for a total value of $3,250. On January 4, 100 units at $5 were issued in requisition #2,965 reducing the balance to 550 units valued at $2,750. 

The next issue, (on January 7, authorized by requisition #2,997 of 75 units valued at $375 reduced the quantity on hand to 475 units, which was below the minimum quantity shown in the heading. Apprized of this fact, the purchasing department ordered a replenishment which arrived on January 10. Invoice #592 for 

1,000 units at $6 each was entered causing the balance on hand to increase to 1,475 units valued at $8,375. It should be noted that while previous entries were all at the price of $5 per unit, this purchase entry introduces a new complication. The units now on hand are valued at two prices; the 475 units of the old balance are valued at $5 each whereas the 1,000 units of the latest purchase are valued at $6 each.

The next issue, requisition # 3,112, of 400 units is assumed to
