This technique for pricing the requisitions is based on the first-in, first-out concept. It corresponds to the pricing technique for first-in, first-out (or going back on invoices) previously mentioned as one of the methods available for pricing a physical inventory. 

The Moving-Average Method of Pricing Requisitions 

Figure 32 demonstrates another method of pricing requisitions which is often used in industry. The balance on hand on January 1 is at the price of $5 per unit. The first two requisitions are at this same price. The purchase also is handled as in Figure 31, but the method changes at this point. At the time of the purchase the price is recalculated by dividing the total value on hand by the total quantity on hand, and the new unit price is indicated in the upper right-hand corner of the inventory record. Subsequent issues are priced at this new average price until another purchase requires another change of issue price. This method bypasses using the split price requisitions which plague the first-in, first-out method. 

As an added advantage, the moving-average method lends itself effectively to record keeping by machines. 

Note that the moving-average method leaves a balance in the account. Thus it does not correspond exactly with any method of pricing previously discussed (under Physical Inventory Methods). 

If the quantities on hand are quite low when successive purchases are made, the moving-average balance on the books will be quite similar to the last invoice price concept of pricing at cost. If purchases are made while quantities on hand are still high, the price movement is more sluggish.

Perpetual Inventory at Standard Price 

To avoid the difficulties of pricing involved in the first-in, firstout and the moving-average methods, many manufacturing con-

INVENTORIES 

cerns carry their perpetual inventories of raw materials at standard prices. A standard price for each material is fixed at about the average estimated price which may be expected during the ensuing period. As materials are purchased they are debited to the detailed account at this standard price, accounts payable is credited for the actual cost, and the difference is debited or credited to an account with the title, Price Variation from Standard. Under this method the materials are always issued to production at the standard price, which greatly simplifies the numerous entries for issues of materials.

To correct the overall results in the cost of goods manufactured for purposes of determining profits at the end of the period, the balance in the Price Variation from Standard account is closed to 

Cost of Sales or directly to Expense and Revenue. 

When prices of raw materials show decided movements up or down, the standard prices are adjusted to reflect the actual prices. 

This adjustment involves debits or credits to the detailed raw material accounts, but it is still far less work than the other methods which require the making of many changes in prices. In addition, the standard price method has certain other advantages related to standard cost accounting which cannot be analyzed here. 

The Last-In, First-Out (or Lifo) Method 

Theoretically, it would be possible to price requisitions on a last-in, first-out basis, but practically, the difficulties of doing so (on the basis of a perpetual inventory of raw materials) are so great that it is seldom feasible. On the other hand, the last-in, first-out method frequently is applied when relatively few items of raw materials are of preponderant importance, such as the grains purchased by a milling company or the ore purchased by a steel mill. For these materials the record of purchase and use may be kept in terms of quantities only. At the end of the period, the value used and the ending inventory can be calculated on the lifo basis.

The lifo method of inventory has been favored during the long period since the depression of 1932-1934 in which all prices have been rising continually. Under the condition of more or less steady price increases, the lifo method charges the more recent, high-priced purchases to costs of the current period and leaves the

INVENTORIES 

213 earlier, low-priced purchases in inventory. Thus, the results are conservative and tend to have certain tax advantages. 

Physical Inventories To Check Book Inventories 

Since even the best of inventory systems are subject to error, it is desirable to take a physical inventory of quantities to check the book figures. Such an inventory need not be taken all at one time but may be spaced throughout the year. If shortages or overages are discovered, the book figures should be adjusted to correspond to the facts. The balancing entry (debit or credit) to be made will depend on whatever explanation for the discrepancy is accepted. For instance, although shortages of some valuable commodities might indicate theft, shortages of other commodities, the very nature of which rules out the likelihood of theft, might indicate errors in the requisitions.

The methods of valuing perpetual inventories up to this point are cost methods. If the cost or market basis is to be used for income determination, a study must be made either at the time of checking the physical inventory or at some other time, in order to correct the book valuations down to market and to ascertain the total loss to be shown in the income statement. This loss should be shown as a loss; it should not be confused with or accounted for as additional cost of materials consumed. 

In addition to the purpose of keeping the accounts right for income determination and for general management, the checking of the physical quantities may be highly necessary, at least for critical items, as a support for the system of reordering when minimum quantities are approached. It would be unfortunate for the business, if because the quantities on hand were overstated in error, the business failed to reorder and lacked some important raw material. Such a situation actually might cause the stoppage of production and result in the loss of a considerable sum of money. Critical items of raw materials may be defined as those items for which there are no substitutes and for which there are no ready sources of replenishment. Critical items should be flagged for special attention in the raw materials ledger and should be physically inventoried frequently, possibly once a month, even though the general raw material is checked only annually. 

In some instances detailed record keeping by items is not

INVENTORIES 

feasible. A degree of control can be achieved by dividing the total inventory into significant sections, along commodity lines or by storage units, and by maintaining continuous dollar value accounts for each section. This kind of control would not be a safeguard against shortages of individual items, but it might aid management to visualize and control its inventory policy. 

Inventory Loss and the Income Statement 

When the inventory is carried on a perpetual book basis, inventory loss caused by the drop from cost to the lower of cost or market is recognized by specific entries crediting the raw material account and debiting a loss account.

When the inventory is on a periodic physical count and valuation basis, the loss by declining prices is sometimes overlooked and allowed to clear through the accounts and statements in the form of an increased cost of goods sold. Unfortunately this oversight might be misinterpreted by management as a failure to set sales prices at the proper margin rather than as a market loss, as may be shown by an illustration. 

Assume that prices in the Fischer-Hayes Mercantile Company's area have been fairly steady throughout the year 19X1 and until December 19X2, when a substantial drop occurred. The inventory valuations were:

January 1 19X1 

December 31 December 31

In Figure 33 all of the inventory figures are at the lower of cost or market. The $10,000 loss which occurred in December, 19X2 simply has the effect of increasing the cost of sales for that year and of decreasing the gross margin. The drop of gross margin from 

33.3% to 29.6% of sales might be misinterpreted by management as being the result of failure to set selling prices high enough or other operating reasons. The truth of the situation is more clearly presented in the statements in Figure 34, which shows the end inventories at cost in the cost of sales calculation.

CHAPTER IX  

WE HAVE already encountered the simpler aspects of the depreciation problem in earlier chapters. Although exhaustive treatment of depreciation is beyond the scope of this book, some aspects of depreciation are considered in this chapter. 

Depreciable Assets 

Assets subject to depreciation are commercial buildings and factories; specialized structures such as water tanks, tramways, docks, and grain elevators; factory machinery and equipment; office furniture and equipment; and movable equipment such as cars, trucks, tractors, cranes, earth-moving equipment; and the like. Basically, the problem of accounting for depreciation on all these assets is the same. These assets differ in cost, in length of expected useful life, and in their probable salvage or scrap value. 

The Application of Cost to Fixed Assets 

Under exceptional circumstances the accountant may be justified in using appraisal values, or other specialized concepts of value for fixed assets, but in the majority of cases the value to be recorded on the books is the cost of the asset. This chapter confines itself to the cost of the asset and the depreciation based thereon. 

Even cost, however, must be carefully defined as it is not always a simple, single figure. Basically cost is the amount paid to the previous owner, but to this figure must be added any normal and necessary incidental outlays involved in the acquisition of the asset. This rule is closely parallel to the similar definition of cost as applied to current assets, but there are of course some differences in the kinds of incidental outlays that are necessary and normal. 

In the acquisition of a piece of heavy machinery, for instance, cost should obviously include the purchase price, packing costs, freight and cartage, insurance during transit, import duties, unpacking costs, and similar items. Not quite so obvious but still 223

properly included in cost would be the special foundation that has to be built for the machine, the cost of special power connections, and even the expenses of the factory expert who is employed to adjust the machine and to teach the company's employees how to operate it. The controlling concept is that the company is not purchasing a machine in a box on the loading platform but a machine in place, connected, adjusted, and ready for normal operation.

Sometimes the work of getting the machine uncrated, set up, and adjusted is performed by employees regularly on the company's payroll. In this case it may be difficult to arrive at a proper amount to debit to machinery for their services. Similarly the materials used in building the foundation or making the power connections may be taken from supplies which were purchased for operations or maintenance. If so, their costs should be ascertained and debited to machinery. When the company uses its own labor and its own supplies in this way, the account credited would not be cash but would be the accounts that normally are debited when the labor or supplies are purchased.

Sample entries may clarify these points. Assume that a company purchases a machine for $10,000; that freight and cartage, $800, are paid specifically for this machine; that a special insurance policy, costing $20, is purchased to protect the machine in transit; that the work of uncrating, worth $125, is done by the company's own receiving department; that the company's own maintenance department erects the machine and makes the connections for the power. Assume that the maintenance foreman estimates the cost of the work to be $1,200; that special materials are purchased for the foundations, $300; and that materials, $700, drawn from regular supplies are used in the power connections for the machine. 

The following journal entries are needed to record the costs involved in purchasing this machine: 

Machine #6262 (invoice price)

Accounts Payable 

Machine # 6262 (freight and cartage) Cash ..... 

Machine #6262 (insurance in transit) Cash ..... 

Machine #6262 (uncrating)

Receiving Department Expense 

10,000 10,000 800 800 20 20 125 125

Machine #6262 (erection)

Maintenance Department Expense 

Machine #6262 (foundation materials)

Accounts Payable 

1,200 1,200 300 300 700 

Machine #6262 (power connection materials). . 700

Supplies on Hand 

Altogether these entries bring the cost of machine #6262 to $13,145, which is much higher than the bare $10,000 that a less meticulous accountant might record as the cost of this machine. 

In addition to producing a proper cost for the machine, these entries have the effect of relieving certain expense accounts of improper elements. For instance, if the maintenance department is required to erect half a dozen new machines this month, the costs involved must be credited to the proper accounts, or the recorded maintenance department costs would rise, in comparison to normal periods, with the result that the maintenance foreman might be censured for incurring expenses in excess of his budget. 

To keep the permanent asset accounts uncluttered by detail, the accountant often uses a temporary account to collect the acquisition costs of a new asset. Then when all of the costs are known, he makes an entry closing the total of the temporary account into his permanent asset account as one clean cost figure. This procedure would be especially appropriate when the detailed entries required to record the acquisition of an asset, such as a building, may be spread over a long period and entail many entries. Visualize, for instance, the construction of a new building by a company using its own equipment and employees. This project might actually involve the purchase of materials in hundreds of shipments, the weekly payrolls for a year or more, as well as many incidental expenses, such as architect's fees, building permits, payments to specialized subcontractors, and so forth. In this case the temporary account probably would be called Plant X, Construction in Progress, or some such title, and would not be closed to the permanent account, Plant X, until construction was completed entirely and the final total cost known. 

Trade-Ins and the Cost of Assets 

When a piece of used equipment is traded in as part of the purchase price of an asset, the true cost of the asset acquired is

the cash paid (or liability incurred) plus the real value of the item traded in. This correct cost may be difficult to determine, however, because there is often no way of finding the real value of the item traded in. In most cases, therefore, the new asset is perforce entered on the books at the amount paid plus the book value of the old asset. No gain or loss is shown for the disposition of the old asset because the old book value is carried forward as part of the new asset value. If the depreciation estimates on the old asset were reasonably correct, this method is simple and unobjectionable. It is accepted for Federal income tax purposes. 

If it is known that the old asset is substantially overvalued on the books, it may be sold, rather than traded in, in order to establish the fact of loss for income tax purposes. When the purchase of the new item is then made at the best price obtainable, the resulting book valuation unquestionably would be the correct cost figure and would not be subject to a suspicion of overvaluation. 

A caution may be necessary at this point. If the business, tax, and accounting situation is to be kept clear, the sale of the old asset must be a bona fide one made at arm's length. If the asset is sold to the dealer from whom the new purchase is to be made, the suspicion may arise that neither the sale nor the purchase was at a true price. The loss may be disallowed for tax purposes, and depreciation on the new item may be questioned when it appears on the tax return.

Assume the X Company has a machine on its books as follows: 

Allowance for Depreciation

Machine #82 Machine #82 57,300 40,000

Assume first that machine #82 is now traded in for new machine, # 9-122, together with $60,000 cash. In the absence of any evidence of the real value of old machine # 82 the entry would be:

Machine #9-122 

Allowance for Depreciation of Machine #82. 77,300 40,000

Machine #82 57,300 Cash 60,000

The cost shown for new machine # 9-122 is the total of the $60,000 cash payment plus the remaining book value $17,300 of the old machine.

Assume second that machine #82 was greatly overvalued at the book value of $17,300, and that X Company sold it to a secondhand machine dealer for $1,500, thus establishing a tax loss of $15,800. 

X Company then purchased new machine #9-122 for cash $61,500.

The entries for the sale of the old and the purchase of the new machines would clearly reflect the two separate transactions as follows: 

Cash 1,500 

Loss on Sale of Machine #82 15,800 

Allowance for Depreciation of Machine #82. 40,000

Machine #82 57,300 Machine #9-122 61,500 Cash 61,500 Theoretically if the $1,500 value of old #82 is known, an entry could be made to recognize the loss of $15,800 and to record the purchase of the new machine at $61,500, even if a trade-in is made instead of the arm's length sale and purchase. The resulting loss in book value would, however, not be recognized for tax purposes; hence tax figures and book figures would not agree. This awkwardness would persuade most businessmen to consummate the sale. 

The Basic Problem of Depreciation 

In each case the purpose of accounting for depreciation is to allocate the depreciable cost of the asset (that is the difference between total cost and expected residual value) over the economic or useful life of the asset in such a way that each accounting period is burdened with a fair share of that cost. Since the useful life and the scrap value are at best only estimates, periodic depreciation is not exact. In each case the accountant should avail himself of the advice of the engineers, maintenance men, and shop people in arriving at the depreciation estimates. For standardized types of equipment with which the business has had previous experience, the allowance for depreciation would probably be quite accurate. But for new types of equipment, used under rapidly changing conditions, the accountants' best estimates may prove to be either much too high or much too low. Later in this chapter, the possible adjustments to be made when estimates are discovered to be wrong are discussed. 

The gradual loss of value of plant assets seems to be a perfectly

obvious illustration of the concept of expense as a decrease in proprietorship caused by the consumption of asset values in the normal course of business. Yet it is true that before the advent of the income tax, many businessmen and accountants refused to recognize depreciation as an expense. Even today the depreciation estimate is used occasionally as a device for juggling net incomes. 

This use of the estimate denies the basic fact that depreciation is an expense, and that it must be recognized just as clearly as wages, insurance, fuel, or any other expense.

The banker is interested in the adequacy of the depreciation allowance on his customers' books partly because he is interested in the overall accuracy of the records, but more specifically because he does not want the customer to deceive himself with regard to the profitableness of the enterprise. Depreciation today is by and large quite well understood and handled correctly. Before the income tax law, some businessmen were unaware of the impact that depreciation had on business, and many businesses found themselves in grave financial difficulty after some years of supposedly profitable operation because they had failed to allow for depreciation.

A reverse danger which may be present in relatively rare cases should be of some concern to the banker under today's conditions. 

With a shortsighted attitude toward the income tax problem, a businessman sometimes is tempted to take an unjustifiably large amount of depreciation. When excessive depreciation is discovered by the tax authorities after a period of years, the business may suddenly incur a large tax liability on the tax years still open. Such a liability would be a prior lien on the assets and might prove very embarrassing to the business if the amounts owed to the bank were considerable. 

THE STRAIGHT-LINE METHOD OF DEPRECIATION 

To understand the various ramifications of depreciation accounting, it is necessary first to grasp the pattern of entries for the "clean" situation in which all original estimates prove to have been accurate.

Assume that a truck purchased on January 1, 19X1 for $5,920 is estimated to have a six-year life with $1,120 residual or scrap

value, and at the end of the sixth year, it is actually sold for $1,120.

The depreciation would be calculated as follows:

The annual credit to the Allowance for Depreciation of Truck accumulates year by year until, at the end of the asset's estimated life, the credit equals the estimated depreciation value. The allowance account is a contra against the asset account and is shown subtracted from it in the statement of condition. For instance the December 31, 19X3 statement would show:

Truck 

Less: Allowance for Depreciation $5,920 2,400 $3,520

After the annual depreciation entry on December 31, 19X6 the carrying, or book, value of the truck would be $5,920 - $4,800 or $1,120, its estimated scrap value. When the truck is sold for exactly this value no profit or loss results, and the entry is made to remove the truck account and its contra from the books with a debit to cash for the amount received.

The depreciation expense account is, of course, closed to Expense and Revenue at the end of each year. 

The Handling of Erroneous Depreciation Estimates 

Actually the accountant does not expect the scrap value estimate to prove exactly right. If, in the illustration, the cash received for the truck was $900 instead of $1,120, when the truck was scrapped on December 31, 19X6, the entry would recognize the difference as a Loss on Disposal of Truck. 

December 31, 19X6 Cash 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck 900 4,800 

Loss on Disposal of Truck 220 Truck 5,920 Sale of the discarded item for more than the estimated scrap value would result in a credit to Profit on Disposal of Truck. 

When a business expects to scrap numerous assets during a period, it may use an account with a more general title, such as Profit and Loss on Disposals, to collect the various differences. 

If the discarded item were not immediately sold, its estimated scrap value would be debited to an account entitled Scrapped 

Equipment. The entry would be as follows: 

Scrapped Equipment 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck

Truck 1.120 4,800 5,920

Presumably the accumulated pile of scrapped equipment will be sold to the scrap dealer who offers the highest bid, and the profit or loss on the lot will be recognized at the time of the sale of the truck.

An error which is likely to be more serious than an inaccurate estimate of scrap value is the failure of the asset to endure for its estimated life. If, for instance, the truck had to be scrapped at the end of five years, there would be a loss on disposal caused by the failure of the truck to endure for the expected six years. 

If the scrap value proved to be the amount originally estimated the entry would be: 

Cash .. 

December 31, 19X6 1,120 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck 4,000

Loss on Disposal of Truck 

800 Truck 5,920 The $800 loss in this case obviously represents the sixth year's use of the truck which the owner did not have. 

If both the scrap value and the life estimates prove erroneous, the profit or loss on disposal results from the combination of the two misestimates. This situation is so common, in fact, that it may be considered as the typical case.

Depreciation and Fractional Years 

If an asset is acquired and put into use other than at the beginning of a year, the annual depreciation is calculated in accordance with the method outlined above but the entry at the end of the first year must recognize depreciation for that portion of the year during which the asset was in use.

Assume, for instance, that the $5,920 truck was purchased on 

April 1, 19X1, and that the estimates were as before: Residual value $1,120 and life expectancy six years. The annual depreciation is expected to be $800 but the depreciation to be recognized at 

December 31, 19X1 would be three-quarters of a year or $600. Subsequent year-end entries would recognize full annual depreciation of $800 each. If the truck's life was estimated correctly, the truck would be used until April 1, 19X7 at which time depreciation for one quarter of a year would be entered before the entry was made recording the sale or other disposition of the truck.

The completed life history of the truck would then appear 

T-accounts as follows: 

in 

Truck 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck 4/1/X1 5,920 | 5,920 4/1/X7 4/1/X7 4,800 600 12/31/XI 800 12/31/X2 800 12/31/X3 800 12/31/X4 800 12/31/X5 800 12/31/X6 200 4/1/X7 4,800 4,800

The debits to depreciation expense resulting from the pattern of entries are fair since the truck presumably earned income each year in proportion to the depreciation charged. 

Regardless of when the asset was purchased, the problem of the fractional year presents itself when the asset is discarded at any time other than at a year's end. In this situation an entry must be made to bring the depreciation allowance up to date before the entry for disposition is made. If this were not done, the current year would not have its proper charge for depreciation and the profit or loss on disposition would be correspondingly misstated. Assume that the truck is sold on September 1, 19X6, then the T-accounts and entries are: September 1, 19X6 

Depreciation Expense.. 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck

To record depreciation on truck to date of disposition (3 year) Cash 533 533 1.120 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck 

Loss on Disposition 4,333 467 Truck 5,920 Truck 

Allowance for Depreciation of Truck 4/1/X1 5,920 5,920 9/1/X6 9/1/X6 4,333 600 12/31/XI 800 12/31/X2 800 12/31/X3 800 12/31/X4 9/1/X6 

Loss on Disposition 467 800 12/31/X5 533 9/1/X6 4,333 4,333

The loss on disposition, $467, in effect represents the loss of the use of the truck for seven months, since it was discarded seven months earlier than the date originally estimated for its disposition, April 1, 19X7. Seven-twelfths of a year's depreciation ($800) is $467. Generally, of course, the expected scrap value would not be obtained exactly and the recognized loss would be a composite figure made from the misestimate of the two factors. 

Since depreciation is at best an estimate, it is common practice to round calculations to the nearest dollar as when $533 was used instead of $533.33, and $467 instead of $466.67. Similarly, calculations and entries are made usually as of the nearest month end rather than as of a certain day. The errors that result from such approximate methods tend to balance each other. In large businesses when there are a great many transactions involving depreciation of assets, it may be sufficiently accurate to regard all purchases and dispositions as though made at the nearest half-year or even year end. The degree of accuracy to use requires good judgment in every practical situation. 

Adjustments of Obvious Misestimates 

In previous illustrations, it was assumed that the original estimate of the asset's life proved to be substantially correct. Suppose that after a few years, it becomes obvious that the estimate of the asset's life is quite wide of the mark. There are two methods of handling this problem. The first is the method commonly used by business. This method is for income tax purposes. Briefly stated: 

Write off the remaining depreciation over the expected remaining life of the asset. This method may be considered satisfactory when the amounts involved are not very large, and when the corrections in one direction may be expected to offset the corrections in the other direction on other depreciating assets. 

The second, and theoretically better, method requires a recalculation of the depreciation and of the allowance when the new estimate of the asset's life becomes available. An entry is then made debiting or crediting the allowance to adjust it to its proper amount in the light of the new estimates. The balancing credit or debit is made to the account Adjustment of Prior Years' Deprecia tion. Thereafter, the annual entry for depreciation is made on the new, corrected basis. Formerly, the adjustment was credited or

debited to Retained Earnings (or Surplus) but the leading accountants today consider that it is better practice to reflect the adjustment through the Expense and Revenue account and to report it in the annual income statement as a special item. This method is in pursuance of the clean retained earnings (or clean surplus) concept.

The Remaining-Value-Over-Remaining-Life Basis of Depreciation 

Suppose a building purchased new for $183,000 on January 1, 1937 had an estimated life of thirty years and an estimated net wrecking value of $3,000. Annual depreciation would be estimated at ($183,000 $3,000) $3,000) 30 or $6,000 per year. Suppose depreciation has been written off on this basis for twenty years, that is through December 31, 1956. Under assumption (2), forty years, there remain twenty years in which to write off the remaining depreciation of $60,000. Annual depreciation charges of $3,000 will be required. If this new estimate is correct, the allowance account will receive twenty credits of $3,000 each by December 31, 1976 at which time it will total $180,000, bringing the net book value of the building down to its scrap value of $3,000.

It should be noted that under both of these assumptions the annual depreciation was not really correct either before or after the changing of the rate at the end of twenty years. In each case the accountant in effect balances one series of errors (before the

change point) against another set of errors (after the change point). All that can be said for this method is that it is easy, legal for tax purposes, and that it finally gives correct results for the entire life of the asset, though no one year is correct. 

If there is any reason to suppose that the original estimate of the asset's scrap value is incorrect, a reestimated figure for scrap can easily be incorporated into the recalculation. In spite of the adjustment, the revised estimates may well prove to be incorrect with the result that it is necessary to recognize a profit or loss on retirement, or if the situation is discovered in time, to recognize a second revision of annual depreciation on a new remainingvalue-over-remaining-life basis. 

Adjustment of Prior Years' Depreciation 

The method of making an adjustment for prior years' depreciation may best be explained by adapting the previous illustration to new facts.

Assume that a building cost $183,000, with an original estimate of a thirty-year life and $3,000 scrap value. At the time the reestimates are made depreciation has been written off for twenty years. 

The same reestimates are used, that is: (1) twenty-five years or (2) forty years, and the date on which the adjustment is made, December 31, 1957.

The crucial question is: At this time what would be the amount of the allowance if the new, and presumably correct, estimates had been available originally? For assumption (1), twenty-five years, the annual depreciation would have been ($183,000-$3,000) 

25 or $7,200, and the allowance to date would have been 

20 X $7,200 or $144,000.

OTHER METHODS OF DEPRECIATION ACCOUNTING 

Although the straight-line method has been and still is by far the most popular method used for the calculation of depreciation, other methods are sometimes used. Several of these methods result in greater amounts of depreciation being taken in the earlier years of an asset's life and lesser amounts of depreciation in the later years.

The Curved-Line Methods 

The theoretic argument for such curved-line methods is that the decreasing depreciation more or less balances the cost of repairs and maintenance, which normally becomes heavier as an asset ages. 

Two practical arguments for increasing the depreciation charges in the earlier years are: (1) these charges are deductible for tax purposes and thus reduce the taxes in the early years while the tax rates are high (in the hope and expectation that, later, rates may be lower); (2) these charges reduce income taxes and the

earnings available for cash dividends. Thus they encourage and help finance the expansion of the business. 

One of these methods is known as the sum-of-years-digits method. Under this method if the asset has a six-year life, the digits 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 are added to give a total of 21. The first year's depreciation will then be 6/21 times the depreciable value (that is, cost minus salvage). The second year's depreciation will be 5/21 of the depreciable value, and so forth, down to the sixth year when the fraction used is 1/21. For an asset with a twenty-year life the fractions would be 20/210, 19/210, and so forth, down to 1/210 in the twentieth year. By this method the allowance for depreciation begins with an amount considerably larger than it would have by the straight-line method; it then receives gradually decreasing amounts. The book value of the asset drops rapidly at first and then gradually levels out, but it always is lower than it would be by the straight-line method. 

A second curved-line method (now becoming popular because it may be used for income tax purposes for assets newly acquired after December 31, 1953) is known as the declining-balance method. By this method an annual rate is used that is not greater than twice the applicable straight-line rate but is applied each year to the declining balance (or book value) of the asset. Thus for an asset with an estimated life of twenty years the annual rate would be 10% (that is 2 × 5%). This method results in a book value curve somewhat similar to that of the sum-of-the-years-digit curve, and is considered to have similar advantages. 

Comparison of Three Methods of Depreciation 

Assume that a machine costing $81,000 has an estimated life of ten years and a probable salvage or scrap value of $1,000. The following depreciation allowance accounts for the three methods indicate the handling of depreciation under the methods previously described.

Note that both the straight-line method and the sum-of-theyears-digits method bring the allowance to its proper point of $80,000 (that is, they bring the book value down to estimated salvage). By the declining-balance method, however, the allowance is only $72,300, corresponding to a book value of $8,700, which is far from the original estimates. Since this method does not use the

salvage value in making the calculations, it is only a coincidence when the book value matches the salvage at the end of the asset's estimated life. If the salvage value is relatively low, the book value by this method usually exceeds the salvage value at the end of the estimated life.

Straight-line

The income tax rule remedies this situation by allowing the taxpayer to change from the strict declining-balance method to a straight-line method for the remaining life of the asset whenever it becomes apparent that it is more reasonable to do so. In the previous illustration, for instance, at the end of the eighth year the declining-balance allowance is $67,408, and it is apparent that the book value will not be reduced to the amount of the salvage

value in two more years. The proper technique is to write off the remaining depreciable value ($81,000 - $1,000 $67,408) or $12,592 by a depreciation charge of $6,296 each year for the remaining two years. This change to the straight-line method for the remaining years could have been made a few years earlier if the taxpayer had desired.

The Production Method 

A final method, which is very useful under certain circumstances, is known as the production method. It is useful when the life of the asset can be estimated in terms of its units of production (instead of in years) and when the annual production can readily be ascertained. For instance the owner of a fleet of heavy duty trucks may know that each truck can be operated economically for 50,000 miles, but he cannot know in advance how many years it may take to get that much use from each truck. In a poor year his trucks may be operated for only a few thousand miles each, whereas in a boom year they may run twelve or sixteen hours a day and may each be operated for 20,000 miles or more a year. 

Under these conditions a truck which cost $6,500 and which has an estimated life of 50,000 miles with a salvage value of $500 might reasonably be depreciated at 12 cents per mile. Odometer (mileage) readings at the beginning and end of each accounting period establish the miles of operation very easily, and this figure multiplied by 12 cents per mile gives the depreciation for the accounting period.

The lives of some factory machines can be measured in terms of the number of units of product which can be produced; the life of an airplane engine can be measured in terms of hours of operation; the life of a mine hoist can be measured in terms of the tons of coal available to be removed; the life of a sawmill may be measured in terms of the board feet of lumber available in forests within economic range. 

Whenever the data are available, the production method usually results in charging the depreciation to the benefiting periods in the most reasonable way. This method deserves to be used more than it is. It is, incidentally, fully acceptable to the income tax authorities.

It must be remembered that under the curved-line or produc-

tion methods as well as under the straight-line method, the estimates might be erroneous. If these errors come to light only when it is time to retire the asset, they will result in the recognition of profits or losses on retirement. If the errors are recognized while the asset has a substantial period of usefulness still remaining, adjustments can be made comparable to those described for the straight-line method. Under the curved-line methods, there is less pressure to make such formal adjustments since the heavy depreciation has been recorded earlier and since any adjustment would be relatively small.

The Group Method 

Prior discussion has assumed that depreciation is calculated separately for each asset unit. This approach is necessary to present the problem clearly, and in fact it is widely used. A manufacturing company may have hundreds of machines and have a complete and a detailed depreciation history for each machine on a separate card in the "plant" ledger. The figures shown as Machinery and as Allowance for Depreciation of Machinery on the general ledger and on the statement of condition are summaries of the actual detail shown in the plant ledger. Similar detail and total relationships are often found for office machinery, trucks, and so forth. 

In contrast to the complete records previously described, many firms have only the group accounts for each general category of depreciable asset. Under this system for instance, the accounts show only the Machinery and Allowance for Depreciation of Machinery accounts in the general ledger with no supporting detail. All purchases of new machinery are debited to the Machinery account, and the estimate of annual depreciation is made at ten, twelve, or fifteen per cent on the balance in the account at the end of the year or on its average balance during the year. When a particular machine is discarded, the values to be removed from the asset and the allowance accounts are calculated from any information that is available as to the original cost and depreciation to date. When records are unclear, it is difficult to make the proper entries for loss or gain on retirement. It is difficult to substantiate the reported figures if they are challenged by tax authorities.

It is of course possible to operate a group method in the case of such uniform multi-unit properties as the poles of a telephone

line, the cross-ties in a railroad, and so forth. This proper use of the group method is beyond the scope of this text. It is mentioned merely to highlight the questionable use of group methods for nonhomogeneous categories, such as machinery and office equipment.

Depreciation and Improvements 

When, after an asset has been in use for some time, a firm spends money on that asset to add to its capacity, to increase its efficiency, or to reduce its operating cost, such an expenditure is considered an improvement. The amount so spent is debited to the asset account, and a recalculation of annual depreciation for ensuing accounting periods usually is required. Frequently it is well to reconsider the estimates of the asset's life and scrap value, which may be affected by the improvement.

Assume that the straight-line method is in use. After the improvement, the depreciation is calculated by dividing the remaining years of the asset's life into its remaining depreciable value, that is, its total cost including the improvement, minus its expected scrap value and minus the balance already in the Allowance for 

Depreciation account. 

If the improvement is made at or near the end of a year, only whole years are involved, and therefore the calculation is quite simple. If the improvement is made earlier in the year, the depreciation for the year should be calculated at the old rate for the fraction of the year before the improvement (¼, ½, ½, etc.), and at the new rate for the remaining fraction of the year. Great care must be exercised to understand the facts with regard to any changed estimate of the asset's life. Will the asset last five years longer than originally estimated? five years after this year? five years from the date of the improvement? or what is the meaning of the reestimate? Careful attention to these several possible interpretations is as necessary in actual accounting practice as in working problems. 

Maintenance and Depreciation 

Generous maintenance can extend the life of any asset appreciably, whereas neglect of maintenance can shorten it. In estimating the life of any asset for depreciation purposes, it is gen-

erally assumed that the asset will be given normal maintenance. 

The cost of such normal day-to-day and week-to-week repair, cleaning, adjustment, and so forth is charged to Repairs Expense or Maintenance Expense as a current operating cost and does not affect the depreciation accounting.

Occasionally, however, a company finds it advantageous to give an asset an exceptionally thorough and costly overhauling, which was not contemplated originally. To charge such cost directly to expense, when incurred, would distort the showing of the net income for the period since the period does not as a matter of fact receive the entire benefit of the expenditure. If the effect of the exceptional maintenance expenditure is to benefit two or three following years by reducing their maintenance costs, the proper accounting is to record the part of the expenditure that is in excess of normal as a deferred expense to be recognized as expense in the benefiting years. If the effect of the exceptional maintenance is to lengthen the life of the asset beyond its original estimate, the proper accounting for the excess over normal is to charge it against the Allowance for Depreciation account. The same annual depreciation can then be continued for the extra years, presumably added to the asset's life. Admittedly this kind of accounting is a makeshift expedient, but it is widely used, is allowable for income tax purposes, and is not unreasonable in terms of expense distribution to the periods affected.

A theoretically better method for spreading heavy maintenance expenditures is the use of an Allowance for Maintenance in conjunction with certain major asset items, however, this method is not allowable for taxes. For instance a firm owning a warehouse whose probable life is fifty years may reasonably estimate major maintenance events as follows: 

Painting, every five years, 9 x $1,000 

New roof, 16th and 32nd year, 2 x $5,000 

Renewal of heating plant, 13th, 26th, and 

39th years, 3 x $2,000

Total heavy maintenance, estimated Annual allowance for heavy maintenance 

$ 9,000 10,000 6,000 $25,000 $500 Each year, in addition to charging the expense for current repairs covering dozens or hundreds of minor items, the accountant would debit Maintenance Expense and credit Allowance for

Maintenance $500. When painting, reroofing, and furnace renewals occur, the entry would be a debit to Allowance for Maintenance and a credit to Cash for the amount expended. If the estimates turn out to have been correct, the total cost of repairs and maintenance is spread equitably over the years of the asset's life history.

If it should become evident, after some years of operation, that such an allowance is either inadequate or excessive, the annual amount can be raised or lowered in accordance with the revised estimates. When the asset is finally retired from use the Allowance must be closed, any balance therein being closed to Retirement Profit and Loss, together with the profit or loss from the depreciation allowance.

During the life of the asset, the Allowance for Maintenance is regarded as a contra account and is shown as such in the statement of condition.

Small Tools and Depreciation 

A final practical problem concerns the depreciation on small items that technically are fixed assets since they may be fairly longlived but their value is so slight that we do not like to bother with full depreciation accounting for them. Small tools, such as hammers, planes, and wrenches used in a factory, may have useful lives of several years, yet to establish an asset account and an allowance for depreciation account to spread the cost of a $2.50 hammer over five years obviously would be an uneconomic use of time and effort. 

One method is to handle all purchases of such small items as expenses. This method is simple but incorrect in that no account shows the asset value of small tools, which in the aggregate may run to some thousands of dollars. A compromise method is to establish a fixed asset account, Small Tools, to carry the original purchase of small tools. Any subsequent sizable purchases, which obviously increase the small tools on hand, are added to the original Small Tools account. This account is not depreciated but left as a permanent balance. Subsequent, periodic, normal purchases are charged to expense since they are obviously replacements of tools worn out or lost. In a sense this compromise balances two errors against one another and results in a fairly reasonable showing of both asset values and expenses.

CHAPTER X  

THERE IS a pronounced tendency in discussions among cost accountants and in many writings to enlarge the definition of the term cost accounting to include virtually all the devices of accounting which are proving really useful to management. Thus the careful analysis of expenses by departments for the purpose of accounting for responsibility is considered by some cost accountants as their own province. This definition is probably too broad to be useful. The concept of cost accounting discussed in this text is the narrower concept of cost accounting, namely, the accounting for the manufacturing operations as such. 

Although it is not possible to cover adequately the subject of cost accounting in a single chapter, the general outlines of the subject may be stated. The banker, in his capacity as adviser to the businessman, need not be intimately acquainted with the details of the cost accounting system, but he should be familiar with the types of cost systems in order to consult with business management on matters of policy, and to interpret the facts developed by the cost accounting system for purposes of management. 

Comparison of General Accounting and Manufacturing Accounting 

Fundamentally, there is no difference in accounting whether the business involves a manufacturing, a trading, or a service situation. The basic classifications of transactions are still the same. By and large, the clearest statement of the difference between cost accounting and general accounting is that in cost accounting much closer attention is paid to the great number of internal transactions of the business which are characteristic of the manufacturing operation. In the trading or service businesses we concern ourselves largely with transactions that affect parties outside the business, the purchases and sales of commodities on account and the payment therefor, the purchase of expensive materials and services, the collection of revenues, and so forth. A few strictly internal 251

transactions involving depreciation of fixed assets, expiration of prepayments, and so forth are recognized as the rather infrequent happenings for which adjustments are made. 

In cost accounting, the many internal happenings which constitute the manufacturing process are followed through the entries on the books. In manufacturing, commodities are not merely bought in one step and sold in another, but are bought, stored, issued to the factory, worked on in the factory, moved as completed product to a finished goods warehouse, and finally sold. Each one of these steps is considered worthy of being separately recorded in the cost accounting process. The labor is likewise treated in much greater detail in a manufacturing business than in a trading or service business in which labor commonly is recorded as expense at the time the payroll is paid, a one-step entry. In cost accounting we recognize the day-by-day accrual of the wages payable with an extensive analysis on a daily, weekly, or monthly basis of the debit or cost phase, the entry somewhat paralleling the treatment of materials. In cost accounting factory labor is not considered an expense when the actual labor is performed but is recognized as an addition to the value of the goods in process. Thus typical labor, directly involved in the manufacturing process, does not become an expense, when purchased, when paid for, or when consumed, but only when it becomes a part of the cost of the manufactured product sold. This movement of labor value through the books is distinctly characteristic of cost accounting and should be noted very carefully by the newcomer.

Paralleling the movement of labor value through the books is the movement of the values of miscellaneous factory costs or expenses. Whereas in general accounting the expiration of prepaid insurance and the using up of supplies and services are considered expenses (that is deductions from capital or from income), in cost accounting we take the more exacting view that these values are simply changes of form, and they adhere to or are incorporated in the value of the goods in process. 

This shift in viewpoint and in recording technique is not arbitrary but is the consequence of the basic premise that accounting should portray as clearly as possible what is happening in the objective world before us. Faced with a typical manufacturing situation, the accountant would be misrepresenting the facts if he

were to portray the cost of labor spent on raw materials as a deduction from capital (that is, an expense). Certainly it is far more realistic to show the labor as adding to the value of the goods upon which the work is performed. Similarly, the objective facts of such factory costs as supplies consumed, power, depreciation of machinery, and so forth are portrayed more accurately if these values are shown as additions to the goods in process rather than as expenses. Thus the cost accounting techniques have firm support in the best accounting theory, as well as in their usefulness to management.

Figures 38 and 39 present graphically the differences between general financial accounting and cost accounting. They should be studied in conjunction with the discussion in the text. Note that the transaction lines do not reflect all transactions but only those types of transactions involved in the special relationships that reveal the differences between general and cost accounting. 

COST ACCOUNTING IN MANUFACTURING CONCERNS 

Most manufacturing operations may be classified either as work performed in accordance with a specific contract or as uniform units produced in quantity. Each type requires quite distinct treatment in the accounts. These two distinctive accounting patterns must be studied in order that the principal functional relationships may be clearly understood. With a working knowledge of the two pattes clearly in mind, the executive can adapt his thinking quite readily to situations requiring combination or hybrid forms.

Cost Accounting for Specific Contracts 

When production must be initiated, controlled, and studied in terms of specific lots or orders of the product the business usually is based on a competitive bid. Many manufacturers of heavy machinery produce only to the specifications of customers. It is difficult for management to estimate the costs on which the bids. are based. As each order is completed, the actual costs must be compared with the estimated costs. If the actual costs are higher than the estimated costs, the business makes less profit than anticipated or may even suffer a real loss on the contract. If actual costs

are lower than the estimated costs, the business makes a greater profit than intended.

In either case the actual costs must be compared with the estimate in great detail in order to assure accurate estimates in the future. A business bidding on contracts must be able to estimate its costs accurately since otherwise it would operate at a loss if it contracted for business at too low a price or even worse if it failed to obtain an adequate volume of business by bidding too high in the competitive market.

Other businesses, not in a competitive bidding situation, still may find it desirable to regulate production on a special order or job lot basis if their products are highly varied and if production cannot be maintained on a continuing basis. In this case, the cost of the products should be known within fairly close limits for the purpose of establishing a price policy. In some instances selling prices may be set to cover costs plus a desired profit. In other instances when the selling price is determined by competition, costs must be known before a decision can be reached on the advisability of withdrawing from competition.

If contracts are entered into on a cost or cost plus basis either with the government or private parties, it is necessary to develop a defensible cost figure. Often production is organized on a special order basis since such a basis is the only feasible way to isolate the costs of each specific contract.

Process Cost Accounting 

Manifestly a different situation confronts the manufacturer of cement, cereals, or soft drinks, whose production consists of millions of identical units which flow, day after day, through a standardized series of processes. The calculation of costs on any particular hundred-unit lot would be of no particular consequence. 

Under these conditions, the management is interested in watching the cost of the product (or products if the line is multiple), day by day, week by week, and month by month separately for every step in the entire sequence. Thus the slightest deviation in cost may be noted, traced, and corrected if it be excessive. 

Technically such a production pattern is known by the term sequential process. The accounting for this process generally is known as process cost accounting. This type of cost accounting,

if it can be used, entails far less detail than does special order accounting or job cost accounting. It also lends itself more easily to the application of standard costing techniques. For these reasons accountants prefer to use process cost accounting.

The Flow of Costs in Multiple Department Businesses 

Although some of the principles of special order and process costing may be illustrated against the background of very simple businesses, in actual practice accountants seldom deal with the single department business. Therefore it is desirable to visualize the structure of the accounts and the typical flow of entries for the manufacturing business with multiple departments. 

Figure 40 shows the flow of expense entries in a business that recognizes a group of selling and general administrative departments and then, within the manufacturing departments, recognizes the further grouping of service departments and production departments. Significant also are a group of nondepartmental expense accounts which are called prime expense accounts. Note that the flow lines of the chart (Figure 40) recognize four kinds of entries involved in consolidating the expenses into the production departments. Type (a) entries recognize transactions that, at their first recording, may be charged directly to the departments without going through the prime expense accounts. Type (b) entries (that is, b1, b2, b3, and b4) recognize transactions which at the time of their first recording must be charged to prime expense accounts. 

Entry type (c) recognizes the distribution of the prime expenses to the departmental expense accounts. Note that, as shown in Figure 

40, there are two steps in the expense distributions. First, the prime costs are distributed to all departments involved. Second, the totals of the service departments are distributed to the production, sales, and general administrative departments as shown by the dotted line (d).

In each instance, the specific basis for distributing the prime expenses and the service department totals should be that basis which best expresses the casual-beneficial relationship between the expense and the department. In practice there should be considerable investigation and discussion when the distribution system is first established in order to make certain that the several bases are the best that can be developed and are satisfactory to all concerned.

The Special Order or Job Cost System 

The heart of a job cost system is the Work in Process account in the general ledger. This account is a control account and is supported by detailed accounts in a subsidiary ledger. Each detailed account records the accumulated charges for one job. As in any relationship existing between a control account and its subsidiary ledger, procedures must be devised in order to assure the essential agreement of the control account and its subsidiary detailed accounts. Accordingly, every dollar of material, labor, and factory expense (overhead, burden) charged to the account for an individual order also must appear in the control account. If this principle is comprehended clearly, then following the procedures inherent in any system should not be too difficult. 

Standard ledger rulings are not used, but instead the subsidiary work in process ledger consists of cost sheets that are specially ruled. In addition to columns for dates, references, and so forth separate columns for each of the three elements of cost commonly are provided on cost sheets. Sometimes a section of the cost sheet is specially ruled for summarizing the detailed columns upon completion of the contract. 

Many large businesses mechanize their cost accounting systems by using posting machines to enter the detailed charges for material, labor, and overhead on the cost sheets. In punched card accounting, instead of a sheet or series of sheets on which the costs are noted and accumulated by pen and ink or by machined figures, there are carefully filed batches of cards whose coded holes contain the information, namely charges for material, labor, and overhead. 

Regardless of the format or mechanical aid used for the general ledger account and for the detailed cost sheets, we never should lose sight of the central fact that we are dealing with a general ledger control account and its subsidiary ledger. The agreement between the control and its subsidiary ledger should be proved periodically because omissions or errors may be occurring that could seriously affect managerial judgments based on the figures. 

Figure 41 portrays in simplified form the relationships in a typical special order system. It should be studied carefully.

Material Costs in the Job Cost System 

An outline of the procedures evolved to assure agreement between the control account and its subsidiary ledger may provide the banker student with a background for understanding similar accounting systems which he may encounter later. 

The accounting for materials to be charged to jobs should be tied up as closely as possible to their authorization and to their actual movements around the factory in order that the records may correspond with the facts. Materials for production may be ordered from the raw materials warehouse by the production control department (engineering, planning, and so forth) or by the departmental foremen. The system rule should require that either of these orders for moving materials must be in writing, with one copy of each requisition to be sent to the cost accounting department. This requisition must be signed by the authorized person, and it must show the number of the special order for which the materials are intended. All requisitions are sorted by order number and posted daily (either in detail or in summary) to the material column of their respective accounts or cost sheets. 

The totals of all materials issued are credited to the Raw Materials account and debited to the Work in Process account in the general ledger. These entries can be traced in Figure 41. 

Labor Costs in the Job Cost System 

The routine of accounting for labor to get the proper charges to each job involves more detail techniques but essentially follows the same pattern as accounting for the raw materials. Each workman's time must be accounted for in terms of the minutes or hours that he works on each job. During one day a man may work on several orders. A time ticket for each order showing the time spent and his rate per hour must be filled out by each workman or by the timekeeper. When these tickets have been completely extended, they are sorted by order numbers and summarized. The summary totals are posted to the job cost sheets. If the laborer is working on a piece rate basis, the time ticket must show this rate and also the number of pieces completed. All direct-labor costs must be accounted for by job numbers and charged to their respective cost sheets regardless of any complicating factors, such

as bonuses and other incentive devices. The total of all charges to the individual orders must be charged to the Work in Process control account. These entries are traced in Figure 41. 

There is one practical problem, namely that of differentiating between direct labor, which can be charged to orders, and labor that is indirect. Labor that is essential to the operation of the factory but that has only a remote relationship to specific orders, and which cannot therefore be accounted for by charges to the cost sheets, is known as indirect labor. Maintenance men and truckers are examples of indirect labor. Such labor is accounted for as an element of overhead and is handled in the same way as insurance, depreciation on machinery, and other indirect factory expenses. Occasionally certain elements of labor appear to border between being direct or indirect, and they can be handled either way. For instance, the time spent by a workman specializing in the setting-up operation in a lathe department could be charged directly to the order for which the lathe is being prepared. This method of costing his labor, however, would involve a great deal of timekeeping for him and many small charges to different orders. 

Under these conditions it is much easier to charge his entire day's work to indirect labor for the department in which he works. 

Eventually when the overhead is computed, the cost of this workman's labor, along with the expense of other overhead items, is distributed among all the work in process. 

Labor expense is complicated by such incentive payments as salaries paid to workers for their vacation periods, sick leaves, and "portal-to-portal" pay, and so forth. These costs are valid and must be absorbed in the cost of product. Accordingly, since these costs cannot be relegated to any particular order, they must be considered as indirect costs and handled as overhead. 

Overhead Costs in the Job Cost System 

The initial accounting for factory expenses that are to be treated as overhead is similar to the treatment accorded them in general accounting. In addition however, one new element is involved: factory costs are segregated from the selling and general administrative expenses. This procedure might necessitate some careful rethinking of expense classifications in a small business, in which expenses are not recorded on a properly designed departmental

basis. A larger business with a proper classification of accounts has no such problems since the accounts following the actual organizational plan, already clearly identify the factory costs. 

The simplest treatment of overhead is as follows: At the end of the month ascertain the total overhead for the month. Divide this total by the total of direct labor charged to each job during the month to ascertain the dollar amount of overhead to charge to each job. This results in charging each month's actual overhead to the jobs in proportion to the direct labor charged to them. 

Actually, this simple method is seldom used as we described it. 

However, it is basically very important, since the more highly perfected methods can be most clearly understood as a refinement of this simple approach. In general, this simple method needs to be improved in two respects. The first respect is in the assumption that overhead should be apportioned to the orders on the basis of direct-labor dollars.

By trying to think through the problem of the casual-beneficial relationship between overhead and the production of specific orders, it becomes apparent that the labor-dollar basis is both arbitrary and questionable. Some overhead elements, such as workmen's compensation insurance, are determined as a function of the wages. Some elements, the cost of supervision for example, are probably related closely to wages whereas other elements, like fire insurance and depreciation of machinery and buildings, are related only very indirectly if at all to the wages paid.

The Labor-Hour Basis 

The first improvement in the allocation of costs in the special order cost system that is suggested usually is the allocation of overhead on the basis of the hours of labor charged to the orders rather than on the basis of dollars of wages paid. In the case of the supervision element of overhead, the labor-hour basis would be nearer to the ideal of recognizing casual relationships. Supervision is a person-to-person function and the worker in the low income scale may need as much of the supervisor's time and attention as does another worker who receives a much higher salary. 

Certainly the housing-factor elements of overhead such as depreciation, taxes, and insurance on buildings, heating, and lighting are related to the number of persons provided for rather than to

the wages which they receive. If the labor-hour method is used, however, it involves the recording of the hours of labor charged to each order as well as the dollars. The overhead rate, on the labor-hour basis, is calculated at the end of the month by dividing the total monthly overhead by the total number of hours of direct labor charged to jobs. This rate is expressed in terms of dollars and cents per man hour of direct labor and can be applied by multiplying each job's direct-labor hours by the rate. 

The Machine-Hour Basis 

The second improvement is based on the observation that in modern industry more and more overhead tends to be related to work done by machines, and (proportionately) less and less work tends to be related to labor, whether dollars or hours. Such elements as insurance, taxes, and depreciation on machinery, the power to run the machines, and the special structures needed to house the machines are obviously related to the function of the machines used in the manufacturing process. The cost of these elements is increasing continually, and the merit of the older labor-base methods is diminishing correspondingly. In order to allocate overhead in proportion to machine use, it is necessary to keep a record of the additional data of machine time devoted to the production of each job. This is a new element in the recording procedure and would need to be planned for carefully at the operating level. 

The mechanics of applying a machine-hour rate are simple.

Divide the total of the month's overhead by the total hours of machine time charged to work in process to arrive at a machinehour rate in dollars and cents per machine hour. Multiply the hours of machine time recorded for each order by this rate in order to allocate the entire overhead to the work in proportion to machine hours utilized by each job. 

The Double Rate Basis 

The most exact method to use probably is the double rate basis whereby the overhead elements are separated into two groups: labor factors and machine factors. The total of labor-factor overhead is divided by total direct-labor hours to produce a labor-base rate per hour, and the total machine-factor overhead is divided by

total machine hours to produce a machine-base rate per hour. 

Both factors are utilized on each order during the month thus requiring the distribution of overhead on a double basis. The double rate basis provides an excellent illustration of the practical application of good accounting theory-the very close observation of factual relationships and the effort to parallel them closely in the accounts.

The Element of Timing in the Allocation of Overhead 

The four preceding methods are all based on the assumption that it would be feasible to wait until the end of the month to determine and allocate the overhead for that month. But commonly, this delay is impracticable. As jobs are completed during the course of the month, it is desirable to complete the picture of their respective costs immediately upon completion. For this reason it is necessary to develop an overhead rate on an estimated basis, as follows: At the beginning of each month the cost accountant forecasts (1) the total overhead for the month and (2) the total rate-base data (direct-labor dollars, labor hours, or machine hours) for the month. Then he divides (1) by (2) to obtain the rate to be used during the month.

When the overhead is applied to jobs on the basis of an estimated rate, the charges are entered on the cost sheets as before, but the general ledger entry becomes a debit to Work in Process and a credit to a new account, Overhead Applied. At the end of the month the total actual overhead, as shown by the Factory Expense accounts, is closed into the debit side of Overhead Applied which will then show the difference between the estimated overhead and the actual overhead for the month. 

Insofar as the estimates of costs and volume differ from actual cost and volume, it means that overhead charges to jobs were not based on the actual costs for the month. If there is a credit balance in the Overhead Applied account then overhead has been overapplied, and the costs per order as shown exceed the actual costs for the month. Conversely, if there is a debit balance in the Overhead Applied account then overhead has been underapplied, and the costs per order shown are insufficient. Of course, if the difference between estimated and actual overhead is insignificant, the balance in the Overhead Applied account may be closed out to

Profit and Loss, or it may be allowed to remain in the Overhead Applied account on the assumption that opposite misestimates during the months following may cancel out the balance. 

Further discussion of the problem of overhead, especially in relation to seasonal businesses or with reference to the changing volumes of depressions and boom times is beyond the scope of the present text.

The Process Cost System 

The chief characteristic of special order cost accounting is that the Work in Process account is broken down to specific orders in its subsidiary ledger. The chief characteristic of process cost accounting is that it breaks down the Work in Process account into separate Work in Process accounts for each productive department. 

The accounting situation becomes complex when it is adapted to a multiple sequential process, which involves several lines of product that flow in a parallel or twisting pattern, or which involves joint raw materials which separate after one or more common processes to flow in different courses. At present it is better to confine the discussion to the manufacture of a single product that flows evenly through the departments of the factory. 

Note that Work in Process accounts are asset accounts. They are merely subdivisions of the familiar Inventory account. Therefore the entries made in such accounts must adhere to the normal accounting for assets: every debit adds to the asset and every credit subtracts from it.

The essential flows in a simple process cost system are illustrated in Figure 42, in which each of the following features of process costing is demonstrated. 

1. The handling of the entry for raw materials ordered for production is quite obvious. Requisitions from the production control department or from the departmental foremen are summarized to show the department processing the materials. These summaries are the basis for entries made daily, weekly, or monthly debiting the departmental Work in Process accounts and crediting 

Raw Materials. 

2. The direct-labor entries follow the pattern of the entries for materials. The timeclock records from which payrolls are accrued show the department in which each worker works. The typical

pattern of entries is : (1) a debit to Direct Labor Cost and a credit to Accrued Payroll and (2) debits to the departmental Work in 

Process accounts and a credit to Direct Labor Cost. 

The entries for overhead in process cost accounting are much simpler than overhead entries in special order cost accounting. In process cost accounting the total of each month's expenses in each production department is closed into the Work in Process account for that department. This closing is a simple matter, if the business is nonseasonal or if the management is willing to disregard the seasonal effect. If management desires to eliminate the seasonal effect, it can accomplish this closing through the use of an Overhead Applied account for each department. In this case the actual expenses for the month would be closed into this Overhead Ap plied account instead of directly into Work in Process. An entry for the normal monthly overhead (1/12 of the year's estimated overhead) would then be made, debiting Work in Process and crediting 

Overhead Applied for each department. 

3. The mechanics of recording the cost relationships of successive departments is one of the unique features of process costing. 

Consider the simple case in which the actual work in process in the departments at the end of each month is negligible. The balance of each department's Work in Process account is closed into the next Work in Process account at the end of the month. 

Less simple cases of process costing require careful attention to the problem of inventories and their relation to costs. 

In Figure 42 the value of the completed work in process is credited to Department I and debited to Department II. The same operation is performed in the case of Department II. But this time the total debit also includes value added by the first department. These transfers of the value of the completed work from department to department continue until the final department discharges its completed production, and the Finished Goods account is debited in order to reflect the physical delivery of the month's completed production to the finished goods warehouse. 

This process is shown in Figure 42 by the slanting arrows in the 

Work in Process accounts and in the Finished Goods account. 

Although a knowledge of the actual cost of the product in any given month may be useful, it will mean even more to management to have figures reported in such a way as to facilitate com-

parison with the costs of prior months. This data should include not only the total cost but also detailed costs within each department analyzed according to the three basic elements of material, labor, and departmental overhead. Such a complete array of information enables management either to discover and to correct any inefficiencies which develop, or to recognize and reward improved performance.

Standard Cost Techniques 

Customarily standard cost techniques apply most closely to the typical process cost situation, although the principles of standard costing also can be applied to aspects of the job cost situation. 

To illustrate: Imagine the use of standard costs in a one-product manufacturing business with three production departments, through each of which the product flows. The basic framework of accounts is the same as that envisaged in the process costing shown in Figure 42. Assume a manufacturing process which has no inventory of goods in process at the end of the month. Inventories of goods in process complicate the work, but do not change the basic principles of standard costing illustrated in Figure 43.
