FIGURE 43. STANDARD COST SHEET

The chief requirement of standard cost accounting is the establishment of a standard cost for the product. This standard is based on past experience, engineering know-how, time-study methods, research, and any other intelligible guide as to what the product ought to cost. The standard is built up in terms of the same three basic elements of material, direct labor, and overhead. The amount of each element that ought to be applied to a unit of product at each departmental stage is determined and incorporated into the standard cost. The standard cost sheet for assumed product nuts and bolts might appear as shown in Figure 43. 

Note how costs are added as the product passes through each department. The product brings with it the costs accumulated from each preceding department when it enters a department for processing. 

As production proceeds the accumulation of actual costs in the departmental Work in Process accounts is handled as before. 

However, the month-end handling of transfers is different. The value to be transferred to the next department is determined by multiplying the number of units transferred by the standard cost. per unit at that departmental stage. Figure 44 shows Work in 

Process accounts for the three departments and Finished Goods for the month of February, to illustrate the transfers at standard costs that would be made for 10,000 units completed on the basis of the standard cost sheet shown in Figure 43.

The standard unit cost for Department I is $4.65 (see Figure 43). 

Therefore the amount of $46,500 credited to Department I and debited to Department II is simply 10,000 times that standard unit cost. Since the debits in the Department I account are the actual costs of the month's production, the balance of $600, called Variance for February, shows the excess costs of Department I's work over what the 10,000 units ought to have cost. Recognition of the total variance of each department should be the first step in the accountant's analysis, which he presents to management at the end of each month.

Each departmental variance should be analyzed still further into its basic components of material, labor, and overhead. Reference to the standard cost sheet would enable the accountant to show the composition of the total variance of Department I.

Note that when actual cost is less than standard cost the variance is considered plus (+), or favorable; when actual is more than standard it is considered minus (−), or unfavorable. Actually, the foreman would want each of these variance figures analyzed still further to show how much of the (plus or minus) amount was the result of price changes and how much of it was caused by changing efficiency. The overhead variance is also affected by the volume of operations since many elements of overhead are fixed and therefore amount to less per unit as the volume increases. A full standard cost analysis examines these and many other factors in the variances of each department. Such a system is the acme of service that accounting is able to render to management in the manufacturing area.

This entry, when posted, serves to put all the expenses for the month into the department accounts and to close out the natural expense accounts. Up to this point our accounting has ignored the movement of work in process between departments and of finished work to the finished goods warehouse. Entries for these movements can be made quite simply under the standard cost technique such as is assumed in this case. Each stage in the manufacturing process has been carefully studied and a unit standard established for what the work ought to cost at that stage. Regardless of what actual costs are building up within a department for any month, the product transferred from each department to the next, or to finished goods, is accounted for at this standard cost. At the end of the month the goods in process inventory in each department is valued at the proper proportion of standard cost, and such balance is carried forward to the next month. 

Each work in process account thus will show actual costs for the month's production on its debit side and standard costs on its credit side. The balance (debit or credit) will be the measure by which the department has failed to meet its standard or by which it has exceeded its standard. This is of course only a rough gauge of the efficiency of the department management as many things may have happened to costs over which the management has no real control. These balances are known as the departmental variances. They are closed into expense and revenue and shown, with the material price variance, in the income statement. m. CHAPTER XI  

IN THE EARLIER chapters of this text, the management viewpoint is utilized as the motivating purpose behind each step taken in building the framework of the accounting system. In this chapter and the following chapters, the problem of accounting service to management is presented in a much more direct, specific, and useful form. 

This approach is made more realistic by the abandonment of the previous assumption of an existing accounting system. The assumption now is that it is our task to try to develop the accounting system in such a way that it will be of maximum usefulness to management. By this systems-building approach, our appreciation of the management aspect of accounting should be sharpened, and we should be equipped to obtain the best results for management from any system of accounting that is currently being used.

In the long run, this approach is useful because most students using this text will be associated with the management side of business as bankers advising clients. Thus, the systems approach takes on the significance: What has management a right to expect of its accountants? In the long run, the demands of management properly determine the nature of the accounting system used in the business. 

The Business Pattern 

The first task in establishing an accounting system for any business is to analyze that business for its overall structure or pattern. 

Sometimes a business, especially a very small one, has a single significant source of revenue, and all its expenses are incurred in the production of this one revenue. Such a business is called a single venture business.

Many businesses, which start out as single ventures, develop sideline activities which involve revenues and expenses of their 280

own. Even though these sidelines may be relatively small as compared with the main venture, it is desirable for the accounts to reflect them clearly in order that management may be kept fully aware of their impact on the total net income. If such sidelines are not clearly reflected in the accounts, they may be an unnecessary drain on the business year after year without management's ever becoming aware of the fact. If the accounting system clearly reflects the facts of such a situation, management may take the measures necessary to convert the sideline venture to profitable status or to discontinue it completely to make room for some entirely new developments in the business.

Many businesses own investments which produce revenues but which have no significant expenses. Such investments may be regarded as nonoperating sideline ventures. In the income statement, the incomes should be listed separately to reflect their net status.

Larger businesses frequently have multiple operating ventures which produce revenues and cause expenses in significant amounts. 

Certain management problems may concern the business as a whole, but by far the greater number of day-to-day problems that accounting must aid management in solving are those concerned with the operating units as such. The accounts, therefore, must reflect clearly the revenues and expenses segregated by ventures, so that each operating problem may be seen in context and not confused by entanglements with the various other phases of the business.

The multiple ventures of a business may be similar, or parallel, in their operations, like the departments in a department store, or they may be quite dissimilar as in the case of a public utility that produces and sells electricity and gas and also sells household appliances. Both the department store and the public utility should have accounting systems that reflect the net income or net loss produced by each division or department.

Ventures that start out as sidelines may of course grow in importance until they become full-fledged ventures. Sometimes it may be a close decision whether a particular phase of a business should be regarded as a sideline or as one of the major ventures. 

Generally the sideline is characterized and may be recognized by the fact that the business is not operating it voluntarily but be-

cause of some kind of pressure. For instance, many companies operate lunchrooms for their employees at noon; other companies in undeveloped areas are forced to provide housing for employees or perhaps to run bus lines from the plant to the residential areas. 

In each case the company is engaged in the sideline venture through necessity. Usually a company tries to make the sideline venture at least self-supporting, although the company may have to operate it year after year at a net loss. At least, the accounts should provide full information to enable management to base its decisions on a full understanding of the facts. 

Management and the Organization Chart 

As a business grows, management necessarily delegates some of its responsibility. Properly the organization divides the management along functional and subfunctional lines, which means that recognition is given to the principle that there are many special activities within a business which require specialized abilities or capacities. When the divisions, departments, sections, units, or groups are organized along the lines of these specialized tasks, the business is organized functionally. An important factor in the success of any large business is the degree to which its organization follows true functional lines.

If the business comprises multiple ventures that are quite dissimilar, the organization at the top may follow the venture lines with functional organization within each of the major ventures comprised within the business.

Whatever the pattern of organization, the accounting system must follow organization lines in order to reflect the performance of every organization unit in the accounts. Not only will the accounting system measure the success or failure of each venture, but it will also measure the cost and, so far as possible, the performance (or production) of each functional unit. This aspect of accounting is called accounting for control. Its purpose is to enable management to watch every organization unit and to make certain that each unit discharges its function efficiently and economically. 

If efficient operation can be assured throughout the organization management has a solid base on which to stand in facing competition in the outside business world and in developing the enterprise to the fullest possible extent.

THE CHART OF ACCOUNTS 

The implementation of the foregoing philosophy of accounting usefulness lies primarily in the provision of an adequate list of accounts. Each account must be properly titled and clearly identified.

An accounting system comprising seventy-five or one hundred accounts might reasonably rely entirely on descriptive word titles for the identification of all its component accounts. When a system grows to hundreds or thousands of accounts, however, it becomes necessary to work out a more flexible identification scheme in order to facilitate the economical handling of thousands of transactions and to aid in comprehending the intricate relationships of the many groupings and subdivisions. In relatively small systems, when there is no likelihood of installing punched card systems, initial letters of significant titles of the account groups are sometimes used, as A for asset, AC for current asset, L for liability, and so forth. In systems using punched cards, straight numerical systems prove more feasible. Combinations of letters and numbers may be used quite effectively for manual systems, but these are practically never found used in combination whenever machine methods are employed.

Many account numbering systems presently in use exhibit the unfortunate effects of having been begun under a too limited concept of the expansion that would eventually be necessary. They therefore evidence crowding at some places, and at other places they fail to make the numbers themselves as definitive as they might be. To avoid this situation, it is desirable to plan any system, even for a comparatively small business, as though it were expected to expand indefinitely. If the system remains small, this expansion plan does not create an appreciable handicap, but it is of inestimable advantage if indeed the business managers should desire to expand.

Basic Theory and Formal Classification of Accounts 

To establish the categories of accounting which should receive basic recognition in the formal classification, the accountant wisely returns to fundamental theory. The most practical adaptation of

theory is made by a scheme that recognizes the five basic categories of accounts: assets, liabilities, proprietorship, revenues, and expenses. Accordingly, the student will discover that most account classifications are constructed with a numbering system that is built on the following basis: 

1000 Assets 

2000 Liabilities 

3000 Proprietorship (capital) (stockholders' equity) 

4000 Revenues (incomes) 

5000 Expenses 

If a very extensive system is contemplated, another digit might be used by making the assets the 10,000 series and so forth. Sometimes the expenses are given the 4,000 series and revenues the 5,000 series. This arrangement is purely optional and has no real consequence. 

Theoretically no accounts are possible other than these five categories. Close inspection of apparent deviations from this rule. reveals that each deviation is the result of a special, usually temporary situation which it has not been feasible to integrate into the accounting system. For instance accountants in really active systems occasionally run into debits or credits whose real nature is not known simply because information is incomplete. Such unclassifiable items may reasonably be handled in a 9,000 series of accounts. The simplest handling is to establish number 9,100 for suspense debits and 9,200 for suspense credits. Of course every effort should be made to ferret out the real nature of these suspense items and to dispose of them properly as promptly as it is possible to do. 

Some corporations, having established the 4,000 and 5,000 series. for revenues and expenses, wish to restrict these series to operating revenues and expenses and therefore establish series 6,000 for nonoperating revenues, and series 7,000 for nonoperating expenses, rather than to use specific subdivisions within the 4,000 and 5,000 series for such purposes. Any specific accounting system is likely to have some items that do not seem to fit directly into the regular categories. Interestingly, however, the effort to interpret such special requirements against the standard background is the best possible way to recognize the exact nature of these special situations. This technique of interpretation by reference to a cleanly

organized industrial system is the greatest possible help in understanding any system.

Although much that is said in the next few pages is a repetition of what has been said informally earlier in this book, and is the kind of material that is quite obvious to the person with practical imagination, yet it needs to be repeated to complete the record. Sometimes, too, the obvious is not thereby unimportant. 

Accountants have seen many actual account classifications in which some of these presumably obvious points had been missed, much to the disadvantage of the systems. The following development of an account classification is by concrete illustration of a classification and numbering plan that might be appropriate for a moderate sized manufacturing firm. 

The Classification of Asset Accounts 

If 1,000 represents the asset accounts; then 1,100 may be used for current asset accounts; 1,200 for plant asset accounts; and 1,300 for nonoperating asset accounts or for investments. This assignment provides a specific meaning to the hundreds' digits. Further development may cause 1,110 to represent cash; 1,120 accounts receivable; 1,130 notes receivable; 1,140 inventories; 1,150 prepaid expenses. The numbers 1,160; 1,170; 1,180; 1,190 would remain open for any other current asset categories. 

Within the 1,110 cash category, the unit's digit is still available for further detailing of cash. For instance 1,111 may represent cash-commercial deposits; 1,112 may represent cash-savings deposits; 1,113 cash-undeposited receipts; 1,114 cash-imprest petty cash, and so forth. Another business may decide to use the unit's digit in the 1,110 series as follows: 1,111 Cash on hand; 1,112

Cash-First National Bank of Cork-general; 1,113 Cash-First 

National Bank of Cork-payroll; 1,114 Cash-Second National 

Bank of Demarest; 1,115 Cash-Farm Mutual Bank of Omaha, and so forth.

Current Asset Accounts 

In the more detailed phase of any classification, it is necessary to pay more attention to the unique requirements of the business, to its specific objectives, its differences from other businesses, and to the special needs of management. A schematic classification for

the current asset section of the accounts which gives effect to the preceding suggestions follows: 

1000 Assets 

1100 Current Assets 

1110 Cash 

1111 Cash on hand 

1112 First National Bank of Cork-general 

1113 First National Bank of Cork-payroll 

1114 Second National Bank 

1115 Farm Mutual Bank of Omaha 

1116 to 1119 unassigned 

1120 Accounts Receivable 

1121 Regular Customers 

1122 Instalment Customers 

1123 Officers and employees 

1124 Utility Deposits, etc. 

1130 Notes Receivable 

1140 Inventories 

1141 Raw Materials 1142 Goods in Process 1143 Finished Goods 

1144 Supplies 

1150 Prepaid Expenses 1151 Prepaid Insurance 

1152 Prepaid Rent 

1153 Prepaid Water

It should be clear that what was said as to the possibility of variations of development under Cash would also be true of the detail development under every heading. Whereas one firm may use the classifications previously suggested for accounts receivable, another may wish to have a breakdown by sales areas, and still another may need a breakdown by its major product lines. 

In the illustration, the notes receivable category required no further detailing. Although the detail suggested may be adequate for one firm, another might have use for a considerable development at this point.

The inventories would be subject to wide variations in the actual needs of different companies. The classification of the

inventory accounts in the illustration may be accepted as typical for manufacturing firms, although there would frequently be need for the inventory category, Partial Assemblies. The inventory of raw materials may have to be broken into purchased raw materials and self-produced raw materials. Finished goods may likewise merit division into Finished Goods-Purchased and Finished Goods-Own Production. 

The wider variety in inventory classification, however, comes within the major groupings suggested. The detail within the raw material category, for instance, may comprise 10,000 to 50,000 separate kinds of materials and parts. The items in this category could be numbered of course in a simple series from 1 to the number needed, but such a system would be far less useful than one which again used digit positions to identify different natural groupings. Detail in commodity numbers can be fitted into the general account classification as the decimal portion of the raw material number, 1141. For instance, the raw material inventory accounts of a manufacturing business might be developed as follows: 

1141.0000 Inventory-Raw Materials 

1141.1000 Ferrous metals 

1141.2000 Nonferrous metals 

1141.3000 Nonmetallic chemicals. 

1141.4000 Lumber and natural wood products 1141.5000 Plastics 

1141.6000 and so forth

For most routine purposes, it would be unnecessary to express the whole number part of this classification since the normal flow of transactions would be such that documents and papers representing the details of raw materials could not possibly be confused with references to other portions of the entire classification of information. Probably, therefore, there would be a section of our manual devoted to raw material classification as such with the full development of the numbers 1000 Ferrous metals, 2000 Nonferrous metals, and so forth as though they were in fact independent whole numbers. The major account numbering scheme should never be ignored, however, when the scheme is being organized or reorganized or when it is explained to newcomers to the business.

Further development under number 1142 Inventory-Goods in Process is tied up with the nature of a company's productive process and with its cost accounting system. Under special order (job cost) production systems, the breakdown of Inventory-Goods in Process, 1142 will be separated into the individual jobs that are in process at any particular time. The detail records on each of these orders will show the value of material, labor, and overhead that has been applied to the particular job up to that time. 

Under process cost accounting, the materials in process at any time will be broken down by ledger accounts to show the value of materials in process in each department or stage of production. 

Plant Asset Accounts 

In the suggested classification of accounts, the basic 1000 series uses 1200 to indicate Plant Assets. A typical development of this area is given in the following illustration. 

1200 Plant Assets 1210 Land 

1211 Land-Main Plant Site 1212 Land-Storage Yards 

1213 Land-Proving Ground 

1214 Land-Site (held for expansion) 1215 Land-Branch Sites 1220 Buildings and Structures 1221 Buildings and Structures-Main Plant Building 1222 Buildings and Structures-Roads, Ramps, Tram ways, and so forth 

1223 Buildings and Structures-Water Tower and Dis tribution System 

1224 Buildings and Structures-Proving Ground Struc- 

tures 

1225 Buildings and Structures-Branch Buildings 1230 Machinery and Equipment 1231 Machinery and Equipment-Production Depart ment Machinery 

1232 Machinery and Equipment-Service Department 

Machinery 

1233 Machinery and Equipment-Office Equipment

1240 Automotive Equipment 1241 Automotive Equipment-Highway Trucks 1242 Automotive Equipment-Cranes 1243 Automotive Equipment-Other Yard and Plant Equipment 1250 Small Tools 

As in the case of the current assets, it must be recognized that the many possibilities for change permit the system to be tailored to suit particular companies. The formal account classification, illustrated, is not intended to show the ultimate detail available to management. For instance, account 1231, Production Department Machinery, might be further classified to show the value of machinery in each department in order to isolate the depreciation by departments. Without this subclassification it would not be possible to arrive at the total overhead by departments, which is necessary for proper costing of the departments' products. 

Underlying the department totals would be the detailed record for each machine in the department. This detailed record would show the full information on each machine: the composition of its original cost (invoice price, freight, set-up, and so forth), any improvements made for the machine, and depreciation for each period in which the machine has been in use. As in the case of the raw material inventory classification, machines would be identified by department and individually by the number to the right of the decimal in designing the overall system. In actual use, however, the entire number, 1231.7325 for instance, would seldom have to be used. Presumably, people would think of the machine simply by the decimal part of the number as machine 7325 which would probably mean machine 25 in department 73.

Nonoperating Assets 

The nonoperating section of the classification, to which we have assigned number 1300, might be nonexistent. It might record the ownership of a few thousand dollars invested in securities held for pension funds or it might record the principal asset of a corporation, which, while retaining the actual production activities for which it was originally organized, has developed into the parent corporation in a large holding company empire. Thus there is hardly any account that is standardized in the nonoperating section

of the asset classification. However, the clear differentiation between operating and nonoperating assets is very important if calculations of earning power ratios are to have definitive force either in the comparison of successive periods, or in the comparison of the business with trade association standards, or within a large business in the comparisons of the several presumably parallel operating units.

Classification of Liability Accounts 

Among the liabilities as among the assets, there is room for wide variation especially with relation to the degree of detail to be shown. In general the liabilities, being contractual, constitute cleaner categories than do the assets. A simple, but fairly representative, illustration suffices to complete the discussion. The limited expansions shown serve to suggest further possibilities. 

2000 Liabilities 2100 Current Liabilities 2110 Accounts Payable 2120 Notes Payable 2121 Notes Payable-Trade Creditors 2122 Notes Payable-Banks 2123 Notes Payable-To Stockholders 2130 Accrued Expenses 2131 Wages Accrued Payable 2132 Taxes Accrued Payable 2133 Utility Bills Accrued Payable 2134 Miscellaneous Accrued Expenses 2200 Fixed Liabilities 

2210 First Mortgage, 20-year 4% bonds 

2220 Debentures, 5%, refunding 

2230 Notes Payable, 5-year, Equipment Purchase The Proprietary Equity 

The common variations from the simple categories shown below would be those necessary properly to describe the different kinds of stocks in the actual corporate framework and the special conditions recognized by the management in establishing various kinds of reserves. The use of the modern term, retained earnings, in

place of the traditional, surplus, is of course purely optional. A simple classification of the proprietary equity follows. 

3000 Proprietorship 

3100 Capital Stock, Common, $100 par 

3200 Capital Stock, Preferred, 6% cumulative 

3300 Retained Earnings (Surplus) 

3310 Reserve for Expansion of Plant 3320 Reserve for Retirement of Bonds 

3330 Earnings Permanently Retained 

3340 Retained Earnings Available for Dividends 

3350 Expense and Revenue Summary Contra Accounts in the Classifications 

In the three major classifications previously illustrated, there is no mention of the use of contra or valuation accounts. Contra accounts may be necessary at several points, and their relationship to the general problem of classification must be considered. For instance, it is common practice to establish a credit balance account as a contra to accounts receivable in order to reflect the estimated realizable value of this asset without destroying the agreement of the control account with the formal amounts still legally due as shown in the subsidiary ledgers. Such an account is usually termed, Allowance (or Reserve) for Bad Debts. It should be given a number relating it to the account or accounts against which it stands contra. The classification shown earlier for accounts receivable might be expanded by the addition of an account, 1129 Allowance for Bad Debts, provided one contra is considered sufficient for the entire accounts receivable group. Or we could use 1121.1 Allowance for Bad Debts-Regular Customers; 1122.1 Allowance for Bad 

Debts-Instalment Customers, and so forth.

In the plant assets section, an account would be required to show accrued depreciation, for instance, 1229 Allowance for Depreciation-Buildings and Structures, or separate allowances would be used for each category of buildings and structures identified by the use of .1, or some similar technique.

Among the liabilities, the only common contra would be that for discount on bonds, shown in our classification, for instance, as 2219 Discount on First Mortgage Bonds. An account for Bond 

Discount is still occasionally found among the assets, but such a

classification indicates a fundamental misconception of the mathematics of finance pertinent to the determination of bond prices at time of sale.

Possible contra accounts also appear in the net worth section if stocks with a par value are sold below par. Thus a business might need 3109, Discount on Common Stock. If capital stock is sold above par, that is at a premium, such excess would be shown separately in what is technically known as an adjunct account. 

Thus account 3209 might be Premium on Preferred Stock.

The Revenue or Income Classification 

By far the most important objective in the classification of revenue accounts is the adequate recognition of the earnings of the several ventures within the business. These ventures may be parallel as in the department store or nonparallel as in a public utility. In any case, the segregation of revenues by venture segments is the beginning of all effective operations analysis. 

The revenue accounts for a gas and electric utility company might be arranged as follows: 

4000 Revenue 4100 Electricity Sales-Commercial 4200 Electricity Sales-Residential 4300 Gas Sales-Commercial 4400 Gas Sales-Residential 4500 Appliance Sales 

Each of these accounts could be subdivided by geographical areas, branches, main lines of appliances, and so forth. 

The department store accounts would be classified by sales departments. The departments might be numbered from 1 up to the total number of departments or they might be grouped in relation to management with groups recognized by the hundred's digit and individual departments identified by the ten's or unit's digits. In addition there would have to be provision for revenues other than sales. These revenues might well be grouped in the 4900 series. 

In manufacturing concerns the major revenue classifications would refer to the several or many products, related by product lines and by final products in detail. The product lines would be especially significant when the sales division is organized by prod-

uct lines. In this situation the problem of measurement becomes not only one of measuring the significance of the products and groups of products but also of measuring the effectiveness of the units of the sales organization.

In any discussion the classification of revenues should be related to or conditioned by the corresponding classification of expenses. 

It would be pointless to separate the revenues into categories that could not be matched by similar categories of expenses. Thus in actual practice the revenue and cost classifications are built together and neither should be developed without corresponding attention to the other.

Classification of Expenses and Costs 

The proper classification of expenses and costs requires the recognition of two patterns, first, the revenue segments of the business and its organizational units (that is, divisions, departments, sections, or other echelons). The relation between these two classifications varies from business to business. Beyond these two patterns, the systems accountant can provide for other combinations as the need may arise. 

Two degrees of separation among revenues should be recognized. Under the first pattern, several revenue producing elements are technologically quite different and therefore are separated physically and managerially. The generating of gas and electricity and the selling of appliances are an example of elements which can be clearly segregated. In manufacturing the products may be so different that their respective manufacturing processes may be conducted by separate units of the organization. On the other hand, in the other typical manufacturing situation the several products have a fundamental similarity and therefore are, at least in part, processed jointly. In the first case the major breakdown by account numbers within the 5000 series will recognize the outlines of the product or revenue pattern as controlling the expense-cost categories. For instance, the 5000 series for our gas and electric utility company may be: 

5000 Expense and Costs 

5100 Expense and Costs-Electric Generating Division 5200 Expense and Costs-Gas Generating Division 5300 Expense and Costs-Appliance Division

A large automotive company may show a major classification as follows: 

5000 Expense and Costs 

5100 Car A Division 5200 Car B Division 5300 Tractor Division 5400 Army Tank Division 

5500 and so forth

Within each of these divisions, a further breakdown would be made along the lines of the organization chart, presumably built on functional lines, and following, with respect to detailed development, the principles elaborated in the following paragraphs. 

Under the second pattern, the major expense-cost category does not recognize revenue segments but follows the organizational units along basic functional lines: 

5000 Expense and Costs 

5100 General Administrative Division 5200 Sales Division 5300 Manufacturing Division 

Each of these major functional divisions is broken down by organizational units in strict adherence to the organization chart. With this type of major classification the costing of the separate products, which is still necessary, is accomplished through the detailed operation of the cost accounting system. Even when the major 

5000 classification is made on venture lines, there is within each venture the same necessity for detailed cost accounting. Cost accounting is developed in Chapter X. 

A manufacturing business which has a number of closely related products will be used to illustrate the second pattern of classifying expenses and costs. However, the classification would serve effectively for any one of the divisions of a business whose major cost classification follows the venture pattern. The discussion of certain especially significant points follows the formal outline of the accounts.

Expense-Cost Classification in a Manufacturing Corporation 

The principal feature of the account numbering structure is that the numbering of the accounts should be made to parallel

the organization structure. The successive digit positions can be used to represent successively subordinate organizational units. 

In the following illustration, considerable expansion is shown for the controller's department purely for illustrative purposes. This expansion more or less would be paralleled in other departments in the general administrative and sales divisions. It should be obvious also that within the sales division the account structure for district and branch supervision might be expanded considerably if the business has such a feature in its actual organization.

Note that the use of the ten's and unit's digits in the manufacturing division is different from their use elsewhere. In a small business the ten's digit in the 5300 series might have been used to differentiate the service departments from the production depart ments as follows: 

5300 Manufacturing Division 5310 Manufacturing-Service Departments 

5311 Power Department 

5312 Personnel Department 

5313 and so forth

5321 Iron Foundry 

5322 Nonferrous Foundry 5323 and so forth This use of the numbers would be better than that in the full classification above if there never were more than nine service departments or production departments. The use of the ten's and unit's digits in single series as in the earlier illustration allows room for expansion up to twenty service departments and up to seventy-nine production departments. This alternate use of the

digit positions should be remembered as a potentially useful device at many other places in any systematic classification of the accounts. 

Object of Expenditure Classification 

Regardless of the degree of detail to which the organization unit classification is carried there is always a need for further subdivision of the expenses within each organization unit. The basis of this further breakdown is the physical or technological nature of the material or service which is being consumed. This type of breakdown is known as object of expenditure classification, object classification, or kind classification.

Common experience indicates that a large part of the object breakdown may be uniform for all organization units within the business, which makes the system much easier to learn and use. 

Such basic uniformity need not cramp the necessary flexibility required for the collection of unique expenses, if the system is designed with open numbers that are reserved for the nonstandard requirements of specific departments. Additional flexibility may be gained by reserving certain sections of the numbers within the customary object groups.

For formal number relationships it is well to resort again to the use of the figures at the right of the decimal point. Thus whereas the whole number portion of any expense item identifies the organizational unit, the decimal portion identifies the object of expenditure within the unit. An illustration may clarify these points.

Within any number of the 5000 series the following numbers are used as decimal components to identify the object of expenditure.

The titles and subtitles shown indicate the use of the numbering system and demonstrate its adaptability to special situations. Note that the .600 and .700 numbers are open for expenses unique to the department. In the advertising department these numbers might be used for the actual payments for newspaper and magazine space used in the 5222 section, Publications Contracts. This section would of course also use many of the standard accounts such as those for supervision, wages, and supplies. Accounts 

5223.600 and 5223.700 might be charged for the actual cost of radio and television time purchased.

.000 Supervision 

.010 Salary-Department Head (s) .020 Bonus-Department Head (s) .030 Paid Vacation-Department Head (s) .040 Paid Sick Leave-Department Head (s) .100 Wages .110 Regular Wages, Per Diem Basis 

.120 Regular Wages, Piece Rate, Incentive Basis, and so forth .130 Paid Vacation .140 Paid Sick Leave .150 Premium Pay .151 Overtime Pay at 1.5 rate .152 Overtime Pay at 2.0 rate (Sundays, holidays) 

.153 Night Shift Premium .154 Dirty Work Premium .160 Payroll Taxes-Employer's Portion .200 Supplies .210 Communication and Record Materials (office depart ments) 

.220 Personnel Convenience and Protection Materials .230 Janitorial Materials .240 Auxiliary Production Materials .241 Metal and Metal Products .242 Wood and Wood Products .243 Plastics 

.300 Public Utilities .310 Telephone, Telegraph, Carrier Pigeon, and so forth

.600 Open .700 Open 

.800 Miscellaneous

.900 Prorated Expenses 

.910 Rent Factor .920 Supervision 

.930 Miscellaneous Factors 

In addition to the formal open numbers, there is almost unlimited flexibility available within the hundredth's and thousandth's digit. For instance, under supplies the .250, .260, .270, .280, and .290 numbers are still available. Under .240 Auxiliary 

Production Materials the numbers .244 to .249 are still open.

Management Use of Object Classification 

It is reasonable to ask why management should be interested in the large amount of detailed information supplied within each organizational unit by the object of expenditure classification. 

Basically, management is interested in holding the head of each unit responsible for the total expenses of his unit. If these expenses are kept at the standard established by the budget or by other proper measure, the unit head is presumed to have controlled his expenses well. If this total rises, or rises faster than the volume of work in his unit justifies, he is held responsible and is duly censured. The detailed object classification provides a breakdown of the total of the unit's expenses. It shows the unit head exactly which item or items caused the overall increase, and thus it helps him to defend himself if the increase was necessary or if it was caused by conditions beyond his control. If the unit head should have been able to control the item of increased expense, he is really in trouble, but at the same time he is given the information necessary to aid him to control the item during the next period. Thus the object of expenditure classification provides aid for the heads of units at all levels in the organization. 

ACCOUNT DESCRIPTIONS 

To make the system of accounts as previously outlined become workable and consistent in successive periods, requires further definition at least for some sections of the accounts. The accounting manuals of many companies include detailed descriptions for each account in the entire classification. The primary element in

such a description for expense accounts, for instance, would list the kinds of items that could be charged to that account. Such detailed descriptions become more necessary as the account classifications grow because the contents of adjacent accounts in a system become closer to each other. 

For instance, if one account is used for the supplies of a particular department, it would be relatively simple to define the concept of supplies. However, we may require the separation of the supplies used into such classifications as the following: 

.200 Supplies .210 Communications and Records Materials .220 Personnel Convenience and Protection Materials .230 Janitorial Materials 

There will be many items which might seem to fall somewhere in the area between .220 and .230. This fact necessitates a detailed listing of items properly chargeable to each classification. 

Often the overlapping in the classification is not apparent when the classification is originally designed, but it becomes obvious with actual experience. When such questions subsequently arise, they should be carefully considered and made the subject of formal rulings by the proper accounting authority. These rulings should be incorporated in the accounting manual and publicized to all persons who use the manuals. The task of keeping the manuals up to date involves not only adding new and changed rulings but also adding rulings resulting from the reorganization of departments and sections and from the basic growth or other development of the business itself. Without specific provision for this task the manual rapidly becomes out of date and loses a large part of its potential value.

In addition to the primary consideration of the content of accounts, the detailed descriptions should include a statement of any special relationships which the account may have to other accounts, such as contra accounts to their parent accounts, expense accounts to the successive summarizing accounts into which they are to be closed at the end of the period, prime expense accounts to the departmental expense accounts to which they are prorated through the burden distribution sheet, and so forth. Another

element in the description may be the conditions attaching to the valuation or amortization of certain accounts. For instance the description of a patents account may outline the principles under which values are added to the account (purchase of patents, self-development of patents, and so forth) and the assumptions as to the expirations of patent value (full seventeen years, or accelerated over an expected practical economic lifetime). Descriptions of the accounts of assets under 60-month amortization as allowed under federal tax law should indicate such a fact. The account for bond discount might contain the complete precalculated schedule for its amortization by the actuarial or compound interest method. The account for the investment value of the life insurance on officers' lives should outline the method of determining additions by reference to the table of contractual surrender values in the insurance policy. 

The Study of Actual Classifications 

One of the best ways to study the subject is to examine actual classifications. At this point the student is well qualified to study any actual chart of accounts available to him. The technique for studying would be to compare an actual classification with the stereotype discussed earlier in this chapter. Such an attempt to reconcile the actual classification with the schematic classification would result in a more certain appreciation of the actual. When a deviation from the stereotype is found, the accountant should try to discover the reason for the basic difference in the physical or technological nature of the business under study. 

Two other questions should be borne in mind constantly when studying the practical classifications: (1) Are these accounts de signed in accordance with basic accounting theory? and (2) What management problem is responsible for the particular design of these accounts? Finally, a word of warning. Probably no one, no matter how skilful, should expect to comprehend fully the reasons behind the classifications in any new system at an initial contact with that system.

The proper approach is to try to reason out the angles but not to be too concerned at one's inability to understand each item immediately. A mental attitude of qualified or tentative acceptance, with a consciousness of the additional questions one would

like to ask to really clear up remaining confusions, will probably give the best results.

CHAPTER XII  

OUR STUDY of accounting up to this point can be thought of as putting information into the accounts and statements. There remain to be considered the techniques of analysis of the accounts and statements which may be thought of as the process of getting the maximum information out of the accounts and statements. 

Although the actual figures in the statement of condition and the income statement give information of considerable importance to management, these statements are more meaningful when certain special relationships between the figures are developed. Some of these relationships have been mentioned previously but in this chapter they are developed systematically and thoroughly. 

Mechanically, a series of ratio, or percentage, relationships between certain figures must be calculated, but greater importance should be placed on the consideration in each case of the questions: 

To whom is this information important? Is it desirable that this ratio be high or low? Is it desirable that this ratio should increase or decrease indefinitely, or is there some determinable optimum? 

Is this ratio independently significant or must it be judged in the light of other ratios? A final question should perhaps always be kept in mind: In the light of this and related ratios what management action is desirable?

STATEMENT OF CONDITION RATIOS 

In terms of the source of their figures, ratios may be classified as (1) those that concern only the statement of condition, (2) those that concern only the income statement, and (3) those that concern both statements. Each of these classifications is discussed in terms of a concrete illustration. The discussion of the first group of ratios and percentages is based on the statement of condition for a manufacturing concern, Figure 45. 310

$320,000 50,000 $ 96,000 $ 80,000

FIGURE 45. STATEMENT OF CONDITION FOR A MANUFACTURING CONCERN Equity Ratio 

The equity ratio measures the proportions of the total assets (or capital in the economic sense) that were provided by the stockholders and the creditors. In the Acme Corporation the stockholders' equities amount to $224,000 which is 70% of the total equities, or a ratio of .7 to 1. The same basic relationship can be expressed in several ways: The total of the liabilities, or creditors' equities, is 30% of the total equities; the stockholders' equities are 21/3 times the creditors' equities; the assets are 333 3% of the liabilities.

The equity ratio measures the basic long range safety factor for the creditors. In whatever form it is stated its meaning must be grasped. In the illustration, 70% of the total value of the assets must be wiped out before the creditors would lose any of their equity. 

Current Ratio 

The current ratio is the ratio of total current assets to total current liabilities. In the Acme statement of condition the current

ratio is $144,000 to $46,000, or 3.1 to 1. This ratio measures the more immediate safety of the short term creditors and is a test of solvency, that is, the ability of the debtor to meet its maturing obligations. If this ratio is high, there is less likelihood that the corporation will encounter financial difficulties in the near future. 

Acid Test Ratio 

The acid test ratio is the ratio of cash and receivables to the current liabilities. In the Acme statement of condition the acid test ratio is $63,000 to $46,000 or 1.4 to 1. This ratio, like the current ratio, measures the more immediate safety factor of the credit situation. It is aimed at bringing out a possible weakness in the current ratio. If the Acme Corporation had had $121,000 in inventories and only $23,000 in cash and receivables, the current ratio would still have been 3.1 to 1, but the acid test ratio would have been only .5 to 1. Thus the acid test reveals the potential danger to creditors when such a large proportion of the current assets is invested in inventories. 

Working Capital 

Under another approach the immediate safety factor of the credit situation may be appraised by calculating the excess of the current assets over the current liabilities. This calculation does not produce a ratio, but a dollar amount. Acme's working capital is $144,000-$46,000, or $98,000. This figure together with the current and acid test ratios effectively rounds out the immediate or short term credit picture.

Other Statement of Condition Ratios 

It may be revealing to study the composition of the statement of condition further by working out the percentages of the current and fixed asset totals to the total assets, and even the percentage of each asset to the total assets. This comparison may disclose an overextended position in one or more of the assets. 

A Study of Comparative Ratios 

Most of the ratios discussed, as well as others to be developed later, do not have much inherent meaning; they must be compared to similar ratios of other periods or to standard trade or other

ratios. For instance, Acme's current ratio of 3.1 to 1 and its acid test ratio of 1.4 to 1 may be judged quite satisfactory against the commonly accepted standards of 2 to 1 and 1 to 1 for these ratios. 

Acme's equity ratio of 70% appears fairly safe against the background of the customary equity ratios of industrial corporations. 

If in addition to these general standards the corresponding ratios of Acme's statements of one and two years ago were available, the situation might be even more revealing. Consider the illustration:

ACME MANUFACTURING CORPORATION Ratio Comparative Ratios 

Dec. 31, 19X5 Dec. 31, 19X6 | Dec. 31, 19X7 

Equity ratio

Acid test ratio .65 .60 .70 3.0 2.5 3.1 1.2 .9 1.4 

Current Assets to Total Assets..

.63 .41 .45 $106,000 $74,000 $98,000

A full analysis of these figures might require more investigation than can be accomplished in this context, but it should be amply clear that the trend of ratios during this three-year period is significant. After dropping from .65 to .60 the equity ratio recovered to .70. The current ratio and the acid test ratio had gone down from a quite satisfactory point in 19X5 to 2.5 and .9 in 19X6 which might have been alarming to the management. However, both of these ratios recovered by December 31, 19X7 to better their 19X5 status. Working capital decreased between 19X5 and 

19X6 but recovered materially by the end of 19X7. The ratio of current assets to total assets dropped very significantly in 19X6 (from .63 to .41) and recovered only slightly to .45 in 19X7. The reason for these shifts is not fully apparent. We should not conclude that there was mismanagement in 19X6 because the phenomena might have been caused by the purchase of equipment or by an addition to the plant either of which could be clearly traced if all the accounts were available. 

If an even more detailed study were made of the percentages of certain specific assets to total assets and if a study of the percentages of specific equities to total equities were made and compared on a basis similar to three-year period comparison, important trends would be revealed.

FIGURE 46. INCOME STATEMENT FOR A MANUFACTURING CONCERN 

In the final right-hand column the significant elements of the income statement are related by percentages to the sales figures as 100%. The percentage of the cost of goods sold to sales and the

complementary percentage of gross margin to sales reveal the pricing policy of the business. In some cases the business may be in a monopoly position and be able to determine its price margin more or less as it chooses. In other cases competition may determine the margin. The margin may be a fixed percentage or it may fluctuate competitively or seasonally. Often the margin shown in the income statement results from many different margins on many different products. In any case the actual margin attained for the accounting period is a figure of considerable importance. 

It should be noted that many published statements do not reveal the gross margin as such but rather combine the cost of manufacturing with the general administrative and selling costs and thus take one step from sales figure to net operating income.

Total Cost of Goods Manufactured FIGURE 47. MANUFACTURING STATEMENT FOR A MANUFACTURING CONCERN 

Although such a single-step statement may be desirable in a report to stockholders or to the public, it does not provide the full analysis which the management is likely to require. 

Further analysis of the income statement is made by calculating the percentage of important subtotal expenses and of the net operating income to the sales. The percentages of the detailed expenses within the groups to sales are also calculated. In the lower section of the statement the percentage of the income tax "bite" and the final percentage of net income are very revealing. 

Here again as in the case of the statement of condition, the per-

centages of the income statement are not very significant unless they are studied against the background of some significant standard. An experienced industrialist who knows the area in which 

Acme operates might be able to judge the adequacy of the 55% gross margin, the 47% total expenses, and the 3.7% net income by reference to general standards which he carries in his head. The general manager of Acme may remember the corresponding figures for the past year and may know whether 19X7 is a better or a worse year. The percentage analysis takes on its full significance, however, only when it is compared to prior years' results or to trade association standards, as may occasionally be possible. 

In order to keep the illustration statement from becoming awkward, the manufacturing section is shown separately (Figure 47) as a supporting schedule with its own percentage analysis. 

This development obviously is a sensible one especially since in actual practice much more detail probably would be shown both for the manufacturing and for the administrative and sales divisions of the income statement. The percentage analysis of the manufacturing statement uses the total cost of goods manufactured as the 100% base, to give a sharper picture of the trends in actual manufacturing operations than would be given if total sales were used as a base.

The expense classifications previously studied indicate that in actual practice the manufacturing statement would be broken down by departments and possibly by cost centers within departments for its complete percentage analysis. Only by breaking down the costs and apportioning them to the ultimate units responsible can management attain the maximum benefits of control. At this point, it is only by a comparison of detailed percentages with standards of some kind (previous periods, trade association, budgets, or scientifically engineered standards) that the real effectiveness can be attained.

INTERSTATEMENT RATIOS 

Some of the most important ratios are those concerning both the income statement and the statement of condition. One group of these ratios, the earning power ratios, analyzes the earning power of the business from several standpoints.

Earning Power Ratios 

The ratio, net operating income to total assets ($77,000 ÷ $320,000 in the Acme statements), is the most frequently used of the earning power ratios. It represents the viewpoint of the general manager, who thinks of it as measuring his success for the year. This ratio sees the business as a technological unit, disregarding the debt and capital structure in the statement of condition and the interest and income tax factors in the income statement. It seeks to measure the overall efficiency and success of the operating management for the year. 

From the special viewpoint of the bondholders the ratio of net operating income to the annual bond interest requirements ($77,000 ÷ $2,000) measures the safety of their annual income. 

Thus the times interest earned was over 38 and was very safe. 

From the viewpoint of the preferred stockholders, the important ratio is the net income to stockholders divided by the preferred stock dividend requirements ($36,000 ÷ $4,800). Since the preferred stock dividend requirement (6% of $80,000) was earned 7.5 times over in 19X7 the preferred stockholders would have reason to consider their investment a safe one.

There are several ratios of importance from the common stockholders' viewpoint. First, of course, we must realize that $4,800 of the $36,000 income must go to the preferred stockholders since apparently no dividend has been paid to them in 19X7. Thus (assuming the preferred to be nonparticipating and not in arrears) $31,200 is left for the common stockholders' share of the 19X7 net income. The common stockholder may regard this $31,200 as $3.12 per share ($31,200 ÷ 10,000 shares of common), or as a return of 22.4% on the equity of the common stockholders less the preferred stock dividend requirement ($144,000 — $4,800) as shown in 

Figure 45, the December 31, 19X7 statement of condition.

Since the common stockholders are generally in control of the board of directors and thus in control of the dividend policy, they could have paid out the entire earnings to themselves in dividends had they so wished. Therefore, it is usually proper to regard the retained earnings as belonging to the common stockholders. 

These ratios, based on book figures, should not be confused with the price-earnings ratio which investors calculate in comparing

market prices of stocks with the reported earnings per share. If 

Acme stock is quoted in the market at $31.00 per share, the prospective investor calculates the price-earnings ratio as $31.00 ÷ $3.12 or just under 10. Many good stocks sell for 8 to 10 times their annual earnings, and the blue-chip stocks commonly sell for 12 to 15 times their earnings or even higher in many cases. This ratio would give a sidelight on what investors in general think of Acme. 

Miscellaneous Interstatement Ratios 

The operations of the business may be understood more readily, if the special relationships between certain income statement and certain statement of condition items are examined. 

If all sales are made on account, the ratio of the accounts receivable at the end of the year to the total sales indicates the condition of the collections. For instance, if at the end of the year, ten per cent of the sales on account are uncollected whereas if at the end of the prior year only eight per cent were uncollected, collections are obviously slower for some reason. If sales are partly for cash and partly on account, only the sales on account should be used in this comparison. If notes are taken from customers as a step in the collection of slow items, the total of accounts and notes receivable should be used. Sometimes the 10% of uncollected accounts is referred to as 36.5 days' accounts uncollected. (Eight per cent would be 29.2 days' accounts, and so forth.) A more complete appraisal of the condition of the collections usually is made in connection with the estimate of the bad debts allowance; the aging of the accounts, as treated in Chapter VII, provides a more reliable method of analyzing the essential factors involved. 

If Acme's entire sales, $962,500, were all on account, and if its notes and accounts are considered together as the balance of receivables arising from those sales, the uncollected percentage on December 31, 19X7 would be $48,000 $962,500 or 5%, or 

18 days' accounts uncollected.

The condition of inventories and the rapidity of their use is spoken of as turnover. In the statement of condition of the Acme 

Manufacturing Corporation, raw materials were $30,000 and in the manufacturing statement materials used were $71,700. These figures give a raw material turnover of $71,700 $30,000 or 2.39 times. This turnover presents the crude beginning of the study of

inventory control. If similar figures for the turnover of raw materials are available for successive years, some judgement can be formed concerning the adequacy of the investment in raw materials. If the turnover is lower each year, it is probable that unused and possibly unusable materials have accumulated and are causing a waste of storage space, insurance, and interest on investment. 

To fully analyze the condition of the raw materials, the accountant must study each item in terms of the balance on hand, the quantity used per week or per month, and the time required to obtain a replenishment. Such a detailed study accomplishes two purposes: (1) It assures an adequate supply of each item and (2) it guards against the waste occasioned by an excessively large inventory. 

The accountant for the Acme Manufacturing Corporation would also calculate the turnover of finished goods by dividing the cost of goods sold by the average inventory of finished goods. For 

19X7 this turnover would be: $433,125 ÷ $35,000+ $50,000 2 or 10.2 times.

In a trading concern, the turnover of merchandise (that is, the cost of sales divided by the average inventory) is considered a very important indication of merchandising efficiency. It ranges from a slow rate of one or two turns per year in the jewelry business to a fast rate of ten, twelve, or fifteen turns per year in independent grocery businesses and to still more rapid turns in chain stores. For any particular business, the merchandise turnover in successive years is especially significant when studied together with its changing price policies, with the amounts spent on advertising, and so forth. Sometimes an intentional reduction in the percentage of gross margin and increased advertising can increase the rate of turnover to such an extent that the final results in net income are improved substantially. Sometimes, of course, such pressure for an increased rate of turnover fails to compensate for the additional advertising cost and the lower percentage of gross margin. To weigh all of these factors and to obtain the maximum benefit is the essence of the merchandising problem.

Working capital turnover is obtained by dividing the net sales by the working capital (or the average working capital if available). 

This ratio, in a general way, measures the effectiveness with which management uses the working capital at its disposal. The use of

the average working capital is obviously desirable if there has been substantial change in working capital as is commonly the case in a business which is rapidly expanding. Generally a high turnover of working capital is desirable, but here again this ratio must be judged against the background of related ratios. Note that this ratio may be raised either by increasing the net sales or by decreasing the working capital. If sales can be increased, while the working capital is held steady without too much sacrifice of margin or too much increased expense, the resulting increase in the working capital turnover is obviously desirable. However, if the increase in the turnover of working capital ratio is achieved through decreased working capital or through sacrifice of margin and excessive expenses, the change is patently unfortunate. 

RATIOS AND MANAGEMENT ACTION 

The most refined analysis of statements is useless, of course, unless it indicates a proper course of action for management. 

Generally such action should evade or reverse some undesirable trend or should take further advantage of some desirable trend. 

The accountant, of course, does not take the action; that is the province of management. The accountant, however, is not likely to make an effective analysis unless he can see these ratios as management views them, that is, unless he can think in terms of action by management.

Some of the possibilities and some of the dangers involved in an interpretation of ratios have been discussed in this chapter. Some valuable practice and demonstration may be experienced in the problems based on this chapter. Complete realism however can come only in the face of actual concrete situations in which the dollars are real and the management is concerned with building a sound business.
