CHAPTER XIII THE BUDGET 

A CERTAIN industrialist, who had himself come up through the path of accounting and controllership, once remarked "Practical management decisions are very seldom based on actual accounting data. Historical accounting is virtually useless from the standpoint of management. Management bases practically all important decisions on estimates and forecasts rather than on figures of past performance."

These comments are probably literally true, and the point of view which they emphasize is highly significant to anyone who seeks to make accounting useful to management. The comments do not, however, suggest that it is useless to record correctly the actual financial facts of business. An exact knowledge of the actual business facts under varying conditions is one of the principal factors in making accurate forecasts. Properly understood, the industrialist's comments constitute a valuable guide to the best possible service which accounting may render to management and at the same time they constitute a shrewd appraisal of the importance of proper record keeping. 

THE FORECAST BUDGET 

On many occasions, businessmen must forecast the probable income and cost of new ventures with very little in the way of direct precedent. For instance, an entirely new business may be organized by investors, or an established business may embark on a new activity or may make radical changes in its organization. In these cases, the forecast must be based on the businessman's general experience and knowledge, plus the technical figures provided by engineers and other specialists.

This type of business forecasting is mentioned merely for purposes of contrast, in order to throw a clearer light on the kind of forward use of accounting data which constitutes normal business 330

budgeting. When business moves ahead into new periods possibly with some change in its pattern of operation and with considerable change of volume but still with its major form and purpose unchanged, its forward plans take the form of budgeting. 

The Purpose of Budgeting 

One way to understand the purpose of budgeting is to consider the plight of a sizable manufacturing firm which does not use budgets in its forward planning. 

Assume that the business has completed a reasonably profitable year in which 100,000 units of product were manufactured and sold. In the new year the sales manager expects to sell 120,000 units, and he coaches his sales force with this figure in mind. The purchasing agent expects an actual drop in volume and makes contracts with suppliers for raw materials necessary for a 95,000 volume. The production superintendent expects a 5% increase and orders new machinery to take care of this volume. The personnel manager recruits and trains employees for his own estimate of a 

15,000 increase in volume. The vice president in charge of finance negotiates a line of credit with the company's bank to carry the company through its busy season on the same volume as last year. 

Such a lack of planning would certainly cause the business endless difficulty.

Probably no business ever failed in its planning to the extent indicated in the illustration. Obviously such a situation requires coordination in the planning of the various divisions of the business. Coordination is the essential ingredient of budgeting. Although the actual steps and routines followed in devising a budget may vary, the basic aim and purpose of budgeting is always to get the business to pull together in one sensible plan of action. 

Forecasting the Volume of Business 

Usually the treasurer or the controller is given the primary responsibility for organizing the budget. Generally, though not always, the first estimate concerning the volume of operations is made by the sales manager, either from data in his files or from estimates submitted by his salesmen in the field. There is no one right way to arrive at the volume estimate. The products of

THE BUDGET 

different businesses vary tremendously. In one case the salesmen, by virtue of their close contact with customers, may have the best opportunity to estimate future sales. In other cases careful statistical research by the home office may be far more reliable. In still other cases the sales possibilities may not be the determining factor at all because the demand may be known to be far in excess of possible productive capacity.

Regardless of who makes the original estimate, it must be discussed back and forth in terms of production, personnel, financing, procurement, and general business policy in order that the volume determined on for the budget will be feasible from all viewpoints, and that all divisions of the business will know what is expected of them. 

The Master Budget 

After the volume has been determined, the head of each division estimates the costs of his division for that volume. When all these costs are assembled, they become a forecast income statement for the year. Often it becomes evident that the net income thus forecast will be unsatisfactory. In this case the divisional cost estimates are sent back to be reworked by the division head, or the division head may be asked to consult with the controller in order to review the detailed figures. Naturally there must be flexibility at this stage. The master budget must be hammered into such form that it shows a reasonable net profit. Each division head, however, knows that subsequently he will be held responsible for the figures to which he now agrees and that he must be careful not to accept impossibly low estimates. In some corporations the budget does not become final until it is approved by the executive committee or even by the board of directors. 

When the budgeted income statement has been determined, it becomes possible to forecast the end-of-the-year statement of condition. Sometimes the two statements are considered together and formally approved as the master budget. 

The Capital Expenditures Budget 

If the anticipated volume requires substantial additional equipment or plant capacity, the problem of financing the necessary expenditures must be carefully considered. Possibly cash from

THE BUDGET 

333 operating sources is insufficient for this purpose and the corporation must seek new financing. Whether to issue more stock or to borrow the necessary amounts on bonds or notes becomes the question. Possibly the additional equipment or plant may be leased. In any event the plan adopted, usually referred to as a capital expenditures or facilities budget, must be prepared with careful attention to timing and must be fitted to the remainder of the budget.

The Cash Budget 

The in-and-out cash flow is determined to a considerable extent by the remainder of the budget. Any shift in the situation, however, such as a change in the sales plan from cash to thirty-days' time, or to instalment, or to the granting of different sales discount terms would change the pattern of the cash flow substantially. If the sales show distinctly a seasonal influence, a change in volume from that of the previous year must be considered in terms of its impact on the cash position. If the capital purchases are to be handled without outside financing, another factor enters the cash picture. 

In the cash budget, all these influences are gathered together, the cash position is forecast month by month, and the treasurer is enabled to foresee clearly when, how much, and for what periods he must borrow from the bank. Certainly the banker will be favorably disposed toward granting a line of credit when the request is supported by a careful study of the requirements, such as is visualized in this type of cash budget.

As a matter of fact, many cash budgets have their origin in the advice given by a banker to a prospective borrower to consider his cash requirements over a longer period. Frequently, the experienced banker realizes that a request for a $10,000 sixty-day loan would leave the borrower in a more difficult position at the end of that period than when the loan was granted. After the borrower has studied his real requirements and after he has made a study of his ability to repay his loan, he may request $15,000 to be repaid in $5,000 monthly instalments beginning three months from the date of the loan. This loan, based on a realistic appraisal of all factors, probably will be more satisfactory to both the borrower and the bank. Behind the borrowing lies the cash budget,

THE BUDGET 

which, once it has demonstrated its usefulness, is likely to become a regular part of the business procedure. 

Initially many businesses make budgets because they encounter pressure for more careful planning of cash expenditures. After a series of uncomfortably tight periods in the cash position, management prepares a cash budget to avoid such difficulties in the future. 

If any capital expenditures are in prospect, obviously they must be planned carefully in order that their impact on the cash position will be clear. Thus the business begins to budget capital expenditures. Although he is primarily interested in cash, the treasurer realizes that he must know the anticipated volume of sales and the probable drain on the cash position caused by payroll and other expenses. Seeking this information he is convinced sooner or later that the business needs an overall master budget. 

THE CONTROL OF EXPENSES THROUGH BUDGETING 

Although the master budget is chiefly a coordinating device, the related detail budgets may be found chiefly valuable as instruments of expense control. In earlier chapters we have studied the effects of expense and cost control as they may be effected through analysis of actual or historical figures especially when used in comparative form in successive periods. These various controls become more effective when they are reinforced by budget techniques devised to accomplish the same purposes. 

It has been shown that the division heads are brought into the discussion of the expenses that are proper in their areas. In a similar manner each division head discusses with the heads of departments under his jurisdiction the amounts that will be reasonable for each department. In a large corporation this process may filter down the chain of command to still lower echelons. 

When the final budget has been thus approved by the executives affected up and down the line, they are in a better mood to keep their expenses within the budgeted amounts if it is humanly possible. In a sense, each executive has contracted to manage his own department effectively at the budgeted figure. 

To further aid in this process of expense control, the accountant makes a report each month to each responsible executive comparing each item of the departmental expenditure with the

THE BUDGET 

335 amount budgeted for that item. This report may include both figures for each month and cumulative figures for the year to date. 

If certain expenses become excessive in any one month, the executive has an opportunity to apply corrective measures in subsequent months and to follow the results of his efforts in the succeeding monthly reports. In many instances circumstances are such that the cumulative budget comparisons are more significant than the figures for any single month.

Budget Administration 

In order to understand how a budget works, we should visualize a series of conferences occurring shortly after the monthly budget reports are issued by the accounting department. The president reviews these reports with his divisional heads, usually one at a time. Then the divisional heads confer with department heads in their areas. The department heads call in the foremen and so forth.

In each case the conference may range from a pleasant conversation with commendation to a severe reprimand with censure if the report shows inexcusable excesses over budget. In each case, however, the supervisor should consider carefully the reason for the variance and should provide an opportunity for the lower tier executive to explain and justify any excess. 

Remember that expenses vary widely as to their controllability. 

Some expenses are more or less fixed by their nature; other expenses allocated to the department may be controllable elsewhere in the business, but are in no sense the responsibility of the head of the department to which they are allocated. Some expenses, normally controllable by the department head, may be subject at certain times to specific influences that are not within his area of responsibility. Price changes, shortages of supplies, weather, accidents, strikes, and so forth are samples of such factors. In order to make a deserved reprimand effective, it is very important never to reprimand unjustly. In the long run, salary, promotion, and even continuance of employment depend on performance, and a good record as measured by the budget report is a significant part of each executive's performance. The ability and willingness to stand the pressure of such a situation is the mark of a man who aspires to be an executive.

THE BUDGET 

THE FLEXIBLE BUDGET 

Up to this point it has been assumed that the business can determine its volume of operations with reasonable certainty and that the monthly budget reports are made against the background of the fixed annual budget. Many businesses do, in fact, proceed on this assumption. Then, as the actual volume varies from the expected volume, the monthly comparisons show wider variations between the budget estimates and the actual expenses. If the volume has increased, the department heads feel that they can justify the excess of their actual expenses by pointing to the increased volume. The administration of the budget, therefore, becomes more and more troublesome. If the volume falls below the budgeted amount, the situation is no less difficult. Each head defends himself by pointing out that he is within the budget and that he could not be expected to reduce his expenses after he had been told to plan for the budgeted volume. 

To get around these difficult problems of administration, it is necessary to build some kind of flexibility into the budget. This purpose may be achieved in several ways. The most obvious and probably the commonest way of obtaining flexibility is to set up a multiple-step budget in the first place. This is of course much more work than the simple fixed budget, but it is often very worthwhile in the long run.

The Multiple-Step Budget 

If a flexible budget is to be built, the initial budget discussions proceed as previously outlined. Instead of fixing upon a single expected volume of business, however, all estimates are made on the most probable volume and also on a whole series of other volumes related to this volume in certain intervals. Thus Company X may establish its multiple budget for volumes of 500,000 units as the most probable estimate and also for volumes of 450,000 and 400,000 on the low side and 550,000 and 600,000 on the high side. If the business volume cannot be measured in physical units, the budgets may be established in terms of 90%, 95%, 100%, 105%, 110%, and so forth, with 100% representing the most probable dollar volume. The measurement in units is considered better since it encourages more independent thinking about the

THE BUDGET 

337 effect of volume on costs. With the percentage figures at the heads of columns, there is too much temptation to assume that various costs and expenses will vary directly in accordance with the percentages of expected volume. Such direct variation with volume may be anticipated properly only in a relatively small percentage of cases; it is much more desirable to consider the volume-expense relationship of each item on its merits for each volume. The careful thought required to prepare a budget on this multiple-step basis often brings to the attention of management certain facts about expense relationships that result directly in savings entirely aside from the operations of the budget. 

Probably a budget in five steps as suggested is quite satisfactory in most cases, but there is certainly no objection to adopting additional steps if the situation seems to require them. 

The administration of the multiple-step budget requires that the actual costs for each month be reported and compared to the figures of that budget step whose volume is nearest to the actual volume. Thus if the January volume of Company X is 38,000 units, the monthly figures used would be 1/12 of those in the 450,000 unit budget; whereas if the actual volume was 44,000 the budget figures from the 550,000 unit volume would be used. In each case the division or department head, having agreed to the budget when it was formulated, would now be under effective pressure to meet its requirements. Of course, it would still be necessary to judge the propriety of variation between actual expenses and budget estimates with the same degree of care that was described previously in discussing the administration of the fixed budget.

Another approach to the problem of flexibility is to set up the expected-volume budget and to prepare two additional budgets, one for each extreme of volume (high and low) that might reasonably be expected. For instance the low estimate of volume might be 66% and the high 133 % of the normal expectation. The probable expense should be established carefully for each item in each division of the organization at normal activity and at each extreme. Certain items may be completely fixed for all these volumes, other items will vary somewhat less than volume, still others may vary directly with volume or even more than volume. 

With this three-level budget agreed upon, the company is ready to

THE BUDGET 

handle any situation on the assumption that the expenses should react on a straight-line basis for any actual volume within the range between normal and plus or minus 333%. Although this assumption is obviously not valid for some of the expenses, it is a useful, practical, and effective assumption to employ as a starting point in analysis.

Each month's budget report will show the actual expenses for that month compared to budget figures calculated by the accountant on a straight-line basis to reflect the actual volume for the month. Any items that are really fixed and which show as fixed at the three control levels will of course appear at the same amount in the calculated monthly budget. Items that vary in direct proportion to volume will appear in this proportion in the calculation by the straight-line method. Items that are semi-fixed (or semi-variable) appear accordingly in the calculated budget. The variances between the actual expenses and the budget estimates for the month are presumed to be budget (or efficiency or usage) variances which must be explained by the executive responsible. 

Note that in using the multiple-step budget a part of the variance in each item occurs because the actual volume does not coincide exactly with the budget, even though it was the closest step. In the three-control level budget, the calculation of the monthly budget recognizes the actual volume, and therefore the variance shown in this case may be assumed to be purely a budget variance.

A Critical Inspection of the Concept of Volume The term volume as used in business requires further definition. 

It is difficult, however, to establish a proper criterion of volume. In some businesses, the proper definition of volume may represent the critical problem of budgeting. 

The simplest volume situation is one in which a single product can be measured in physical terms, as for instance the tons of coal mined by a soft coal mining company. A fuel dealer might handle five or ten different kinds of coal and still find total tons sold a reasonable method of measuring volume for budgeting purposes. 

In a manufacturing firm the units of product may be sufficiently similar and homogeneous so that the number of units produced, cars or refrigerators or television sets would be an adequate meas-

THE BUDGET 

339 ure of the volume of production. However, if the firm manufactures a diversified series of products, such as cars, refrigerators, and television sets, the total number of units of all products manufactured would not be an adequate measure of overall volume. In this case, if it is not feasible to separate the three ventures and to give each venture its own budget, some other measure of volume must be found.

The total dollars of sales for all three products combined may be significant for some purposes but would not be considered a proper measure of volume for budgeting purposes because sales dollars encompass price changes between successive periods as well as actual volume changes. Manufacturing companies in this position (or those having dozens or hundreds of different products) often find that the total hours of direct labor in their plants seem to be the best overall measure of volume. Note that the figure used is hours of direct labor rather than number of employees or dollars of wages paid. The hours figure reflects overtime but fails to reflect changes in wage rates, therefore, it is fairly close to the ideal criterion of volume. 

There is another facet in the problem of volume that is very important when the budget is used for purposes of cost control. 

Even when there is a satisfactory method of measuring volume for overall purposes, and of measuring the actual performance of the production departments, the method might be misleading if it were applied directly to some of the manufacturing service departments, such as power, personnel, and maintenance or, possibly even worse, if it were applied to some of the sales and general administrative departments. An increase of 25% in volume as measured in terms of product would be unlikely to justify a 25% increase in expense for the power department, the maintenance department, the personnel department, the credit department, the advertising department, or the controller's office.

For each of these and for many other departments, a serious attempt must be made to find some measure of its service that justifies the appraisal of its costs in successive periods. Sometimes the factory is so busy that there is little opportunity to make repairs other than emergency maintenance jobs, whereas the maintenance department can make all desirable repairs when the machines are idle. In a period of falling production, the personnel department

THE BUDGET 

may have an increased load of work involved in processing layoffs. 

In the depth of a depression the credit department may have more work checking on prospects than it does in time of prosperity when credit is easy. When the factory is running at capacity with a big backlog of orders, it may be good business to cut down materially on the activities of the advertising department. 

The following formulas are suggested as possible ways to measure volume for departments which are not directly engaged in production. 

1. Personnel department. The total of new employments plus layoffs plus one-tenth of total on payroll 2. Maintenance department. The total labor hours spent in maintenance work 

3. Credit department. The number of credit applications processed 4. Accounts receivable unit of the controller's department. The number of billings plus the number of cash receipts posted in the department.

Obviously these formulas are attempts to devise a way of measuring the service rendered to the business by these departments, but every one of them is probably open to some objection. In some departments it may be literally impossible to find any general measure of this kind, for instance, the controller's own office, the file department, the legal department, the public relations department, and the stenographic pool. In these departments the best budget technique is probably to work from last year's cost or from several past years' average costs, adjusted by specific known factors that ought to decrease or increase the cost. If the stenographic pool is being converted from manual shorthand to dictaphone techniques, it is hardly likely that the costs would be the same. If the public relations department is dismissing its outside consultants and engaging permanent experts, the costs will certainly change one way or the other.

SUMMARY 

In budgeting, as so often in accounting, the only real guide to success is to apply common sense and imagination to an understanding of the basic needs of the particular business. Budgeting

THE BUDGET 

341 has two great purposes; the coordination of all facets of the business to the production of the greatest net income cognizant with safety and the underlying control of all operating divisions to insure their most economical and efficient performance. Anything that contributes to the accomplishment of these purposes is good budgeting and good accounting.

In addition to these management or internal aspects, a careful budget has an important impact on external relations with creditors, especially with banks that are extending credit. The experienced banker is usually pleased to see a cash budget in the hands of a prospective borrower. He regards the loan with more favor if the prospective borrower presents a budget that is complete in all its facets.

Problems Based on Chapter XIII 

1. The Borough Manufacturing Company had a modern, well designed factory, a loyal and efficient group of employees, and an excellent classification of accounts which paralleled its organization perfectly. 

Its journals were well designed, its office routines were economical and accurate, its accountant kept the books in a careful manner and prepared complete and detailed statements at the end of each month in a form approved by the public accountants who certified the annual statements. The company's product was excellent and sold in an area where there was little competition from any other manufacturer of the same type product.

Yet, year after year the Borough Company's net profits were low or actually negative. Every year, even though sales had increased nicely, the expenses somehow crept up and wiped out the possible profits.

The prior description probably could be applied without much change to many businesses. What factor is missing? What caused the poor showing that was made by the Borough Manufacturing Company?
